Modern China

Vol. 52, No. 6 · 2026-11 · 4 篇 · SAGE · China · Peer-reviewed
  1. A Changing Legal World: Christianity and Extraterritoriality in a Sichuan Courtroom (1810–1911)
    Yuan Tian · pp. 639-674 · online 2026-07-30
    摘要

    This article examines the juridical interactions between Christian congregations and local communities in late Qing Sichuan by tracing how extraterritoriality operated in everyday judicial practice. Moving beyond accounts centered on anti-Christian violence, it analyzes how Chinese converts, non-Christian litigants, Western missionaries, and Qing officials navigated the uncertainties that imperial encroachment brought to local adjudication. Drawing on county-level judicial cases, imperial documents, local gazetteers, and missionary accounts, the study shows how Chinese Christians learned to mobilize missionary-backed extraterritorial leverage to circumvent routine barriers to legal access, while non-Christians and local elites developed strategic countermeasures to cope with foreign interference. Rather than treating extraterritoriality solely as a colonial imposition, the article argues that it became embedded as an additional, treaty-produced dimension of judicial operation in local society. By foregrounding the agency of multiple social actors, this study offers a nuanced account of how extraterritoriality reshaped late Qing local justice.

  2. Pulling Strings at the Margins: Blind Storytellers and the Politics of Survival in Socialist China, 1938–1977
    Yi Ren · pp. 675-705 · online 2026-08-04
    摘要

    This article examines the mobilization and governance of blind storytellers in Southeast Shanxi from 1938 to 1977, arguing that disabled bodies occupied a structurally ambivalent position within the Chinese Communist Party’s revolutionary project. Their mobility, endurance, and bodily labor enabled the extension of state power into rural society and were endowed with political value. However, these same qualities proved increasingly incompatible with socialist labor regimes organized around standardized productivity and administrative control. As a result, blind storytellers were progressively reclassified from effective propagandists into marginal or welfare-dependent subjects. Drawing on local archives and oral histories, this article shows how revolutionary inclusion did not resolve social difference but reorganized it, producing new forms of differentiated value and exclusion. The case of blind storytellers thus reveals a fundamental tension within socialist governance between incorporation and control.

  3. Hindered by Informal Property Relations: A Case Study of Developmental Strategies in Rural Industrialization in China
    Xinxing Duan · pp. 706-749 · online 2026-06-10
    摘要

    Why have existing developmental strategies failed to promote rural industrial development more effectively in many regions of China? This question cannot be fully answered by prevailing developmental state theory, which emphasizes the institutional conditions of embedded autonomy, market competition, and enterprise investment capacity, while overlooking the influence of informal property relations within industries. This article’s case study of an industrial township in northern China demonstrates that informal property relations, tied to entrepreneurs’ informal access to labor, land, and environmental resources, have been persistently maintained through socially embedded power interactions. These relations have contributed substantial profits to rural industries and greatly alleviated pressure on entrepreneurs, making them unwilling to reinvest profits into development activities. Instead, profits are allocated to simple industrial expansion or personal consumption. As a result, the developmental strategies fail to achieve their intended effects because of a lack of response from enterprises. Furthermore, this study examines two attempts by the government to formalize these property relations—the construction of industrial parks and more stringent environmental governance—and explains how their impact has been constrained. This research provides valuable insights for late-developing regions with lagging political and economic reforms and deepens our understanding of the substantive view of the economy.

  4. Public Opinion on Criminal Trial Proceedings in China: An Examination of a Capital Case
    Bin Liang, Jiaqi Yan, Ming Hu, Hua Zhong · pp. 750-785 · online 2026-08-26
    摘要

    Historically, opportunities for members of the public in China to observe criminal trials have been limited. Other than jurors and lay judges (“people’s assessors”), the public has rarely had opportunities to observe criminal trial proceedings in person. Along with China’s reform measures to increase judicial transparency, the move to broadcasting trials online via platforms like China Court Trial Online offers an invaluable opportunity for the public to observe trials. In this study, a fully recorded homicide trial in mainland China was shown to students enrolled in law majors (n=97) and non-law majors (n=86) from one Chinese university. Their evaluations of the trial proceedings were subsequently surveyed. This is the first known study that allows members of the public to watch a full-length trial and provide their assessments of criminal trial proceedings in China. The issues reported by students echo problems identified by scholars and shed significant light on potential lessons to be learned from public observation of Chinese criminal trials.

Journal of Contemporary Asia

Vol. 56, No. 5 · 2026-10-20 · 11 篇 · Routledge · Political economy of Asia · Peer-reviewed
  1. The Price of Freedom: Lèse-majesté Prosecutions and the Politics of Bail in Thailand
    Khorapin Phuaphansawat, Puangchon Unchanam · pp. 733-755 · online 2025-10-24
    摘要

    In recent years, Thai courts have arbitrarily used the lèse majesté law to prosecute hundreds of defendants. This use of lèse majesté has been an important part of a reaction against a movement that had been mobilised nationwide during 2020–2021 calling for reform of politics. Breaking the kingdom’s biggest taboo, the movement explicitly criticised the monarchy and called for monarchy reform. Besides an unprecedented number of cases, what distinguishes the recent wave of persecutions is the way the court handles the question of bail. In the past, almost all lèse majesté defendants were likely to be imprisoned from an early stage of investigation, and they were constantly denied bail. Recently, however, granting bail has been more common. This research examines the politics of bail in lèse majesté cases, arguing that the trend of granting bail should be considered a political tactic of the courts while maintaining the prosecution of disloyal subjects. Setting bail so high means ordinary people cannot reach it. Despite this high price for freedom, the monarchy reform movement and several civic associations manage to secure bail for most defendants. This article examines fundraising role as a form of resistance against the power of the court and the crown.

  2. Digital Defiance: Analysis of Public Responses to Youth Activism in Thailand, 2020–2023
    Kampanart Chaiyarat · pp. 756-783 · online 2026-02-18
    摘要

    During Thailand’s 2020–2023 wave of youth activism, once unsayable political topics moved into online debate, pushing the boundaries of public discourse. Anchored in the concept of Thai-Style Democracy, this article examines how discourse on Twitter/X shapes what becomes sayable, respectable, or sanctionable. The article argues that platformed talk did not merely reflect opinion but recalibrated legitimacy under Thai-Style Democracy. Using Critical Discourse Analysis of 4,705 tweets, five recurrent patterns were identified: (i) self-identification and labelling; (ii) rights, freedoms, and duties framed through a duty-first moral order; (iii) guardianist legitimacy and platform scaling; (iv) karmic idioms as moral justifications; and (v) conditional support and opposition tied to compatibility with duty and non-violation of royal authority. Together, these dynamics illuminated a paradox: expanding speakability around monarchy reform alongside a narrowing acceptability of youth activism. The analysis shows how online discourse participated in legitimating institutional responses and reshaping horizons of action. This study contributes to broader discussions of digital activism, generational conflict, and the resilience of Thai-Style Democracy under democratic pressure by linking the nuanced dynamics of online discourse to political events.

  3. Illicit Parallel Trading, State Permission, and Legality: A Comparative Analysis in the Southern Chinese Seaboard
    Lawrence Ka-ki Ho, KuoRay Mao, Henry Hin-Yan Chan, Alexander Hagan · pp. 784-811 · online 2026-03-03
    摘要

    This article applies institutional and media content analysis to investigate the legitimation and criminalisation of cross-border trade within the "Southern Chinese Seaboard," specifically between Taiwan and China and between Hong Kong and mainland China, a region characterised by diverse taxation and legal jurisdictions, as well as rapidly evolving political-economic and regional geo-political dynamics. This study re-evaluates common media portrayals and simplistic perceptions of "illicit" and "parallel trading" as merely spontaneous actions by individuals embedded in criminal networks. Instead, we posit that such activities stem from deliberately vague taxation and legal definitions, as well as enforcement strategies resulting from collusion between state and non-state actors. Utilising the "Regime of Permission" framework, this study examines how political actors manipulate the definitions of licit and illicit trading to achieve political objectives, thereby creating loopholes that non-state actors exploit. The findings demonstrate that the delineation and oversight of illicit trade have evolved in response to shifting economic relationships and the development of enforcement institutions within a changing geo-political landscape, offering deeper insight into the state's role in creating and maintaining liminal spaces that regulate the boundaries between lawful, unlawful, and socially illicit trade.

  4. Banditry in the Sundarbans: The Political Economy of a Mangrove Frontier
    Md. Tanvir Mahtab, M. Anwar Hossen, Md. Shihabul Islam · pp. 812-829 · online 2026-03-05
    摘要

    This study examines the political economy of forest-centred banditry in the Sundarbans, the world's largest mangrove forest, where the Ganges, Brahmaputra, and Meghna rivers meet. It focuses on the roles played by patronage networks in banditry. Employing a qualitative and case study approach, the research seeks to provide a comprehensive understanding of the political economy of forest-centred banditry. The interviews were conducted in the Mongla and Rampal areas of Bagerhat district, Bangladesh. Findings reveal a deeply entrenched system of exploitation and control. Local business elites (mahajans) and political actors exploit individuals through the dadni debt system and political manipulation, coercing them into banditry. This system is sustained through hierarchical structures led by boro bhai (big brother figures) and enforced by violence. Bandits abduct fishermen for ransom, with local politicians acting as intermediaries, collecting significant amounts annually. Businessmen support bandit gangs to monopolise natural resources, resulting in fierce competition among traders. Additionally, powerful figures profit from illegal enterprises such as the arms trade and prostitution. Further, the study identifies a deep relationship between bandits and local government officials, highlighting systemic corruption through monthly payments for protection and information.

  5. Unequal Chains: Global Value Chains and Income Disparity
    Kay Kiang Tan, Andrew Jia Yi Kam, M. Niaz Asadullah · pp. 830-865 · online 2026-02-24
    摘要

    As countries deepen their integration into global value chains (GVCs) to pursue industrial growth and economic advancement, a critical question remains unresolved: Who truly benefits from this participation? This study examines the effects of GVC participation on income disparities within the manufacturing sector. Three measures of income inequality are employed – value-added distribution, wage distribution, and labour income share – based on the World Bank Economic Enterprise Survey data for 44 countries and 16 manufacturing industries between 2006 and 2016. The results reveal that the choice of inequality measure is crucial. GVC participation reduces income inequality in value-added and wage distribution but increases inequality in labour income share. At the country level, backward and forward linkages reduce income inequality in lower-income economies, while effects are not significant in upper-middle- and high-income countries. Regionally, Asia shows more equitable value-added through backward linkages and equitable wages through overall GVC and forward linkages. However, overall GVC participation and forward linkages are also associated with a decline in labour income share, suggesting that while GVC integration may improve wage equality, the proportion of value-added accruing to labour tends to shrink.

  6. Indonesia’s Illiberal Capitalism and the 2025 Protests: A Reply to Wijaya and Jayasuriya
    Abdil Mughis Mudhoffir · pp. 866-880 · online 2026-06-08
    摘要

    Indonesia was less affected by the 2008 global crisis than many developed capitalist economies, but can it be said to have followed a similar path, leading to austerity that deepens disincorporation of the subordinate classes and the decline of liberal democracy as a source of legitimacy for capitalism? Wijaya and Jayasuriya suggest so. However, it cannot be forgotten that Indonesia’s capitalism has long relied on an illiberal political-legal order as a means of accumulation and legitimacy, despite post-1998 democratisation. This arrangement persists because there is neither a coherent progressive movement capable of shifting the balance of power nor a rupture within the capitalist fractions that introduces a new way to sustain bourgeois hegemony. Hence, characterising Indonesia as evolving towards Poulantzas’s model of authoritarian statism overlooks the specificity of the mode of capitalist accumulation. What has happened in Indonesia is the deepening of the already illiberal mode, often provoking mass protests. However, this new direction is not the result of a post-crisis re-organisation of capital but rather arises from the logic of accumulation in which sections of oligarchic alliance seek to further concentrate power and wealth in the absence of the organised left.

  7. State, Capital, and the Crisis of Social Reproduction in Indonesia: A Rejoinder
    Trissia Wijaya, Kanishka Jayasuriya · pp. 881-891 · online 2026-06-08
    摘要

    This rejoinder to Mudhoffir (Citation2026) identifies three key theoretical points of disagreement in the response to our commentary on Indonesian protests of 2025 (Wijaya and Jayasuriya Citation2026). First, the “authoritarian continuity thesis” advanced by our critics misses the way in which the “rule of capital” is organised within the state apparatus. The response to our article rests on an instrumentalist reading of the Indonesian state, which is contrasted to a more structural understanding set out in our 2026 commentary. Second, this perspective enables a better comprehension of the shifts in the form of authoritarian political regimes in response to a variety of often overlapping crises. Third, protests must be understood not simply as a set of grievances about elite impunity but signify a fundamental shift in the structure of the working or labouring class, which is itself the product of capitalist transformation over the last decades. Here, it is contended that these conflicts now find expression in new forms of politics over access to means of social reproduction.

Online first（尚未編入期別）· 3
  1. Invisible Brokers, Visible Haze: Value Chains and the Political Ecology of Northern Thailand’s Maize Economy
    Danny Marks, Songkran Jantakad · pp. 1-28 · online 2026-10-28
    摘要

    Each dry season, thick haze blankets northern Thailand. Maize cultivation, driven largely by demand for animal feed, is one of its most politically contested sources. Policy responses often target farmers through burning bans, technical fixes, and behavioural change campaigns. This article focuses on the middlemen who link upland maize fields to downstream feed markets. It shows that brokers, traders, and silo operators, by controlling access to credit, logistics, and markets, translate corporate demand, price volatility, and regulatory pressures into everyday farming practices. Moral economies of debt, reciprocity, and patronage intersect with buyer-driven value chain pressures to shape brokerage practices and disperse accountability for environmental harm. These practices reinforce farmers’ dependence on maize, and make burning appear the most viable option while displacing responsibility onto farmers and shielding large agribusinesses. The article demonstrates that market power operates through everyday social relations of credit, trust, and loyalty to reproduce unequal commodity relations. This helps explain why haze persists despite repeated policy interventions. The article integrates middlemen into environmental governance analyses, shows that value chain governance and moral economy combine to reproduce environmental injustice, and contributes to meatways scholarship, tracing the intermediary networks through which national and regional meat demand reproduces environmental harm.

  2. Shopkeepers of Chinatown: Singapore’s Business World in Motion, 1819–2025: Loh Kah Seng and Stephen Dobbs. Petaling Jaya: Strategic Information and Research Development Centre, 2026.
    Jeremy Goh · pp. 1-4 · online 2026-10-26
    摘要

    Small and medium enterprises (SMEs) are vital drivers of Singapore’s economy, accounting for more than 99% of locally incorporated firms and employing 70% of the local workforce in 2025 (Straits Ti...

  3. The Contours Of Working-Class Debt: Precarity, Gender, And Labour Discipline In Cambodia’s Construction Sector
    Michele Ford, Vichhra Mouyly · pp. 1-20 · online 2026-10-21
    摘要

    This article argues that in Cambodia’s construction sector, overall levels of precarity play a key role in shaping the extent to which debt functions as a mechanism of labour discipline. Building on LeBaron’s reconceptualisation of debt bondage as a class-based form of labour discipline, it situates worker indebtedness within the broader structural conditions through which it acquires coercive force. Drawing on data collected from 600 workers employed in cement factories and on construction sites, the article reveals that debt’s coercive power stems less from its absolute burden than from its interaction with employment precarity. Although workers in both sub-sectors faced significant indebtedness, cement factory workers benefited from relatively stable employment, monthly wages, and access to social security, whereas construction workers were paid irregularly, experienced wage theft, and were excluded from social protection. These dynamics operated in profoundly gendered ways, reflecting women’s household financial responsibilities and difficulties securing formal credit. By foregrounding the relationship between indebtedness and structural precarity, the article argues that it is the configuration of precarity itself that determines the depth of debt-based coercion in contemporary Cambodia.

Asian Diasporic Visual Cultures and the Americas

Vol. 11, No. 1-2 · 2026-10-09 · 14 篇 · Brill · Asian diasporic art & visual culture · Peer-reviewed
  1. Expanding Self-Portraiture: Visual Narratives of Roots and Routes
    Sarah Magnatta, Yi Yi Mon (Rosaline) Kyo · pp. 1-10
  2. Archaeology of the “Self”: Kenneth Tam Standing in Discontinuous Becoming(s)
    Ihnmi Jon · pp. 11-35
    摘要

    This article examines contemporary artist Kenneth Tam’s recent body of work, focusing on a series of sculptural pieces in his larger project, Tender is the Hand Which Holds the Stone of Memory. It explores how the artist engages with past moments of atrocity and violence through his artmaking, incorporating research components such as archival and site visits as a basis for his processes of casting, manipulation, and juxtaposition of objects purchased online. Through close analysis of the visual and material properties of the objects he crafts, as well as the historical and institutional contexts within which they are enmeshed, this article suggests that Tam’s works serve as his “self-portrait.” I argue that he leverages his artmaking as a means to explore a changing sense of “self,” acting as a co-constitutive force in reshaping the dynamics of custom, ritual, and memory—dynamics that have not only enabled but also delimited his aspiration for navigating the discontinuities in his becoming.

  3. “Unlikely Friendship”: Jimmy Tsutomu Mirikitani’s Life-Narrating Art and The Cats of Mirikitani
    Maki Kaneko · pp. 36-67
    摘要

    This article explores how painter and collagist Jimmy Tsutomu Mirikitani (1920–2012) narrated his life through art, alongside his collaboration with Linda Hattendorf, the director of the 2006 documentary The Cats of Mirikitani . Drawing on scholarship in life writing studies and art history, it examines the ways in which Mirikitani’s street art and life narratives—created in collaboration with pedestrians while he was unhoused in New York between the late 1980s and 2001—challenge conventional forms of auto/biography. The article also analyzes The Cats of Mirikitani as a collaborative project between Mirikitani and Hattendorf, revealing the political potential of such collaborations. Through the case study of Mirikitani’s collaborative life narration, it emphasizes the significance of cross-ethnic collaboration and intersubjective modes of life narration in fostering new forms of community and understanding across social and racial divides.

  4. Migration and Memory: Navigating Intersectional Identity Through Sarindar Dhaliwal’s Triple Self-Portrait with Persimmons and Pomegranates
    Roopa Kanal · pp. 68-86
    摘要

    This article analyzes Sarindar Dhaliwal’s autobiographical artwork, Triple Self-Portrait with Persimmons and Pomegranates (1988) , exploring how her life, marked by geographical transitions, shaped her identity and artistic vision. By engaging with critical scholarship on memory, place, and identity, this study discusses how Dhaliwal’s use of landscape imagery connects her emotionally to the colonial legacy as a member of the South Asian diaspora. Simultaneously, through the multilayered visual language and techniques employed in the artwork, Dhaliwal engages with shifting post-colonial theories, offering a decentred, globalized view of her art and identity. This paper argues that through self-portraits embedded in the artwork, Dhaliwal imparts a sense of temporality, mirroring the evolution of her identity as an artist. As one of the first South Asian female artists in Canada with a career spanning over 40 years, Dhaliwal’s contributions to Canadian and the diaspora South Asian visual cultures are path-breaking.

  5. How Could One Paint a Self Portrait! Identity and Erasure in the Work of Rasheed Araeen
    Karen Greenwalt · pp. 87-106
    摘要

    Throughout the late 1970s and early 1980s, the contemporary Pakistani artist Rasheed Araeen created a series of self-portraits in response to the institutional racism he experienced in London. Araeen described one such self-portrait as “trying to deal with my own existential reality, the reality of a [B]lack person living in a white (racist) society.” 1 A careful analysis of his work reveals that not only was he barred from participating in discourses of minimalism, but that self-portraiture—as it is conceived of in Eurocentric discourses—is also foreclosed to Araeen. His work thus underscores the strict definitional boundaries of art historical categories, which inherently and unconditionally exclude the voice of the Other. Read through the work of Franz Fanon, Araeen’s self-portraits reveal how the Black, colonized body is denied any notion of a fully embodied self, but rather how their identity is fixed and determined by the white gaze.

  6. Beyond Japanese Crafts: Kōgei in Brazil from a Transcultural Perspective
    Liliana Morais, Michiko Okano · pp. 107-128
    摘要

    As craftspeople migrated between Japan and Brazil, Japanese crafts ( kōgei ) underwent negotiations, adaptations, and transformations. This article explores the transference of kōgei from Japan to Brazil, beginning with a historical examination of the term within the context of East-West dialogues at the turn of the nineteenth century and the challenges of translation. In the twentieth century, as Japanese immigrants formed a discrete community in Brazil, kōgei, as a category of objects and a system of value, was introduced in the country. We document the institutionalization of kōgei through an exhibition and association and trace the professional paths of four ceramic practitioners: two Japanese migrants and two Japanese Brazilians. These trajectories illustrate how kōgei , through its integration into Brazilian society, has evolved from a distinct Japanese cultural practice into a transcultural phenomenon that crosses national, cultural, and ethnic boundaries.

  7. Extending Ornamentalism: Yellow Woman as Decolonial Object/Plant/Land/Person in Kelly Akashi’s Formations Exhibition
    Phuong T. Vuong · pp. 129-151
    摘要

    This article engages with ornamentalism, a critical theory often presented as a problematic condition of feminized Asian personhood/objecthood. In this article, I argue that ornamentalism can disrupt settler colonialism and geontopower, as well as the dominant social and epistemological construction of Life and Nonlife. By examining Kelly Akashi’s Formations exhibition at the Museum of Contemporary Art San Diego, I extend ornamentalism as an opportunity for Asian Americans to witness their historical and present conditions on Indigenous land, specifically through Japanese American incarceration. In this article, I examine the exhibition and the ways in which Akashi entangles objects, plants, land, and bodies; share my racialized and gendered encounter as an object at the museum; and discuss how Akashi’s work challenges viewers to see the liveliness of “nonliving” “objects” through their slow witnessing and low activity. Akashi's disruption of the boundaries around land-based materials and “humans” questions geontological and economic discourses that uphold settler colonialism.

Asian Studies Review

  1. The Review at Fifty: Celebrating Five Decades of Asian Studies Review

    摘要

    In 2026, the Asian Studies Review (ASR) marked the publication of its 50th volume. Known today as the flagship journal of the Asian Studies Association of Australia (ASAA), the ASR was founded in 1...

  2. Trilateral Relations in Northeast Asia
    Mason Richey · pp. 727-741 · online 2026-08-27
    摘要

    The aim of this introductory essay is to both discuss the overall topic of this Special Issue – trilateralism in Northeast Asia – and briefly present the five research articles that follow. The Introduction begins by explaining the choice of the theme for the Special Issue, and why there are empirico-policy and theoretical reasons to examine trilateral relations in Northeast Asia through a series of dedicated research articles. The second part of the Introduction covers several of those empirico-policy and theoretical reasons in depth, including how trilateral relations in Northeast Asia contribute to power balancing and how information asymmetries within trilateral groupings can make cooperation challenging. Special attention is placed on examining how trilaterals (as a form of minilateral) have the upside of filling some gaps in the international security architecture, while nonetheless also representing potential downside risk through heightened security dilemma dynamics. The penultimate section of the Introduction provides a brief synopsis of the main argument of each research article in the collection. Finally, the fourth section concludes the Introduction by tying together the main points, contextualising them within the evolution of contemporary Northeast Asia, and signposting the value of the articles for scholars and policymakers.

  3. Trilaterals and the Reorganisation of Northeast Asia’s Regional Order
    Sung Chul Jung, Jagannath Panda, Jae Jeok Park · pp. 742-757 · online 2026-08-06
    摘要

    In an era of intensifying competition over the international order in Northeast Asia, both major and non-major states have increasingly sought to use trilateral mechanisms distinct from bilateral and multilateral forms. In this context, this article aims to discuss various types, origins, and influences of trilateral relations in international politics, as well as current and historical trilateral relations that have contributed to the emergence and transformation of regional orders. First, it categorises trilateral relations into four types: (1) three partners, (2) three rivals, (3) two partners against one rival, and (4) two rivals competing for one (potential) partner, encompassing cooperative and conflictual relationships among both three parties and three bilateral relations and clarifying each type’s characteristics and dynamics. Then, it offers a comprehensive way to understand the origins of trilateral relations, identifying three different, but complementary, sources for the formation of trilateral relations: structural balance of power, strategic interests, and a mix of structure and strategy. Lastly, it explores power dynamics both of and within trilateral relations, focusing on three dimensions of power: the trilateral’s collective power as a group, the power dynamics within trilateral relations, and the structural power exerted by the trilateral over its individual component states.

  4. Minilateralism and Trilateral Cooperation in Northeast Asia: Implications for Regional Security and Order-Building
    Daewon Ohn, Er-Win Tan, Hyun Chung Yoo · pp. 758-777 · online 2026-01-14
    摘要

    This article examines the evolving dynamics of minilateralism and trilateral cooperation in Northeast Asia, and assesses their implications for regional security and order-building. It traces the development of minilateral frameworks in the region since the end of the Cold War, focusing in particular on two key trilateral cases: security cooperation between the United States, Japan, and South Korea, and functional cooperation between China, Japan, and South Korea through the Trilateral Cooperation Secretariat (TCS). It is argued that US–Japan–South Korea trilateralism, which was initially shaped by the need to address the North Korean nuclear challenge, has become a core element of the US-led security architecture aimed at balancing China’s growing influence in the Indo–Pacific. By contrast, the TCS reflects a pragmatic form of minilateralism centred on economic and technical cooperation. Yet its effectiveness has been constrained by deep-seated geopolitical tensions and institutional limitations, particularly amid intensifying US–China rivalry. The article concludes that while trilateral minilateralism offers new avenues for coordination, it also risks exacerbating security dilemmas. Strengthening institutional mechanisms for strategic communication, confidence-building, and risk reduction will be essential to supporting regional stability and order-building.

  5. A Comparative Analysis of the China Policies of Japan and South Korea Amid Sino–US Confrontation, 2017–2024: An Ontological Security Perspective
    Sachio Nakato, Mu Ren · pp. 778-796 · online 2026-08-06
    摘要

    This study compares the China policies of Japan and South Korea under escalating US–China strategic competition and asserts that both states’ policy approaches reflect ontological security pursuits. The analysis illustrates how Tokyo and Seoul formulate policies that align with their self-perceived identities within established relational frameworks with the two superpowers to preserve ontological security. The findings reveal that ontological insecurities stemming from abrupt systemic changes or contentious interactions by China drive both states towards strengthened US alignment. Conversely, when ontological insecurities originate from problematic engagements with the US or when there is no insecurity, both states demonstrate an inclination to revitalise economic cooperation with China. The respective patterns of China-engagement strategies and US-aligned balancing against China differ markedly, rooted in distinct identities and interactions among states that generate varying levels of ontological insecurity. By integrating ontological security theory with an empirical analysis of East Asian strategic adaptations to great-power rivalry, the article enhances the theoretical understanding of state behaviour in international relations.

  6. How China, the ROK, and DPRK Balance Each Other’s Security Interests: Dynamic Analysis of Conflict and Cooperation
    Li Li, Dongchan Kim · pp. 797-814 · online 2026-08-17
    摘要

    This article draws on typological approaches to trilateral relations to construct three models characterising the China–South Korea–North Korea triangle. These models – the confrontational alignment model, mediation model, and limited cooperation model – represent different patterns of interaction within this trilateral relationship. To understand why and how these models evolve, the article employs a level-of-analysis framework that identifies key explanatory variables at three interrelated dimensions: systemic, processual, and unit-level factors. By integrating international structure, interactional processes, and domestic actor attributes, this framework reveals the complex dynamics shaping trilateral relations. Given intensified US–China strategic rivalry and North Korea’s accelerated nuclear development, this combined approach highlights emerging and evolving characteristics in China–South Korea–North Korea relations.

  7. What Minilateralism Can and Can’t Do in Cross-Taiwan Strait Conflict Management
    Ming-Min Yang, Kwei-Bo Huang · pp. 815-834 · online 2026-07-12
    摘要

    What can – and can’t – minilateralism contribute to the management of cross-Taiwan Strait conflict? Although US-linked minilateral mechanisms have multiplied across the Indo–Pacific, their concrete relevance to Taiwan’s security has not been systematically assessed. To address this gap, the study develops a comparative framework focused on policy signalling, deterrence by punishment and by denial, and escalation dynamics, and applies it to three prominent cases: the US–Japan–South Korea trilateral, the Quad, and TSD. The analysis finds that these minilaterals generate distinct and uneven security effects. The US–Japan–South Korea trilateral provides the strongest signalling and denial leverage due to institutionalised intelligence-sharing and operational coordination. Across all mechanisms, escalation risks persist and domestic political constraints hinder consistent implementation. The article clarifies the mechanisms through which minilateralism shapes China’s cost–benefit calculus, and delineates the boundaries of what minilateral settings can realistically achieve. Minilateralism cannot substitute for Taiwan’s self-defence, but it meaningfully shapes China’s cost–benefit calculus by raising uncertainty, reducing vulnerabilities, and reinforcing a more resilient Indo–Pacific security environment.

  8. The Politics of Regionalism in the Mekong: Institutional Bypass and Bricolage
    Shintaro Hamanaka · pp. 835-857 · online 2026-06-14
    摘要

    Regionalism in the Mekong has continued to proliferate over the last three decades, despite some ups and downs, but the reasons for this persistent proliferation remain under-explained. The hypothesis of this study is that ‘institutional bypass’, a concept originally developed by international lawyers, provides the logic behind the proliferation of regional institutions in the Mekong. This is a top-down force to bypass existing institutions, often exercised by states in forming regionalism at the international level. In addition, the study draws on the insights of geographers, who have argued that ‘institutional bricolage’ helps to explain how regionalism is rooted in local communities. This is a bottom-up force to re-use and re-shape existing institutional elements associated with regionalism, rather than creating new ones. After analysing the politics of regional institution-building in the Mekong, which seems to support the institutional bypass hypothesis, this article considers how best to synthesise the two seemingly opposing logics – namely, institutional bypass and institutional bricolage. The article proposes an integrated analytical framework that explains the coexistence of various types of regionalism in and beyond the Mekong.

  9. Speaking Back to National History in Thailand: Lanna Historiography and the Question of Colonialism
    Tinakrit Sireerat, Søren Ivarsson, Waraporn Ruangsri · pp. 858-876 · online 2026-03-26
    摘要

    This article examines how the term ‘colonialism’ has been employed in the historiography of Lanna, a historical region in Northern Thailand. It traces the evolution of scholarly debates surrounding Siam’s incorporation of Lanna and analyses how the colonial lens has influenced interpretations of state domination and local resistance. Some scholars explicitly adopt colonialism as a critical lens to highlight asymmetrical power relations and cultural suppression. Some regard it as mere contextual background or a neutral temporal marker for economic branding. Others reject it altogether, instead emphasising the unevenness of modernisation and its socioeconomic consequences. Recent studies have increasingly used the colonial lens to critique Siamese policies, drawing parallels between Siam’s state-building strategies and European colonial practices. However, this article also highlights the potential pitfalls of replacing the teleology of nationalism or modernisation with that of colonialism by overemphasising Siam’s colonising agency, which risks obscuring the uneven, negotiated, and at times resistant engagement of Lanna actors with the Siamese state.

  10. Classical Music Education as National Inculcation: The Case of Crypto-Colonial Thailand
    Hei Ting Wong · pp. 877-894 · online 2026-06-21
    摘要

    Thailand, a ‘crypto-colony’ that has officially never been colonised but has been heavily dependent on Western powers, boasts a so-called ‘classical music tradition’ that has endured periods of neglect and even prohibition before being revived and officially embraced as part of national culture. Education has played a key role in broader civilising movements and, since the 1970s, in the revival of Thai classical music. Through fieldwork and interviews conducted in schools across central Thailand, I examine how Thai classical music is taught and learned in institutionalised educational settings. From making documentation to practising the music, appropriate manners and behaviours are embedded into both teachers’ and students’ daily lives. Education thus functions not only as a means of transmitting traditional musical knowledge and skills, but also as a tool for inculcating and (re-)constructing notions of Thai civilisation, culture, and tradition, within the habitus of institutionalised education in modern Thai society. Using Thai classical music education as a case study, I argue that in crypto-colonial contexts, the (re-)invention of continuous cultural traditions functions not only as a strategy for cultural preservation, but also as a means of asserting civility and modernity independent of Western influence, thereby claiming equivalent status with colonial counterparts.

  11. The Adaptation of Silang Tanmu : Cultural Censorship, Performance Governance, and Negotiation in Cold War Taiwan
    Siyue Liu · pp. 895-911 · online 2026-06-21
    摘要

    This article examines two state-sanctioned adaptations of the Peking opera Silang Tanmu (四郎探母, Fourth Son visits His Mother) in Taiwan to illuminate the interaction of political ideology, grassroots pressure, and cultural governance under Kuomintang (KMT, Nationalist) rule. After retreating to Taiwan in 1949, the KMT promoted Peking opera as an instrument of cultural orthodoxy and a means of politically mobilising waishengren (mainlander) soldiers and civilians. However, Silang Tanmu became politically contentious when some critics argued that its narrative did not embody anti-communist values. This coincided with the harshest phase of martial-law authoritarianism and led to the first official adaptation in the 1950s. Produced under intense political repression, this version left no meaningful space for artistic agency and was ultimately rejected by performers and audiences. By the 1970s, however, Taiwan’s gradual political opening provided space for cultural workers and audiences to exercise greater agency in shaping the opera’s form and messaging, resulting in a second, more accepted adaptation that preserved the original structure while modestly aligning itself with state ideology. The contested revisions of Silang Tanmu thus reveal both the limits of top-down cultural control and the historically contingent negotiations among state, society, and cultural producers in Taiwan.

  12. Learning to be Feminists from Their Own Lives: Interpreting Feminist Identity Among Female Chinese College Students
    Zi-Xuan Pu, Miao Yu · pp. 912-929 · online 2026-08-03
    摘要

    This study focuses on the identity and subjective experience of female Chinese college students who self-identify as feminists, adopting an interpretive qualitative method to explore their interpretation of feminist identity and related experiences. Semi-structured interviews were conducted with 16 female college students, and the interview data were analysed using thematic analysis. Findings conclusively reveal that these students’ identity construction is rooted in daily embodied practices, whereby continuous dialectics between subjective experiences and critical reflexivity foster dynamically fluid feminist frameworks. Female Chinese college students generally self-identify as feminists rather than subscribing to specific theoretical definitions of feminism. Feminist identities are shaped by their learning experiences, are dynamic and fluid, and serve as a means of navigating real-life situations. In their feminist practices, the research participants interpret and fight against their situation as ‘outsiders-within’. They strategically use feminist identities as a means of learning and a way of reconstructing their lives. We show that the participants’ intersectional identities and feminist consciousness developed embodied standpoints from the margins. This study therefore contributes to the field of Chinese feminist knowledge by offering a perspective on the development of feminist identity among female college students.

  1. Equalism Among Young South Korean Men: A Localised Form of Antifeminism
    Minyoung Moon, Gowoon Jung · pp. 1-16 · online 2026-10-28
    摘要

    Young men and women in South Korea have increasingly divergent views on gender equality, with more young men openly expressing antifeminist sentiments. This study examines the prevalence of antifeminism among young Korean men by analysing their perceptions of feminism and the underlying reasons contributing to their antifeminist attitudes. Based on interviews with 23 Korean men in their 20s, we find that while these men acknowledge gender inequality in Korea, they reject Korean feminism and instead advocate for an alternative ideology called equalism. Distinct from, yet overlapping with, modern sexism, we argue that their stance of equalism is grounded in four interrelated beliefs: 1) acknowledgement of gender inequality and nominal support for gender equality as an ideal, 2) support for gender equality, only insofar as it does not challenge existing male privilege, 3) belief in fairness and meritocracy as the primary pathways to gender equality, while overlooking structural causes of inequality, and 4) critique of perceived extreme feminist methods and endorsement of a more moderate, merit-based approach reflected in equalist discourse. Through this research, we contribute to the literature on antifeminism, misogyny, and modern sexism by offering a nuanced understanding of its local manifestation in South Korea.

  2. Framing the Crisis: The Chinese Mainland’s Guidance of Popular Nationalism in the Context of Nancy Pelosi’s Visit to Taiwan
    Le Hu · pp. 1-24 · online 2026-10-28
    摘要

    This study examined whether the Chinese government could guide nationalism to its benefit in a sovereignty-related incident. To answer this question, it used the case study of the visit to Taiwan in 2022 by the former Speaker of the US House of Representatives, Nancy Pelosi, and an innovative framework that combines framing theory and social identity theory, alongside indicators to measure the extent to which the government achieves its goals in guiding nationalism. Based on more than 6,000 comments on the visit, the research finds that the Chinese mainland maintained a certain degree of control over nationalism even in a high-stakes case such as the Pelosi visit. However, the article also highlights the limitations to the government’s guidance of nationalism. Moving beyond the binary conclusions of the existing literature, the study offers a more nuanced account by identifying factors influencing public acceptance of official narratives and by proposing concrete measures for assessing the effectiveness of attempts to guide state-led nationalism.

  3. Reframing Hong Kong: Legitimation in the Post-National Security Law Era
    Stephan Ortmann · pp. 1-21 · online 2026-10-26
    摘要

    In 2020, the Chinese government imposed the National Security Law (NSL) in Hong Kong, which transformed the liberal authoritarian regime into an illiberal one. This article examines how the government has tried to legitimise this regime change while promoting the growing integration of the territory into the Chinese state. It does so by analysing a newly created National Security Exhibition Gallery unveiled in 2024, which displays the new political narratives that are used to justify the changes. The article argues that the regime’s legitimacy discourse has shifted from institutional and procedural foundations to one centred on nationalism and the Chinese Communist Party, while continuing to invoke the longstanding rhetoric of ‘stability and prosperity’. Through a critical reading of the exhibit’s visual and textual narratives, the article shows how the state seeks to reshape public memory, reframe dissent, and align Hong Kong’s identity with the ideological norms of the mainland.

  4. Cigarette Girl , Netflix, and Soft Commodity Nationalism in Indonesia
    Marina Welker · pp. 1-20 · online 2026-10-25
    摘要

    With its 2023 Indonesian historical fiction series Cigarette Girl, Netflix sought to grow its market in the world’s fourth most populous country. Based on a novel by Ratih Kumala, the Netflix series sets personal drama and business intrigue against a historical backdrop of postcolonial nationalism, political violence, and rapid growth in the clove-laced tobacco cigarette (kretek) industry. The series condemns 1960s-era patriarchy and state-sponsored violence but ultimately creates a redemptive arc for the nation and positive representations of the kretek industry, disengaging from the political nature of state massacres and the tobacco-related diseases killing more than 250,000 Indonesians a year. Analysing the series as a ‘commodity nationalist’ text that closely identifies kretek with Indonesian national history and culture, this article illuminates how commodity nationalism – like other nationalist projects – entails forms of forgetting and exclusionary class, gender, and racial dynamics.

  5. What Needs to be Governed? Hip-Hop Cultural Governance in China through the Lens of Modifications to Rap Music Lyrics
    Tianyi Yang, Ge Zhu, Yilin Luo, Fang Wu · pp. 1-23 · online 2026-10-18
    摘要

    Rap music, as a core element of hip-hop culture, is often celebrated for its subcultural resistance but has undergone significant transformation in China amid processes of mainstreaming and state cultural governance. While previous research has highlighted the localisation and commercialisation of Chinese hip-hop, limited attention has been paid to the micro-level textual shifts in the mainstreaming of Chinese rap music. This study investigates how Chinese state ideology exerts an explicit and subtle impact on popular culture through the case of The Rap of China, a widely viewed hip-hop competition show. By analysing 934 pairs of live and studio versions of songs using thematic and critical discourse analysis, the study identifies three major patterns of lyrical modification: explicit depoliticisation, subtle depoliticisation, and the elimination of ‘badness’. We find that content perceived as politically sensitive or morally inappropriate is systematically altered, transforming hip-hop from a rebellious subculture into an ideologically compliant tool for moral education and social harmony. These transformations are not solely top-down; they reflect a negotiation between state agencies, streaming platforms, and artists under China’s model of cultural governance. This study contributes to understanding how subcultural forms are restructured to serve both market logics and ideological purposes in contemporary China.

Japan Forum

Vol. 38, No. 4 · 2026-10-02 · 8 篇 · Routledge (BAJS) · Japan · Peer-reviewed
  1. BCOF at eighty: new perspectives on the British commonwealth occupation force in the occupation of Japan
    Jonathan Bull, Steven Ivings · pp. 489-501 · online 2026-10-02
    摘要

    The British Commonwealth Occupation Force (BCOF) component of the Allied Occupation of Japan (1945-1952) has often been overlooked by researchers because of its minimal role in policy-making. Taking the opportunity of the 80th anniversary of the arrival of the first BCOF personnel in Japan, this special issue introduces new perspectives about the organisation based on a range of hitherto unused or under-utilised sources. The introduction begins by emphasising how BCOF’s commanding officer sought to draw attention to his troops’ contribution to the Occupation. There follows a brief overview of the existing secondary research about BCOF in English and in Japanese. The latter half of the introduction outlines the contribution that the four articles make to the special issue. Taken as a whole, the purpose of the special issue is to begin to reassess how the occupiers and the occupied understood the role and meaning of BCOF. The introduction concludes with a call for other researchers to take part in the process of making this reassessment.

  2. Japan’s ‘multiple occupations’: ceremonial parades, ritual practices, and the performance of authority in the BCOF
    Toshihiko Kishi · pp. 502-522 · online 2026-03-18
    摘要

    The Allied occupation of Japan is most commonly remembered through the dominant image of General Douglas MacArthur and a largely American-led occupation regime. Such representations, reinforced by political authority, the concentration of media institutions in Tokyo, and post-war commemorative practices, have long framed the occupation as a singular and coherent project. This article challenges that narrative by advancing the concept of ‘multiple occupations’ to capture the uneven, regionally differentiated realities of occupation governance in post-war Japan. Rather than treating regional diversity as a secondary effect of a unified occupation, the concept foregrounds how authority itself was constituted through differentiated practices across space. Focusing on the early activities of the BCOF (British Commonwealth Occupation Force), a multinational and multi-ethnic force operating primarily in the Chūgoku and Shikoku regions between mid-1946 and mid-1948, the article examines how occupation authority was performed, negotiated, and sustained beyond Tokyo. Although formally subordinate to GHQ/SCAP (General Headquarters/Supreme Commander for the Allied Powers), the BCOF exercised a degree of practical autonomy in day-to-day regional administration while facing internal tensions rooted in imperial hierarchies, racialised divisions, and declining morale. The article further argues that ceremonial practices – notably military parades, musical performances, and funeral processions – played a central role in managing these challenges. Rather than treating such rituals as peripheral or purely symbolic, this article conceptualises them as practical mechanisms of governance. These ceremonies functioned simultaneously as outward-facing performances that communicated legitimacy to Japanese audiences and as inward-facing practices that disciplined personnel and reinforced cohesion within a fragmented occupying force. Drawing on military newspapers, Japanese local and national press, and archival photographs, the study foregrounds the regional experience of occupation and the cultural performance of authority in public space. By recovering the overlooked role of the BCOF and situating it within a framework of multiple occupations, this article complicates dominant narratives of post-war Japan and contributes to broader debates on power, representation, and transnational encounter in modern Japanese history.

  3. British veteran reflections on the British Commonwealth Occupation Force and experiences in Japan during the Korean War
    Steven Ivings · pp. 523-544 · online 2025-10-27
    摘要

    British Commonwealth participation in the Allied Occupation of Japan is a largely overlooked episode in post-war history. Yet at peak, the British Commonwealth Occupation Forces (BCOF) stationed in Japan numbered a sizeable 37,000 personnel from Australia, India, New Zealand and Britain, spread across the Chūgoku and Shikoku regions, including Hiroshima and the key naval port of Kure. BCOF’s missions were to pursue the demilitarisation and demobilisation of Japan, and to promote democratisation, but historians have tended to downplay BCOF’s achievements or overlook it entirely. This paper reassesses BCOF by considering its afterlife during the Korean War, as the British Commonwealth Forces Korea (BCFK) overlapped with BCOF and continued to use its facilities beyond BCOF’s disbanding in 1952. Examining approximately 800 reflections of former British BCOF/BCFK service personnel collected in the 1990s and early 2000s, this paper argues that for many former personnel the experience in Japan helped them to reconcile with Britain’s former wartime enemy (Japan) through personal interactions with Japanese people that generated mutual respect and lasting friendships. In this way BCOF was, for British veterans, an important part of facilitating an improvement in Anglo-Japanese relations at a grassroots level.

  4. Occupied Japan through British eyes: recollections of the veterans of the British Commonwealth Occupation Force (BCOF) and the British Commonwealth Forces Korea (BCFK)
    Hidekazu Sensui · pp. 545-567 · online 2025-09-30
    摘要

    This study examines the perceptions by veterans who served in the British Commonwealth Occupation Force (BCOF) and the British Commonwealth Forces Korea (BCFK) of the Japanese during their post-war deployment in Japan. Drawing on the recollections of 819 veterans, it explores how such perceptions were shaped by their experiences—ranging from encounters with the devastation of Hiroshima and interactions with local communities to lingering memories of wartime atrocities. While resentment arising from the mistreatment of prisoners of war frequently influenced their attitudes, interactions with Japanese civilians fostered a more nuanced understanding. A comparative analysis with British veterans’ own experiences of the Blitz further contextualizes their views, offering insight into their evolving perspectives. This study also traces a shift in perception—from witnessing the destruction of Hiroshima as an emblem of wartime devastation to recognizing it as a site of historical reflection and learning. Ultimately, it argues that those recollections, despite their fragmentary and sometimes contradictory nature, provide valuable insights into post-war reconciliation and the complexities of cross-cultural relationships.

  5. Writing BCOF as diary, re-writing the occupation as memoir
    Jonathan Bull · pp. 568-589 · online 2026-04-23
    摘要

    This article examines the diaries and memoir written by an Australian soldier who fought in the Second World War (1939-1945) and who served in the British Commonwealth Occupation Force (BCOF) in the Occupation of Japan. The diaries from 1938 to 1945 contextualise how the soldier wrote about BCOF as an important time in his military career. His BCOF diaries (1946-1951) indicate that he was torn between pursuing relationships with local women and trying to secure promotion. This tension lessened towards the end of his BCOF service but never entirely disappeared. In his memoir written in the mid-1960s he re-wrote his experience in BCOF so that the organisation had a greater moral coherence. These writings by the same individual therefore reveal the process by which a good occupation narrative was formed by rewriting, omission and substitution from the personal memory of the diary to the intended public memory of the memoir.

  6. Political allegories of WWII in Haruki Murakami’s works: Johnnie Walker, Colonel Sanders, and the commendatore
    Motohiro Kojima · pp. 590-607 · online 2025-11-19
    摘要

    This paper explores the political allegories embedded in Kafka on the Shore (2002) (Murakami Citation2005), arguing that the novel functions as a historical allegory of World War II, urging contemporary Japanese society to confront its wartime responsibilities. While Haruki Murakami’s works have often been perceived as politically disengaged, this study demonstrates how Kafka on the Shore contains hidden political metaphors referencing both Nazi Germany and the Japanese Empire. Employing close reading and historical contextual analysis, this paper examines key figures—Johnnie Walker, Colonel Sanders, and the imagery of the painting ‘Kafka on the Shore’—to reveal how Murakami engages with historical memory. The conflict between Johnnie Walker and Nakata is interpreted as an allegory of British-Japanese imperial rivalry, while Colonel Sanders symbolizes the Americanization of postwar Japan. Moreover, recurring German references, including a Volkswagen car and the ‘Edelweiss’ song from The Sound of Music (Citation1965), serve as reminders of Japan’s wartime alliance with Germany. The study further suggests that Murakami’s later novel, Killing Commendatore (2017) (Murakami Citation2018), adopts a more explicit approach to political allegory, shifting interpretive agency from the reader to the protagonist. By uncovering these layered political metaphors, this paper highlights Murakami’s evolving engagement with historical responsibility in his fiction.

  7. Disaster Journalism in Post-3.11 Japan – The Commitment of Yoshida Chia, Free Writer
    Veronica De Pieri · pp. 608-631 · online 2025-12-08
    摘要

    The free writer Yoshida Chia represents an example of the engagement of disaster journalism after the Fukushima Daiichi nuclear accident in 2011. Since the Tōhoku triple disaster, Yoshida has devoted her career to reporting information from the refugee shelters provided for people from the evacuated zones of the Fukushima Prefecture. At the core of her journalistic inquiries lie evacuation and post-evacuation issues. By directly interviewing the refugees – especially mothers and children – Yoshida has provided access to information concerning life at the refugee shelters, underlining the political and economic burdens as well as the psychological and social costs that the post-3.11 disaster (in)justice provoked on the affected communities. After introducing the state of art in disaster journalism in Japan with particular emphasis on the role of women journalists in the media, this study examines the new professional figure of the ‘free writer’ presenting the case of Yoshida Chia and her commitment to aiding public engagement in coping with disaster (in)justice during the process of Tōhoku’s recovery. In order to do so, the focus of this article will be on Yoshida’s Reportage of the evacuation of mothers and children. The nuclear victims will fade (2016) and After Fukushima. People who live after the nuclear accident (2018).

  8. Alicia Volk, In the Shadow of Empire: Art in Occupied Japan: University of Chicago Press, Chicago & London, 2025, 468 pp.
    Magdalena Kolodziej · pp. 632-633 · online 2026-08-11
    摘要

Online first（尚未編入期別）· 3
  1. The First World War and Japan’s cabinet system: how the conflict changed the perception and reception of foreign constitutional models by Japanese elites (1912–1920)
    Manon Ramos · pp. 1-25 · online 2026-10-23
    摘要

    The modern Japanese state, built on the Meiji Constitution (1889–1947), has long been understood as inspired by a Prussian model. Recent historiography, however, emphasises a paradigm shift in Japanese national identity after the First World War and the development of party politics in the 1920s. How did the experience of this conflict alter the vision of European constitutional frameworks and the choice of foreign models for interpreting Japan’s own political institutions? This article, while focusing on the cabinet system, addresses this question by analysing constitutional commentaries and reform projects by scholars, statesmen, and journalists across three chronological stages. First, it shows that doctrinal debates from 1912 to 1914 were polarised by ideologies favouring the German or British models. Second, it observes essential changes in information pipelines during the war, a detachment from Germany, and increasing praise for Britain’s resilience. Third, it argues that reform projects by influential actors in the war’s immediate aftermath were designed after the United Kingdom’s recent institutional evolutions. These considerations shed light on an early stage of the global dissemination of the Westminster model and suggest its influence went beyond the Dominions and the post-Second World War Commonwealth.

  2. Ambiguously queer: interrogating youth and queerness in Kyoto Animation’s adaptation of Sound! Euphonium
    Callum Hew Sarracino · pp. 1-23 · online 2026-10-11
    摘要

    Found across a wide variety of genres, youth is a popular setting for media in Japan. Often focused on school club activities, narratives set during adolescence feature intense relationships between members of the same sex that toe the line between homoromantic and homosocial. The language used in the texts and by the creators often supports both readings, with the audience left to draw their own conclusions based on how they understand youth. While previous scholarship has looked at queerness in youth-focused texts, less has been written about how the association a text has with youth affects queer readings of the language of the texts. Through a textual and discourse analysis of the anime Sound! Euphonium (2015–2024), as well as published interviews with creators and online fan discussion about it, this article interrogates the relationship between queerness and youth in the Japanese media. I argue that youth has a normative effect in the Japanese media sphere, obfuscating the existence of queer readings by providing a normative explanation for even the queerest of language. I further suggest that Sound! Euphonium more widely reflects the ambiguity that pervades representations of queerness during female adolescence in the Japanese media.

  3. Ministerial Power in Kantei-Led Policymaking: The Case of the Priority Reform Promotion System for National Universities under the Second Abe Administration
    Yutaro Tsuji · pp. 1-20 · online 2026-10-06
    摘要

    This article examines the policymaking process of the Priority Reform Promotion System, a performance-based funding system for Japanese national universities introduced under the second Abe administration. Existing research has largely portrayed this process through the lens of Kantei-led (prime minister’s office-led) policymaking, which sees the Ministry of Education, Culture, Sports, Science and Technology (MEXT) as following directives set by prime ministerial councils. However, little attention has been paid to whether and how MEXT’s own preferences were incorporated into the policy design. Using policy documents and elite interviews with MEXT civil servants, this article traces the policymaking process from agenda setting to the final budget negotiations, taking seriously the agency of MEXT. The findings show that MEXT held clear preferences from an early stage and shaped the design of the system within the new Kantei-led structure. These findings indicate that the Kantei-led framework does not on its own explain who determines specific policy content and suggest that ministerial agency deserves closer attention in analyses of Japan’s centralised policymaking structure.

Journal of Asian Studies

Vol. 85, No. 4 · 2026-10-01 · 7 篇 · Duke UP (AAS) · Asian studies (flagship) · Peer-reviewed
  1. Editorial Foreword
    Joseph S. Alter · pp. 773-776 · online 2026-10-01
  2. Introduction
    Raja Adal, Christopher Lowy · pp. 777-789 · online 2026-10-01
    摘要

    Before the Unicode Standard, text shared across digital devices was often garbled, a problem that disproportionately affected users of non-Latin scripts. Unicode's emergence in the 1990s resolved this by encoding the world's major scripts into a single global standard that made text interoperable across digital devices—a development especially consequential in Asia, home to more than 90 percent of non-Latin script users. Yet Unicode is not a neutral infrastructure. Each encoding decision—which characters to include, how to handle script boundaries, whether to unify Sinographs across linguistic traditions—was shaped by the competing interests of large technology companies, national governments, user communities, and scholars. And each carried enduring political implications. This introduction argues that it is time for humanities scholars to critically engage with the ways in which Unicode crystallized at the intersection of state power, capital, and culture to become the foundation of a global infrastructure that governs nearly all textual communication today. It situates the four articles in this volume within the existing scholarly literature, provides a glossary of key technical terms, and introduces the closing roundtable in which technical experts and humanities scholars reflect on the field's future directions.

  3. Big Tech, the Big State, and the Battle for Encoding All of the World's Languages
    Raja Adal · pp. 790-812 · online 2026-10-01
    摘要

    For over three decades, the dominant narrative of Unicode has been that it is a technical solution to a technical problem of global communication. This article argues that this narrative has dominated the discourse about Unicode because it is the narrative of the victors. Understanding the social and political interests at stake requires returning to the battle that yielded the Unicode Standard as the sole universal encoding standard: a period from 1988, when a draft proposal of the Unicode Standard was first made public, to 1993, when the Unicode Standard effectively conquered the dominant encoding standard, ISO/IEC 10646. Whereas Unicode was an organization consisting mostly of engineers at large American software companies, ISO was an international standard organization comprising representatives from member nations. Their confrontation over the encoding of Sinographs pitted big tech against the big state, with significant national and financial interests at stake. The last section discusses how a recent attempt by the Chinese state to rapidly encode a large batch of Sinographs used in proper names reveals some of the stakes of this ongoing negotiation between big tech and big states.

  4. ISCII Imperialism
    Anushah Hossain · pp. 813-838 · online 2026-10-01
    摘要

    In 2009, Daniel Pargman and Jacob Palme published an essay titled “ASCII Imperialism,” which discussed how the American text standard, ASCII, was embedded in existing computer protocols and how it marginalized non-English languages. This article hearkens to that concept by calling for the recognition of “ISCII imperialism.” It examines how the little-known Indian Script Code for Information Interchange (ISCII) has been integrated in global communication protocols since 1991 through the Unicode Standard and has created the potential for an uneven digital landscape for different Indian languages and writing systems. Specifically, ISCII has been controversial for seemingly privileging Devanagari, commonly used to write Hindi, at the expense of other scripts. Public outcry against ISCII imperialism has been transformative, however. As this article demonstrates, advocacy by Indic script communities has pushed the Unicode Consortium to reconsider its implementation of ISCII, demonstrating how even rigid technical structures are influenced by regional language politics.

  5. Abstract Code
    Uluğ Kuzuoğlu · pp. 839-861 · online 2026-10-01
    摘要

    Unicode is everywhere and nowhere at once. Since its invention in 1993 by lucrative American companies, including Xerox, Apple, and Microsoft, Unicode has been the global standard for information exchange, enabling the transfer of data across the world by converting more than a hundred and sixty world scripts into standardized bits and bytes. Its spread throughout the world is so expansive, its technical complexity so elaborate, and its integration into daily lives so seamless that Unicode has become a natural part of the world—used everywhere, yet seen nowhere. This article seeks to denaturalize Unicode by examining its origins from a global perspective and situating it within a longer history of script engineering that was deeply intertwined with imperialism, colonialism, and capitalism. It proposes the term “abstract code” to capture a historical process whereby capitalism homogenizes and standardizes diverse cultures of writing to make them exchangeable and commensurable with one another in the market of information. Disclosing the historical links between the nineteenth-century age of script engineering and the birth of Unicode in the 1980s, this article demonstrates that the new information infrastructure sought to abstract all writing systems into a standardized sequence of bits and thus enable the corporate accumulation of capital at a global scale. Unicode, in short, was the critical infrastructure that enabled the birth of a global neoliberal information economy.

  6. Literary Expression at the Ends of Unicode
    Christopher Lowy · pp. 862-887 · online 2026-10-01
    摘要

    This article examines how Unicode reshapes the humanities by asking what happens when a supposedly universal encoding system meets the demands of the Japanese writing system. Focusing on EnJoe Toh's 2018 collection A Whirlpool of Glyphs, it shows how his fiction probes the tension between standardization and expression that underlies script reform from the Qin dynasty to contemporary Unicode debates. EnJoe's stories stage conflicts between inclusion and exclusion—what counts as an encodable character, which graphic forms are discarded, and how these decisions constrain or enable writers working with Sinographs, kana, and their visual and expressive possibilities. By dramatizing everything from Qin-era script reforms to rebellious furigana and proliferating variant characters, EnJoe makes visible the technical infrastructures that underwrite the process of writing. In doing so, the article argues, he exploits the Japanese writing system's capacity for orthographical variation at the limits of possibility. Unicode functions simultaneously as a tool of global standardization and a platform that “standardizes the nonstandard,” with significant consequences for how the humanities preserve, classify, and imagine written forms, and EnJoe's work offers a key case for understanding how script technologies condition literary creativity across languages and periods.

    Raja Adal, Andrew Glass, Zev Handel, Anushah Hossain, Uluğ Kuzuoğlu, Christopher Lowy et al. · pp. 888-907 · online 2026-10-01

  12. Multiple Diplomatic Centers
    Lina Nie
    摘要

    Diplomatic interactions between Japanese Shogun Ashikaga Yoshimitsu and Ming emperor Zhu Di, together with the Ming-Japanese tally trade, have received lively scholarly discussion. This article examines a period before the tally trade momentarily became the major diplomatic channel in Sino-Japanese exchanges. By cross-referencing and comparing sources from China and Japan, the author highlights how various players such as Zhu Yuanzhang, Buddhist monks, Prince Kaneyoshi, Go-Enyū tennō, and Kyushu warrior leaders participated in overseas exchanges. The author argues that the lively diplomatic exchanges between Kyushu and China confirm the existence of multiple diplomatic centers beyond Kyoto. The author proposes seeing Ming's rulership in this light and rethinking it in a broader East Asian context. In particular, Zhu Yuanzhang's flexible policies and careful examination of overseas envoys demonstrate that early Ming's overseas policies cannot be summarized simply as conservative and inward-looking. Instead, they show a high degree of openness and variation in the conduct of overseas affairs. By comparing diplomatic exchanges written in different languages and from different sides, the author argues for the need to move beyond political and territorial boundaries to consider important concepts such as rulership, empire, and imperial order in a connected and transregional perspective.

  13. From Click to Boom: The Political Economy of E-Commerce in China

  14. Correlating Race and Caste in Colonial Burma
    Erin L. McAuliffe
    摘要

    Scholars have made clear the impact of colonial governance on modern states’ racial, ethnic, and national identities. Colonial census efforts were a crucial tool in the creation and politicization of such identity categories. This article analyzes “race” definitions and classifications in the colonial censuses of British India from 1872 to 1931 to show how the challenges of defining race in Burma emerged, in part, because of the stepwise colonization of Burma through British India. Specifically, this article asks: What influenced the definitions of race in Burma? How were efforts at developing and understanding a racial schema in Burma shaped by the colonial landscape in which it was embedded? The article argues that colonial ideas about race in Burma developed out of an initial stage of racial classification and knowledge production in India, revealing the complexity of defining race under the influence of both European perceptions of racial difference and local perceptions of social boundaries in India. This article contributes to knowledge of how the creation and implementation of race categories and definitions is a process embedded in a web of colonial structures that extend beyond the colony-colonizer relationship.

  15. Push-Car Railways, Camphor, and Coal in the Imperial Hinterland
    Youjia Li
    摘要

    This article examines the push-car railway industry in colonial Taiwan from 1895 to the 1930s. It argues that this human-powered transportation resolved a colonial dilemma between the colonial government's economic ambition and its limited capacities as shaped by Taiwan's environmental and colonial conditions. The push-car railways effectively connected Taiwan's local economy to the global market. The article rethinks the relationship between the environment, capitalism, and colonialism from three perspectives. First, muscle-powered technology was crucial to the rise of fossil capitalism at the turn of the century. Second, the push-car railway industry revealed a multilayered relationship between the colonizer and the colonized, profoundly reshaping the environment of the imperial hinterland. Third, as an integral part of fossil capitalism, the push-car railway industry amplified and complicated human cost instead of decreasing it. This analysis unravels how local Taiwanese business actors developed networks under colonial constraints, reshaping socioeconomic and environmental relationships in Taiwan's hinterlands.

  18. Meritocracy Divide
    Takehiko Kariya
    摘要

    As a postscript to the collected articles in this forum, this article first discusses how and why a high-stakes test-based admissions system has historically been established in East Asia, contextualizing meritocratic educational selection within the historical experience of “catch-up modernization.” Second, using a theoretical framework of “compressed” modernity and development, it discusses how broader and more recent societal changes have contributed to the development of a high-stakes admissions system. Third, it discusses the implications of these recent changes in admissions for understanding changes in meritocracy. Finally, it describes the problems these rapid and simultaneous changes in admissions and other social spheres have created and what implications these changes have for our understanding of meritocracy in the region. It concludes by discussing how meritocracy has created a new social divide, representing a duality in meritocratic college admissions consisting of traditional high-stakes tests and newly introduced admissions procedures that are more holistic.

  20. College Admission in South Korea
    Jeehwan Park, Yuki Asahina
    摘要

    South Korea is known for its intense focus on education, and college admission in the country has been central to citizens’ sense of fairness. Citizens strongly believe in procedural justice, which is guaranteed by transparent and objective selection schemes based on the one-shot exam called Suneung. However, as a result of a few decades of college admission reforms, such an exam is no longer the primary route to university today. Against this background, this study explores how Korean college students understand fairness as they navigate “meritocratic” selection processes for prestigious universities.
