  26. Why Bear the Burden of Student Loans?
    Hyewon Park
    摘要

    This essay investigates what it means for young people in East Asia to go to college by looking at the growing number of young adults who attend higher education using National Student Loans. In-depth interviews with nineteen low-income young adults in South Korea and Japan revealed that the decision to attend college has become more common in both societies, with positive evaluations of National Student Loans and student debts. They strategically positioned their student loans by distinguishing them from other loans. In addition, the author found that higher education has more complex meanings in East Asia than simply a means of economic benefit. For example, in South Korea, higher education was equated with gaining prestige goods, while in Japan, going to college was considered the ideal way for young people to spend their twenties.

  28. Out with the Old, In with the New
    Ruo-Fan Liu
    摘要

    Research on meritocracy often assumes equal weighting of different types of merit, overlooking how people assign relative power to them in justifying social superiority. This study, based on sixty-two interviews, examines how educators and students navigate Taiwan's dual-merit admissions system, where traditional test scores intersect with a new holistic screening model. The author finds that students continue to treat test scores as primary markers of success, while professors and teachers increasingly prioritize nontest merit as signals of social distinction. This divergence highlights varying rates of adaptation to Taiwan's dual-merit system, exposing tensions between old and new standards. These findings shed light on how merit is perceived and operationalized amid institutional transformation, and they contribute to broader debates on fairness and legitimacy in East Asian educational systems.

Journal of Korean Studies

Vol. 31, No. 2 · 2026-10-01 · 16 篇 · Duke UP · Korea · Peer-reviewed
  1. Editorial Note
    Dafna Zur, Paul Chang, Yumi Moon · pp. 281-282 · online 2026-10-01
  2. Introduction to the Special Issue: Crossing Boundaries in Modern Korean Literature and Culture
    Susan Hwang, Haerin Shin · pp. 283-287 · online 2026-10-01
  3. Negotiating the Colonial Dilemma Through Science Fiction: Translating Jules Verne in Early Twentieth-Century Colonial Korea
    Sang-Keun Yoo · pp. 289-321 · online 2026-10-01
    摘要

    This article examines the emergence of science fiction (SF) in early twentieth-century Korea by analyzing the first two Korean translations of Jules Verne—Twenty Thousand Leagues Under the Sea (1907–8) and The Begum’s Fortune (1908). It argues that these relay translations, produced during the years between the Russo-Japanese War and formal Japanese annexation, reveal how Korean intellectuals navigated what the article terms the “colonial dilemma”: the challenge of rethinking national identity amid accelerated modernization and encroaching imperial domination. Rather than passively importing European SF, Korean translators actively reshaped the texts through genre relabeling, annotation, and selective adaptation, using them simultaneously as vehicles of scientific enlightenment and as tools for articulating Korea’s precarious place within global geopolitics. The article further contends that the early Korean use of the term “science fiction”—predating its standardization in the Anglophone world—calls for a reconsideration of global SF timelines and assumptions about the genre’s origins. By situating Korean SF within the broader East Asian circulation of Verne’s works and by highlighting the translators’ interventions into colonialist narratives, this study demonstrates that SF in Korea emerged not as an extension of European imperial imagination but through a complex interplay of colonial, anti-colonial, and nation-building impulses.

  4. Translated Poetry in Younghill Kang’s The Grass Roof and the Tradition of Inset Poetry in Premodern Korean Fiction
    Spencer Lee-Lenfield · pp. 323-343 · online 2026-10-01
    摘要

    Younghill Kang’s 1931 novel The Grass Roof, long taken as the first commercially successful novel by a Korean in English, is unusual for incorporating sixty-five of its author’s translations of East Asian poetry into its narrative fabric. These embedded translations represented one of the early mass audiences for Korean poetry in English. This article argues that The Grass Roof adapts the literary convention of inset poetry (sabipsi) from the premodern Korean novel, hybridizing it with English-language autobiographical fiction. It proves Kang knew traditional narrative models by analyzing his depictions in the novel of the Kuunmong and Ch’unhyang chŏn. It then examines two different uses of inset poetry in the novel: (1) retrospective ornamentation by the narrator and (2) dynamic plot advancement by characters. The second function, in particular, reflects classical uses of sabipsi to heighten scenes of courtship and erudition. Kang’s use of inset poetry gave Korean poetry an unusually early global audience at the same time as it drew Western and Korean novelistic techniques together in a new, shared experiment.

  5. Reading O Changhwan Translating Sergey Esenin in Seoul, 1946
    Janet Poole · pp. 345-365 · online 2026-10-01
    摘要

    This article explores the allegorical layers of meaning that emerge from one translator-poet partnership in the aftermath of the collapse of the Japanese empire in Korea. The iconoclastic modernist poet O Changhwan’s first published book in a newly liberated Korea was a translation of poems by the deceased Russian poet Sergey Esenin. O was translating at a moment when the Korean language was returning to public life after suppression under colonial rule and amid escalating violence on the partitioned peninsula. Reading O’s translations and lengthy afterword opens a window onto the scene of Seoul in 1946, one year before O was to cross over the thirty-eighth parallel into the northern zone. With the aim of rethinking the creative legacies of the early Cold War, this article explores the many meanings created in and through O’s translation.

  6. Milan Kundera’s Poetry in North Korea and Socialist World Literature in the 1950s
    Kwon Boduerae · pp. 367-392 · online 2026-10-01
    摘要

    This essay examines Milan Kundera’s unexpected presence in 1950s North Korean literature, revealing a forgotten chapter of socialist world literature in the Cold War era. The study focuses on three of Kundera’s poems related to the Korean War: “The Gardener,” “Korean Ballad,” and his epic poem “The Last May,” which revolves around the anti-fascist martyr Julius Fučík. By analyzing North Korean literary journals including Chosŏn munhak, the essay traces how Kundera’s early socialist poetry arrived in North Korea as part of broader international solidarity efforts during and after the Korean War. Noting how the translation of these works occurred at a specific historical moment when North Korea was actively engaging with Eastern European literature before turning inward in the 1960s, the essay situates Kundera within North Korea’s evolving conception of “world literature,” which shifted from Soviet-centeredness through Eastern European engagement to eventual interest in the Tricontinental (Asia, Africa, Latin America) before closing off entirely. The divided reception exemplifies the broader fragmentation of world literature during the Cold War, gesturing to the power dynamic that shapes literary histories in conversation with but also beyond ideological boundaries.

  7. Interpretive Praxis Under Pax Americana: Paik Nak-chung’s Reading of D.H. Lawrence in 1980s South Korea
    Susan Hwang · pp. 393-416 · online 2026-10-01
    摘要

    This article examines Paik Nak-chung’s (Paek Nakch’ŏng’s) 1982 translation and reading of D.H. Lawrence’s Studies in Classic American Literature (1923) as a case of interpretive praxis under Cold War authoritarianism in South Korea. Situated between the aftershocks of the Kwangju Uprising and the intensification of anti-American student activism, Paik’s essay “The American Dream and the Burden of American Literature” appears, at first glance, anomalous: Why would a leading critic of literary engagement turn to Lawrence at a moment when dissident reading communities centered on Marx, Fanon, and other banned revolutionary texts? I argue that Paik’s sustained engagement with Lawrence is best understood as a metacritical strategy that converts reading and translation into a political intervention. Working through Lawrence’s symptomatic critique of “American democracy” and the ideological work of the American dream, Paik exposes US imperial duplicity while simultaneously resisting the nativist essentialism that could accompany virulent anti-Americanism in 1980s Korea. Lawrence’s insistence on stripping “democratic and idealistic clothes” from American utterance becomes, in Paik’s hands, a method for historicizing—one that links the contradictions of American nation-building to South Korea’s compressed modernization and US-backed authoritarianism. Tracing how Paik reframes Lawrence’s critique of the “whole soul” of America, I show how he articulates a decolonizing stance that resists simple binaries of foreign imperialist versus authentic minjung. Finally, situating Paik’s Lawrence within a longer genealogy of criticism shaped by Matthew Arnold and F. R. Leavis, I argue that Paik’s “double project of modernity” (ijung kwajeron) marks a commitment to hold aesthetic autonomy and political praxis in productive tension with each other.

  8. Archaeological Translation: How to Translate Diasporic Korean Literature
    Ki-In Chong, Joo Young Lee · pp. 417-436 · online 2026-10-01
    摘要

    Min Jin Lee’s Pachinko chronicles four generations of Zainichi (Koreans in Japan) from the colonial era to the 1980s. Its translation process remains enigmatic; Lee imagined the narrative in Korean and Japanese before writing the novel in English. Consequently, the English original and its two Korean translated versions produce divergent meanings. This article investigates the translational issues in the two Korean versions, questioning the hierarchical order between the original Anglophone text and its translations. Building upon postcolonial translation scholarship, we first examine restored dialects in the Korean versions that are absent in the English text, analyzing their intersection with discourses of class, gender, and ethnicity. Second, we compare the opening sentences of the three versions, unearthing distinct insights into colonial and postcolonial histories embedded within each text. By unsettling conventional notions of originality, this comparative analysis introduces a new framework termed “archaeological translation.” Through this lens, we critique Lee’s mode of representation that flattens cultural and historical specificity, while demonstrating how translated versions evolve into distinctive texts with their own historical, political, and cultural perspectives in the transnational age.

  9. The Magical Agency of Parameters as Intent and Meaning: The Translation Application in Decision to Leave
    Haerin Shin · pp. 437-457 · online 2026-10-01
    摘要

    This essay examines how the translation application in Park Chan-wook’s Heŏjil kyŏlsim (Decision to Leave, 2022) functions as a narrative agent and framework for parametric mediation. Semantic entropy designates information loss when neural translation compresses multivalent meanings into probable outputs; yet this entropy proves generatively productive, creating new possibilities in gaps between languages. Close readings examine scenes where the app mistranslates Chinese into Korean, transforming affective complexity into anatomical literalism, imposing unwarranted honorifics, and reducing Confucian ethics to suit consumer preferences. The screenwriters deliberately crafted these failures; paradoxically, such errors generate meanings exceeding both languages. Drawing on Walter Benjamin, Susan Bassnett, and neural translation research, this study shows how mistranslations illuminate both limitations and generative capacities in algorithmic and human understanding. These insufficiencies become narratively productive through a hermeneutics of irresolution: an interpretive mode refusing closure, locating meaning in interstices between competing translations. Characterizing this dynamic as parametric creativity, I argue that Decision to Leave transforms technical constraints into aesthetic method. As artificial intelligence increasingly mediates communication, the film reminds us of interstices arising from encounters between disparate entities: the tremor in voices, the weight of pauses, and the irreducible difference between heart as an organ and heart as feeling.

  10. Between Eco-Governmentality and Forest Management Cooperatives: Reinterpreting South Korean Reforestation in the 1970s and 1980s from the Local Level
    Max Altenhofen · pp. 459-486 · online 2026-10-01
    摘要

    In 1973, the Park Chung Hee government launched its first Reforestation Plan to deal with the pressing issues surrounding forest loss, soil erosion, and forest management through countrywide tree planting and stricter control of forest use for logging. The top-down campaigns, often referred to as a “green Yushin,” can be understood as eco-governmentality with direct state intervention in the sylvan environment. However, to recognize the effects of reforestation on the local level, there is a need for further consideration of the role of forest owners. The West German and South Korean governments promoted the establishment of Forest Management Cooperatives (FMCs). After their introduction in Ulju County, the model was subsequently transferred to other regions all over the country throughout the 1980s. The cooperatives contributed to the economic livelihood of the rural population and enabled the transfer of forest management skills and practices to forest owners amid the government-led campaign. The forest owners could then actively participate in decision-making processes on the local level. This article proposes a new understanding of the reforestation campaigns that took place under the authoritarian government by engaging perspectives from the local level and introducing the new models of cooperatives that were carried out as rural development projects.

  11. Cleared for Takeoff: The Economic and Environmental History of Incheon International Airport in 1990s South Korea
    Howard Kahm · pp. 487-512 · online 2026-10-01
    摘要

    In 1990, the South Korean government announced the construction of a new international airport on reclaimed land to the west of Seoul. This proposal drew the ire of environmental groups and major newspapers as an environmental and economic disaster. The international airport construction proposal arrived at an inflection point of post-1987 democratization, government liberalization of travel restrictions, the growth of the aviation industry, rising environmentalism, and the beginning of a global recession. While large-scale infrastructure projects had enabled South Korea’s rapid economic rise, the high environmental costs sparked intense public interest among South Koreans. However, the openness of the post-1987 period was a double-edged sword for environmental groups. While they found greater freedom to organize and high public interest in environmental issues, the public also had the freedom to decide the type of environmentalism and lifestyle they wanted to pursue. This article examines the Incheon International Airport construction proposal as a case study of the intersection between economic history and environmental history, with popular sentiment as a key swing variable. This study argues that the Incheon airport proposal illustrates the contemporary nature of South Korean environmentalism, lifestyle objectives, and economic consciousness.

Journal of American Ethnic History

Vol. 46, No. 1 · 2026-09-01 · 18 篇 · U Illinois Press · Immigration & ethnic history · Peer-reviewed
  1. Forging a Double Diaspora: Cuban Jewish Migration and Settlement in 1960s Greater Miami
    Mark A. Goldberg · pp. 5-37 · online 2026-09-01
    摘要

    After the 1959 revolution, Jewish migrants joined the waves of emigrants exiting Fidel Castro's Cuba. They developed a close-knit community in Greater Miami as their parents and grandparents had done when they immigrated to Cuba decades earlier. Analyzing oral histories, newspapers, and Cuban and US community records, this article uncovers an often-untold history and reconceptualizes diaspora by exploring the local influences on Cuban Jewish diaspora formation for a population belonging to multiple racialized groups. In the decades after arrival in Greater Miami, Cuban Jews transitioned from Cuban nationals to Cuban exiles to US residents and citizens. They found affordable housing in the white-designated Miami Beach and carved out an enclave, connecting Cuban Jewish newcomers and grounding them in the city. To integrate, the migrants felt they had to prove their worth to the larger society, and they embraced respectability politics to defuse their perceived otherness—which contained a complex combination of racial, ethnic, and religious elements—while maintaining their Cuban identity. Cuban Jews forged a double diaspora rooted in an entangled Cuban Jewish identity born from Jewish life in Cuba, cultural adaptation to the US socio-political environment, and interactions with other Cuban exiles and Miami residents.

  2. Little Guyana in Motion: Belonging, Religion, and Moral Community in Post–1965 Immigrant New York
    Kavita Gawrinauth · pp. 38-60 · online 2026-09-01
    摘要

    This article argues that post-1965 immigrant New York is often analyzed through two largely parallel literatures—migration and community formation, on the one hand, and religion and transnational devotion, on the other—and that reading them together clarifies how belonging in the outer boroughs was made through overlapping material and moral infrastructures. Bringing the urban scholarship of Nancy Foner, Steven Gregory, Madhulika Khandelwal, and Pyong Gap Min into conversation with work on immigrant religion by R. Stephen Warner, Peggy Levitt, Prema Kurien, and R. Scott Hanson, it demonstrates that labor markets, housing corridors, and neighborhood politics frequently intertwined with ritual calendars, congregational institution-building, and everyday ethics of obligation and care. This article, then, uses the relative absence of the Indo-Guyanese community of Richmond Hill, Queens (“Little Guyana”) from both literatures to probe the limits of frameworks that privilege either “settlement” or “transnationalism.” Drawing on Queens-based community and ethnic press coverage of Phagwah/Holi and Diwali, municipal recognitions such as permits, proclamations, and street co-namings, documentation of mandir-building and temple openings, and oral histories with mandir participants and organizers, it traces devotional circulation between Queens and Guyana as a form of neighborhood-making. This Richmond Hill case shows how ritual processions, institutional expansion, and public commemoration make belonging legible through urban space while sustaining obligations and attachments across borders—treating the neighborhood less as an end point of migration than as a node in a wider devotional circuit.

  3. Collective Care Is Essential: Puerto Rican Feminist Activists and Welfare Rights Organizing after 1960
    Emma Amador · pp. 61-88 · online 2026-09-01
    摘要

    In 1970, a Puerto Rican woman named Juanita Serrano was elected to serve as a chair of the National Welfare Rights Organization (NWRO) after spending numerous years organizing poor women in Cleveland, Ohio. In this role Serrano joined welfare rights organizers from across the country as they protested the social, economic, and political conditions faced by poor people. This article provides a history of Puerto Rican women's contributions to welfare rights organizing in the United States. It argues that Puerto Rican women activists within the welfare rights movement articulated a vision of collective care that insisted that working-class people should have control over social welfare institutions and that welfare recipients should dictate the forms that care took in their communities. Their vision also addressed Puerto Ricans’ experience as colonial citizens, migrants, Spanish speakers, and people of color. The article examines how demands for collective care were advanced both in organizing to improve social welfare programs in the United States and Puerto Rico, and in leftist political mobilizations that contested capitalism and colonialism. Thus, one of the particularities of Puerto Rican welfare rights activism was that it was often closely linked to broader struggles for colonial migrants’ rights and for the decolonization of Puerto Rico.

  4. Immigration, Authoritarian Populism, and Donald Trump
    John Bodnar · pp. 89-118 · online 2026-09-01
    摘要

    Donald Trump fueled a political rise by exploiting the issue of immigration. Rejecting the liberal narrative of a “nation of immigrants” that had long portrayed the United States as a land of opportunity for all and a haven for the oppressed, Trump turned the idea of immigration into an existential threat to the nation itself. He dehumanized people crossing American borders with wild abandon, calling them criminals, drug dealers, “dog-eaters,” invaders, rapists, pollutants of the nation's blood supply, and monsters capable of entering the kitchens of citizens to “slit their throat.” In his first run for the presidency in 2016, he promised to build a massive wall on the southern border. In his second inaugural address, in 2025, he declared a national emergency at the border and said he would send military forces to “repel a disastrous invasion.” Why did Trump do this? Why did he turn immigrants—both undocumented and legal—into extreme threats to the nation? Was he simply a radical xenophobe or racist seeking to keep America mostly white or Anglo-Saxon? And where did this extreme form of hostility come from? This essay seeks to probe these issues and offers an interpretation of matters that will be debated for years to come. It argues that in his rhetoric and deeds, Trump's immigration policies need to be understood as more than a resort to traditional forms of American xenophobia and racism. His narrative of immigration as a peril was a means to a larger end that sought to overturn liberal democracy in the United States with a form of authoritarianism.

  6. The Geology of Grids: Decolonial Digitality in Southeast Asia
    Ding-Liang Chen · pp. 120-147
    摘要

    What if the history of digitality began in landscapes instead of laboratories? This essay proposes the geology of grids as a method for tracing the entanglement of empire and ecology in Southeast Asia as the infrastructure of global connectivity. Centering on the region's first fiber-optic cable, I examine how Rithy Panh's documentary The Land of the Wandering Souls evokes elemental thinking of media infrastructure beyond Western epistemologies. Exhuming buried remains of war and genocide, the grounding of cable grids not only epitomizes racial capitalism of digital modernity but evinces the earth as a contested archive of ghostly matters.   7. Endgame Taiwan: KMT's Civil Defense Media and the Emergence of a Cybernetic Aesthetic
    Lawrence Zi-Qiao Yang · pp. 148-178
    摘要

    This article examines the civil defense media apparatus of the Kuomintang (KMT) regime in Taiwan between the 1950s and 1970s, arguing that these media gave rise to a distinctive organizational and aesthetic form I call a cybernetic aesthetic. I trace three registers of this formation: the reactivation of wartime memory in China as mnemonic data; the spread of American operation research and scientific management as an aesthetics of informatization; and the production of a specific mode of enclosure and captivation that trained technocratic cadres and civilian populations to perceive themselves as nodes within a distributed system of signal, noise, and feedback. I situate this formation within Cold War cybernetic thought to show how military-defense aesthetics enclosed social and organizational life in Cold War Taiwan.

  8. Unsettling the Settlement of Land: The Use of Compensation in Base Perimeter Space
    Tony Hyunduck Cho · pp. 179-203
    摘要

    By approaching compensation not only as a mode of redress but as a computational practice, this article highlights how land disputes between South Korean residents and US military bases are settled through the South Korean state's calculation of land and environment. Drawing from archival research and ethnographic fieldwork in Pyeongtaek, this essay traces the emergence of compensation protocols under the Special Act of Support for Pyeongtaek. The article examines how the bill was used to establish the US military base expansion in the city in exchange for redeveloping Pyeongtaek into an industrial city.   9. Searching for My Father's House: Gaze-Scaling the Philippines and Mars Across the (Digital) Diaspora
    Maya Cruz · pp. 204-222
    摘要

  10. Platform Islandization: The Making of the Computational Archipelago
    Merlyna Lim · pp. 223-247
    摘要

    This essay introduces platform islandization to theorize digital life beyond questions of infrastructure or access, through situated rhythms and ruptures that challenge the universalist claims of platform logics. Platform islandization is a socio-technical process in which bounded and selectively connected publics emerge within platformed environments. This process produces computational archipelagos : fragmented configurations of visibility, access, and affect structured by platform logics and infrastructures. I articulate platform islandization across three registers— infrastructural-experiential, epistemic-ethical, and aesthetic-algorithmic —revealing fragmentation as a patterned condition rather than a deficit. Grounded in long-term research in Indonesia, this framework invokes "Indonesia as method" and offers a portable analytic for understanding how people and digital systems mutually reorder publics, emotions, and knowledges across diverse contexts.

  5. Everyday discrimination and mental health among Korean American U.S. citizens and permanent residents: Differential moderating roles of parental ethnic–racial socialization practices.
    Angela Chow, Shuhan Yuan, Hsien-Chang Lin, Kit K. Elam, Ingrid Schoon · pp. 258-269 · online 2026-09
    摘要

    The study aimed to (a) compare Korean American U.S. citizens and Korean American permanent residents studying in U.S. colleges in their experiences of everyday discrimination, parental ethnic–racial socialization (PERS), depressive symptoms, and anxiety symptoms and (b) investigate whether different PERS practices (namely, cultural socialization–pluralism, preparation for bias, and promotion of mistrust) moderate the effects of everyday discrimination on depressive and anxiety symptoms. Survey data were collected from 415 full-time undergraduate students in the United States who self-identified as Korean American (citizens n = 223; permanent residents n = 192). The results revealed that compared to Korean American U.S. citizens, Korean American permanent residents reported more everyday discrimination, PERS practices, depressive, and anxiety symptoms. Everyday discrimination was associated with more depressive and anxiety symptoms for both groups. For Korean American citizens, the associations with both depressive and anxiety symptoms were buffered by cultural socialization-pluralism. On the other hand, for Korean American permanent residents, the association between discrimination and depressive symptoms was buffered by preparation for bias. Despite differential rates of everyday discrimination across the two groups, these experiences are similarly detrimental to their mental health. Also, the findings highlight distinct protective mechanisms conferred by specific PERS practices that can help shield these groups from the adverse impacts of discrimination.   4. Transnational activism and civic engagement among Burmese American refugee youth.
    Xiang Zhou, Phoebe Pham, Mengxi Zhang, Jenni Thang · online 2026-10-01
    摘要

    Research on refugee youth has predominantly centered on challenges and deficits, often overlooking their resilience, contributions, and the positive roles they play in their new communities, including activism and civic engagement (ACE). This study addresses that gap by focusing on Burmese refugee youth, one of the largest resettled groups in the U.S. in recent history. Specifically, we examined four types of activities—protesting, donating, distributing information, and signing petitions—across three domains: ethnic/local (Burmese American causes), transnational (Myanmar causes), and general (other causes). The sample comprised 99 Burmese American adolescents from refugee backgrounds. Over half engaged in at least one ACE activity in the last six months. Transnational engagement was most common, followed by ethnic and general ACE. An explorative person-centered analysis revealed three profiles: moderately engaged (60%), transnationally engaged (11%), and highly engaged (20.7%). Results were discussed in the context of the global refugee crisis and the 2021 February 1 Myanmar coup d'état. These findings have important implications to expand the current conceptual model of activism and civic engagement among refugee and migrant youth.

MELUS

Vol. 51, No. 1 · 2026-08-13 · 15 篇 · Oxford UP · Multi-ethnic US literature · Peer-reviewed
  1. The “Great Disappearing Act of Success”: Technologies of Speculation in Contemporary Black Financial Fictions
    Deborah Thurman · pp. 1-25 · online 2026-06-22
    摘要

    This article examines how two contemporary works of Black speculative fiction imagine a financialized future, playing with racialized logics of prediction that position whiteness as the watchword for the ceaseless accumulation of value over time. Both Boots Riley’s Sorry to Bother You (2018) and Mateo Askaripour’s Black Buck (2021) depict salesmen on trajectories of individual upward mobility, prospering despite the racial and financial inequalities of their near-future dystopian worlds. Like the tech companies where they work, these characters generate profitable projections, fabricating visions of the future that solicit audience investment. For both protagonists, success involves the strategic performance of whiteness as a tool of financial causality that promises future happiness, security, and unfettered growth. Yet, even as they stage the spectacle of success, Sorry to Bother You and Black Buck formally sabotage the plausibility of their own growth narratives, using flimsy props, temporal acceleration, exaggerated plot twists, and situational irony to render their own storytelling fundamentally absurd. The result, I argue, is speculative fiction that undermines the act of speculation, in both narrative and financial forms. Moving beyond exposure, these texts explore how finance capitalism disciplines desire to foster affective attachments to incredible projections. They invite audiences to disinvest from prediction as the means to secure a happy future, turning instead toward strategic destabilization and radical narrative uncertainty.

  2. Postsentimental Horizons in Sui Sin Far’s “In the Land of the Free”
    Xavier Xin · pp. 26-49 · online 2026-06-23
    摘要

    This article explores the racial politics of sympathy in Sui Sin Far’s short story “In the Land of the Free” (1909). Widely celebrated as the “mother” of Asian American literature, Sui Sin Far dedicated much of her writing career to the advocacy of Chinese immigrant rights. Contrary to the common understanding that presumes the early Asian American writer’s appeal to white sympathy for Chinese immigrants as fundamental to her short stories, I reconsider Sui Sin Far as a trenchant critic of the dominant white sentimental politics of recognition who nonetheless experimented with sentimental tropes to provoke a more liberatory model of social justice that I call “postsentimental allyship.” Building on Lauren Berlant’s formulation of postsentimentalism and Xine Yao’s recent work on unfeeling, I honor this horizon of sociality and its affective reorganization of white-Chinese relations amid the stifling pressure to yield to the terms of white sympathetic recognition during the Chinese Exclusion Era. In analyzing the short story, I locate Sui Sin Far’s dissent from the sentimental politics of recognition through reading practices that attend to Sui Sin Far’s often-overlooked narrative experiments and the Chinese immigrant characters in the story, whose words and gestures express their demand for and enact the practice of alternative politics of advocacy. As sympathy has been normalized into the default affective mode in animating white activism, reading Sui Sin Far’s short story in this way helps us see that there are other possible ways to feel solidarity that do not reinscribe oppression but rather can begin rearranging race relations toward more just dynamics.

  3. Beyond “Saving” Life: Biopolitical Dynamics and Virus Narrative in Saving the World
    Tianran Chen · pp. 50-72 · online 2026-05-27
    摘要

    Julia Alvarez’s Saving the World (2006) employs virus narratives to weave two storylines into a shared account of the entanglement of life, medical intervention, and biopolitics. Reading the novel through the biopolitical transformations of the early nineteenth century, this article examines the 1803 Royal Spanish Smallpox Expedition and its reliance on live carriers to show how an ostensibly humanitarian project was structured by colonial power. It then situates the novel within the context of twenty-first-century globalization to trace the neo-imperial logic of US intervention in the Global South during the AIDS pandemic, particularly at the nexus of biomedical aid, neoliberal governance, and counterterrorism. The article further argues that Alvarez imagines forms of affiliation that exceed colonial and imperial containment through a negative affirmation that moves from immunized individuals to communal life. Saving the World thus reworks the immunitary paradigm by envisioning communities forged across racial, national, and territorial boundaries. In bringing a colonial history of smallpox into dialogue with a fictional AIDS narrative and linking both to questions of care, violence, and survival, the novel reframes biopolitical dynamics transnationally and expands the critical scope of multiethnic US literary study.

  4. “A Resurrection That is beyond Death”: African Diasporic Healing Rituals in Toni Morrison’s Paradise
    Martha J Cutter · pp. 73-96 · online 2026-07-27
    摘要

    This article concerns medical racism and sexual trauma in Toni Morrison’s Paradise (1997), but also the innovative African Diasporic healing ceremonies the novel depicts, which are key to understanding the mysterious deaths and seeming rebirth of the women who reside in the Convent and are (in theory) massacred by the men from Ruby at the end of the novel. I argue that the healing figure at the center of this novel (Consolata Sosa) creates a unique boundary-melting ceremony that ties together fragments from three different traditions of Africanist healing to create a new ritual that enables physical and spiritual rebirth. Connie draws on Nigerian Òs∘un healing rituals involving water, Afro-Brazilian practices of repair originating in Candomblé, and a configuration of feminine agency and revivification present in the Gnostic and Egyptian-influenced Christian gospel “The Thunder, Perfect Mind” and its invocation of the Egyptian goddess Isis. This last topic, in particular— Morrison’s use of the Gnostic Gospel and the figure of Isis, who was thought to bring people back to life—is key to comprehension of how the novel creates an innovative ritual that facilitates (in Morrison’s own words) “a resurrection that is beyond death.” The healing ceremony at the heart of the novel engenders new psychic and corporeal restorations even after the “death” of the Convent women. The women can therefore return from death to repair the bonds of their communities and institute ongoing forms of reconciliation and reparative justice.

  5. “Playing Latinx” as Settler-Colonial Reenactment
    Sophia Martínez Abbud · pp. 97-113 · online 2026-05-28
    摘要

    This is an article about the “problem of the indio” in a transamerican context of migration, and about accounting for the political role of Indigeneity in contemporary Latinx and Latin American literature. The figure of “the Indian” has been a central concept for national literatures in the so-called Americas since the nineteenth century, and its role becomes further complicated when considering the convergence of Indigeneities across the United States and Mexico and their unique nationalist literary traditions. In considering the intersections of these literary traditions in the space of the US-Mexico borderlands, this article identifies the narrative act of “playing Latinx,” whereby texts about the US-Mexico borderlands perform the dual reenactment of mestizaje and settler-colonial history. Using a Critical Latinx Indigeneities framework to analyze Valeria Luiselli’s migration texts—Tell Me How It Ends: An Essay in Forty Questions (2017) and Lost Children Archive (2019), to which I refer jointly as the “Lost Children Project”—this article traces the related but distinct intellectual genealogies that reduce Indigeneity to a cultural trait rather than a political condition.

  6. Eva’s (In)visible Man: Reading Gayl Jones through the Lens of Black Male Vulnerability
    Tuula Kolehmainen · pp. 114-133 · online 2026-07-17
    摘要

    Despite its title, Gayl Jones’s novel Eva’s Man (1976) is not a story of a man. Instead, it is a disturbing account of the experiences of an abused woman, Eva Medina Canada, who grotesquely murders Davis Carter, a man she had met a few days prior. While exploring Eva’s Man from the perspective of Black women’s victimization is relevant and necessary, in this article, I read beyond a victim/perpetrator binary and focus attention on the male victim and female perpetrators that are seldom addressed in critical readings of this novel or in scholarship more generally. Drawing on Tommy J. Curry’s theorization of Black male vulnerability (Man-Not), I examine the (in)visibility of Black men as victims and women as perpetrators across the spectrum of intergenerational and interpersonal violence reproduced in society and reflected in the novel. I argue that reading the male character as not only a metonym for masculine aggression against women but also as a victim allows us to understand the novel more deeply. Not only does Davis stand for and remind Eva of her previous abusers but also he is a victim of the same cycle of violence in which Eva is victimized and in which all the characters, men and women, are somehow involved. Rereading the novel through the lens of Black male vulnerability enables us to see beyond racialized gender stereotypes and binaries and focus instead on the spectrum of violence itself to better understand its psychological, social, and political functions and ramifications.

  7. “The Logic Is Built Right into the Architecture”: Speculative Architecture in Contemporary Queer-of-Color Science Fiction
    Kathryn Harlan-Gran · pp. 134-157 · online 2026-07-17
    摘要

    This article studies Rivers Solomon’s An Unkindness of Ghosts (2017) and Larissa Lai’s Salt Fish Girl (2002) to explore a concept that I term speculative architecture, which describes speculative, humanmade spaces and structures onto which authors layer social meaning. I argue that authors such as Solomon and Lai leverage the generic characteristics of speculative fiction to write social ideologies into the physical constructs of the world, posing a challenge to forms of oppression that rely on our understandings of such spaces, and their structuring logics, as natural and innate. In An Unkindness of Ghosts, Solomon transcribes histories of the Middle Passage onto the futuristic spaceship, challenging certain sciencefiction traditions that treat interstellar colonization as an idealized future for humanity without acknowledging the circumstances necessary for such a future to come about. In Salt Fish Girl, Lai critiques the racialized and gendered histories of inclusion and exclusion written into sub/urban borders and the mechanisms of control necessary to maintain those borders. Interestingly, both novels suggest gardens as potentially revolutionary interstitial spaces, in which the protagonists explore possibilities not prescribed by the material structures of oppression that otherwise dictate their lives. Thus, attention to speculative architectures highlights various possibilities for navigating narrative and material spaces, offering alternative epistemologies of both familiar and fantastical structures. Importantly, these characteristics are transferable across other works of speculative fiction, including not only sci-fi but also horror, fantasy, and magical realism, offering an interpretive lens that can be used to broadly articulate representations of spatiality, materiality, and sociality.

  8. Dirty Words: Lack and/of Innocence in Toni Morrison’s The Bluest Eye
    Rachel Windsor · pp. 158-177 · online 2026-06-12
    摘要

    Toni Morrison’s The Bluest Eye (1970) depicts a complex interplay of lack and fetishism. While many scholars read narrator Claudia and protagonist Pecola in opposition—emblematic of childhood survival and failure, respectively—this article argues that the novel links the girls insofar as both are excluded from an innocence primarily associated with white childhood. Both girls respond to the shameful sense of lack that emerges from their unfulfilled desire for innocence by fetishizing symbols of the ideal object. While innocence depends on negation, fetishization operates by substituting one love-object for another. As such, fetishization necessarily fails to “produce” innocence for either girl. Claudia, who fetishizes cleanliness and “good grammar,” recognizes the inadequacy of her substitution even as she maintains an attachment to it. She consequently exhibits what David Eng and Shinhee Han call “racial melancholia,” an orientation toward lack that permeates the formal elements of her narrative. Conversely Pecola, who fetishizes the titular blue eyes, uncritically adores her substitution. Enacting the process of incorporation theorized by Nicolas Abraham and Maria Torok allows Pecola to keep innocence “alive” within her unconscious but also requires she compulsively stage the fantasy of the object’s continued existence. Attending to the girls’ shared melancholia alongside the parallels between them ultimately enables a reparative reading that honors their position in Morrison’s lineage of complex girlhood friendships.

  9. Political Act and Poetic Act: An Interview with Kimiko Hahn
    Tracy Floreani, Kate Ruffer · pp. 178-188 · online 2026-06-12

    This interview with renowned Asian American poet Kimiko Hahn, Chancellor of the Academy of American Poets and New York State Poet Laureate, 2025-27, includes biographical information about her life growing up with artistic parents in a mixed-ethnicity family, the blended influences of Japanese and European poetic forms on her work, her childhood love of language, activism as a young feminist in the 1960s and 70 s, the roles of unconscious processes and formal structures within the creative process, and the place of poetry in our social landscapes.

  11. Colored Women Sittin’ on High: Womanist Sermonic Practice in Literature and Music. Melanie R. Hill
    Lynn Domina · pp. 192-194 · online 2026-06-04
    摘要

    Melanie R. Hill’s Colored Women Sittin’ on High: Womanist Sermonic Practice in Literature and Music provides a useful interdisciplinary analysis of women’s public speech and performance as it is affiliated with theology and social justice. The book includes discussion of African American literary characters as well as actual women, ordained or not, who have preached and sung their way into local, regional, and national influence. Hill’s argument relies on insights drawn from literary theory, critical race theory, classic and contemporary theology, and musicology. Its coverage sweeps across twentieth- and twenty-first-century America with references to earlier periods. Given the disciplinary isolation within which many scholars work, nearly every reader will find much that is new here. In her introductory chapter, Hill links these disciplines through attention to “sermonic practice.” That is, while most of the figures Hill explores are not literal ministers preaching from a literal pulpit, they all engage with readers and listeners as a preacher might through “prayers, prophecy, and praise” (1). As specifically African American women often communicating with a primarily African American audience, they participate in the ministry of the Black church in America, which understands its call as liberatory. Hill interprets scenes from literature and musical performances as doing the work of sermons, but she also examines sermons—the texts and the delivery—as a literary genre. In this chapter, she devotes extensive space to contextualizing her argument and defining her terms, not only the specifically religious ones such as “sermonic practice” but also more theoretical ones such as “womanist,” a term used primarily within literary studies as an African American elaboration on, amplification of, and critique of feminism. She acknowledges the particularly patriarchal assumptions and practices embedded in many religious traditions, a fact that prompts her analysis and approach.

  1. Editor’s Introduction: Technologies of Mediation
    Katsuya Hirano · pp. 349-355 · online 2026-08-01
  2. Esperanto on the Radio: Broadcasting Infrastructure and the Politics of Universal Speech in the Japanese Empire
    Edwin Michielsen, Derek Kramer · pp. 357-381 · online 2026-08-01
    摘要

    This article explores the interface between Esperanto and the radio in the Japanese Empire. Viewing radio as more than simply a mode of conveyance, it examines the tensions that emerged when a universalizing language met a universalizing medium. The following sections explore how new communication infrastructure enabled Esperantists’ claims to political salience. Radio in the Japanese empire helped articulate powerful forecasts of political solidarity, liberated from the restraints of space or state. However, Esperanto on the radio also appeared in ways that deepened the borders of colonial relations and linguistic hierarchies. The article first discusses the history of radio infrastructure in the Japanese Empire and its role in shaping linguistic topography of the 1920s and 1930s. It then turns to a case study of the 1928 “Esperanto Evening” broadcast from colonial Taiwan by Japanese Esperantists. As one of the few surviving examples of when universalizing language and media met, the transcript of this broadcast reveals the limits of Esperanto’s emancipatory reach.

  3. Self-Rescue in Media Networks: De-Blackboxing, Shanzhai Cyborgs, and Survival Among Workers with Pneumoconiosis in China
    Chu Wang · pp. 383-409 · online 2026-08-01
    摘要

    This article examines media networks as both the central object and method of analysis for understanding how Chinese workers with pneumoconiosis navigate survival in a postsocialist risk society where many are systematically denied compensation. Rather than emphasizing overt collective resistance, the study focuses on two forms of media-enabled self-rescue. First, it explores the “de-blackboxing strategy,” whereby workers use and rely on news reports and various social media platforms to give the slow violence of occupational disease public visibility and pursue justice. Second, it investigates the rise of “shanzhai cyborgs,” in which workers draw on technical knowledge shared within grassroots communities to construct life-sustaining homemade oxygen machines. The article contends that these fragile and improvisational modes of survival are neither isolated acts of heroism nor conventional political protest, but instead emerge through ongoing interaction and circulation within decentralized media networks. It argues that the effectiveness of self-rescue often depends on chains of mediation that translate, amplify, and mobilize private experiences into public events capable of driving tangible change. By dissecting these mediated practices, the article offers a new perspective on what resistance and survival mean for pneumoconiosis workers who are rendered disposable in contemporary China.

  4. Entering the Realm of Huan : Rethinking Immersion Through Early Modern Chinese Accounts of the Strange
    Jiayi Chen · pp. 411-436 · online 2026-08-01
    摘要

    This essay explores how immersion—its generative mechanisms, specific experiences, and cultural implications—was imagined and conceptualized in early modern accounts of the strange (yi 異). Rather than locating historical precursors to modern immersive technologies such as virtual reality (VR), it isolates immersion as an experiential mode from technologically driven paradigms that foreground presence within computer-simulated worlds. Drawing on sixteenth- and seventeenth-century accounts of the strange, particularly Pu Songling’s 蒲松齡 (1640–1715) Strange Tales from Liaozhai, this essay demonstrates that immersive experience emerges not from make-believe or technological sophistication but through multisensory engagement with liminal spaces where reality and illusion intermingle. Central to these tales is the rendering of huan 幻—not as abstract illusion but a deeply felt condition of absorption, triggered by techniques such as huanxi 幻戲 (illusion performance) and media like murals and narrative writing. These stories depict immersion as arising when everyday environments shift into the extraordinary and when multisensory experiences challenge established perceptual frameworks. They reveal a phenomenological understanding of the interplay between embodied sensory cognition and mediated environments. By approaching immersion from within these historical narratives and the reading experience they afford, this essay highlights the critical potential of accounts of the strange to illuminate a culturally specific, pluralistic understanding of immersion that technology-centered discourses frequently overlook.

  5. The Neighborhood as Refuge: Dignity, Inclusiveness, and Compassion in Takeda Rintarō’s “Kamagasaki” (1933)
    Ran Wei · pp. 437-461 · online 2026-08-01
    摘要

    Kamagasaki, an urban enclave in Osaka, maintains a long history of marginalization that can be traced to the late 1890s. This article examines the representation of Kamagasaki in Osaka writer Takeda Rintarō’s novella “Kamagasaki.” Published in 1933, shortly after the economically prosperous “Greater Osaka” (“Dai Osaka”) period had ended, the story features the underclass and working-class people in the neighborhood of Kamagasaki, revealing how Osaka’s development has concealed issues, including economic exploitation and social division. This article introduces the concepts of “a compassionate participant” instead of adopting existing tropes of observers of city life, such as the flâneur and the “curiosity hunter” (ryōkisha). Through the perspective of such an observer, Takeda represents the issues of poverty, segregation, and homelessness in a humanistic manner. Simultaneously, by constructing various voices for fictional characters in the novella, Takeda highlights the importance of the “internal gaze” and voices from Kamagasaki. This article argues that by emphasizing how the locals value dignity and interpersonal bonds, Takeda’s representations of Kamagasaki subvert the stereotype of urban margins as morally degenerate. Through his conscious resistance to the social stigma imposed on Kamagasaki by dominant national discourse, which reduces Kamagasaki to a place of degeneration and danger, Takeda depicts a neighborhood characterized by shared attachment and resilience.

  6. Nakai Masakazu and the Revolutionary Potential of Consumption: From Bi-hihyō to the “Logic of the Committee” and Kyoto’s “Housing Problem” in 1930s Japan
    Kyle Peters · pp. 463-494 · online 2026-08-01
    摘要

    This article focuses on the prewar theoretical and print activities of Kyoto school philosopher Nakai Masakazu. It spotlights Nakai’s efforts to confront alienation under finance capitalism and inspire a new “critical” and “cooperative” form of mass subjectivity in consumers. The article contributes to extant scholarship in two ways. First, it develops Nakai’s novel interpretation of Marxism and consumer struggle in connection with his mid-1930s article “Logic of the Committee” (“Iinkai no ronri”) and his work on the tabloid-format newspaper Doyōbi (Saturday). Second, it provides a broader picture of Nakai’s philosophical development by showing how these ideas trace back to his involvement with an understudied set of coterie journals from the early 1930s: Bi-hihyō (Aesthetics/Criticism) and Sekai bunka (World Culture). The article divides into three parts, each of which moves across theory and praxis. Part 1 focuses on Nakai’s work on Bi-hihyō and Sekai bunka. It traces the historical development of Nakai’s early philosophy of collectivity, media technology, and specialization and massification in connection with the “crisis” conditions of early 1930s Japan. Part 2 focuses on Nakai’s attempts to develop and consolidate these concepts in the context of finance capitalism in his seminal “Logic of the Committee.” In particular, it shows how Nakai reinterpreted these early philosophical concepts in terms of consumer alienation. Part 3 then focuses on Nakai’s response to this condition of consumer alienation in his tabloid newspaper Doyōbi. It reveals how these sustained engagements in philosophy and print developed into a novel interpretation of Marxism from the standpoint of consumer struggle, and it shows how this connected with the so-called “housing problem” (jūtaku mondai) or “housing crisis” that peppered the pages of Doyōbi before its censorship. It ends by briefly considering how Nakai’s philosophy offers a novel contribution to twentieth-century Western Marxism.

    pp. 495-496 · online 2026-08-01

  1. Editor’s Introduction: Dis/Ability
    Suzy Kim
  2. Contributors
  3. Flattening and Unflattening Space: Photography and the 1975 Okinawa International Ocean Exposition
    Daniel Abbe
    摘要

    Through an analysis of photographs produced in and around the 1975 Okinawa International Ocean Exposition, this essay develops the concepts of flattening and unflattening in material and visual registers. In line with the construction championed by Prime Minister Tanaka Kakuei, the Ocean Expo demonstrates that the Japanese state took post-reversion Okinawa as a space to be flattened into the mainland. Drawing on a phenomenological approach to space, the essay explores photographs published in Ocean Expo News, an official publication of the Ocean Expo. These promotional photographs visually flattened out the land around the expo site. In this way, they oriented Okinawa within cartographic space, ready for assimilation. The essay also examines a series of photographs taken on the Amami Islands that the mainland photographer Nakahira Takuma made in response to the Ocean Expo. Through consistently disorienting photographic space itself, they suggest a mode of unflattening. Photographs taken by Okinawan activist Ahagon Shōkō of land on Iejima also warp the cartographic space of the state and the US military, as if pushing through the second dimension into the third. Taken together, these three groups of photographs suggest that an unflattening of space might happen through the body.

  4. From “Crawling Grids” to “Processing Words”: “Stylus” Transition and Computer Writing in the 1990s Chinese Cultural Marketization
    Zihan Feng
    摘要

    This essay examines the understudied role of personal computers in China’s literary transformation, focusing on the shift from handwriting to computer writing led by established literary elites circa the 1990s. It first contextualizes writers’ switch to computer writing within early-reform China’s information craze, exposing the purported efficiency of computer writing that allured writers and engineers alike as a constructed technophilic myth. It then contends that the adoption of personal computers has reconfigured literary composition into “processing words,” which manifests in three aspects: compartmentalized and human-machine entangled workflows, reinterpretation of the Chinese language stimulated by input methods, and writers’ negotiation with their new “processor” roles within the burgeoning and diversifying Chinese cultural market. Particularly, this essay closely analyzes Chen Cun 陈村 and his computer-written novel Fresh Flowers And 鲜花和 (1997) as an epitome of established writers’ shared navigation of the computerized creative environment. Writers grapple with the paradoxical effect of literary creation becoming “processing,” which unleashes new textual and paratextual expressive possibilities for literary experimentation but simultaneously desanctifies literary elites in the increasingly streamlined cultural production mechanism. This essay thus also identifies the nation’s pursuit of information modernity as a crucial catalyst of the cultural commodification witnessed in 1990s China.

  5. Father Jiggs in Shanghai: The Rise and Fall of Comic Strips in Republican China in Transnational Context
    Eike Exner
    摘要

    This article traces the popularization of modern comic strips in Republican-era Shanghai via such strips serialized in the English-language newspaper The China Press. Consumed by both foreign and Chinese readers, these comics were some of The China Press’s most attractive features, especially George McManus’s strip Bringing Up Father, which appears to have been nearly universally known in Shanghai. From around 1920, the characters, form, and visual vocabulary of comics such as Bringing Up Father increasingly permeated Shanghai media and society, with significant overlap between English- and Chinese-language publications and audiences. The use of comics for advertising purposes played an especially large role in familiarizing Shanghai readers with comics, culminating in the appearance of the first major serialized Chinese-language comics in 1928. A central factor in this local establishment of comics as a transnational narrative technology of modernity appears to have been their contemporaneous embrace by periodicals published in Japan, which were widely available in Shanghai. Japanese military aggression during the 1930s conversely would preempt the continued development of Republican Chinese comics.

  6. Continuously Leaving the Closet: A Queer/Crip Approximation to Kondō Ginga’s Artworks
    Aliwen
    摘要

    This essay examines the intermedial practice of Japanese artist Kondō Ginga, situating her work within the historical and theoretical contexts of postwar disability activism, queer/crip discourse, and feminist interventions in Japan. Through close readings of her works The Notifications (2021), Queer Street (2021), Separated (2022), Continuously Leaving the Closet (2022), and Prides in Streets (2024), the essay proposes that Kondō’ oeuvre renegotiates the intersections of digital and physical space, public and private visibility, and queer and disabled subjectivities. Kondō’s practice is analyzed along three dimensions: (a) the dissolution of the digital-physical divide; (b) small-scale, affective forms of reworlding and activism; and (c) sustained self-articulation across mediated and relational contexts. Historical case studies, from Ueno Park’ 1948 queer resistance to figures such as Yokota Hiroshi, Yonezu Tomoko, Asaka Yūho, and Hanada Minoru, contextualize her interventions, revealing continuities in gendered/queer and disabled visibility, exclusion, and resistance. The essay argues that Kondō transforms embodied limitation, chronic illness, and digital mediation into a politics of intimacy and care, redefining activism beyond public protest. Her work enacts “crip world-building,” demonstrating that survival, relationality, and aesthetic intervention constitute simultaneous, iterative forms of queer/disabled presence, challenging conventional notions of liberation, pride, and collective identities.

  7. Guo Moruo, André Malraux: Impaired Revolutionaries and the Gramophone in Early Twentieth-Century Anti-Colonial China
    Yu Wang
    摘要

    This article brings together science and technology studies, sound studies, postcolonial history, and critical disability studies by examining how the intellectuals André Malraux (1901–1976) and Guo Moruo (1892–1978), with speaking or hearing impairments, conceptualized sound reproduction technologies during the peak of imperialism. It focuses on how their ideas were entangled with and articulated through bodily experiences during the anti-colonial revolution and the global expansion of technological innovations. By analyzing their writings on the gramophone, this study highlights how bodily impairment influences cultural imagination and challenges the conventional narratives that are inattentive to the social experience of disability. It first investigates how Guo articulated his view of the gramophone against the backdrop of an ableist revolutionary culture, thereby adapting a distinctive sonic imagination to align with anti-colonial movements. It then supplements this with the works of Malraux to evaluate the potential of a disability-mediated view of revolution in the greater anti-colonial world. In so doing, this article assesses how the intellectuals’ impaired bodies, as sites of engagements with revolution and sound technologies like the gramophone, provide a valuable lens for contesting ableist assumptions and a critical understanding of the historical dynamics of auditory culture.

Journal of Japanese Studies

Vol. 52, No. 2 · 2026-08-01 · 28 篇 · Society for Japanese Studies · Japan · Peer-reviewed
  1. A Businesswoman in Meiji Japan
    Garrett L. Washington, Alexandre Roy · pp. 321-359
    摘要

    Women are conspicuously underexamined in the business and economic histories of modern Japan. Although wives and widows played central roles in the financial management of their families and family businesses, they have typically remained hidden behind the names and accomplishments of their more visible male relations. This was true not only in Japan but also in other countries undergoing rapid industrialization in the long nineteenth century. Some women, however, did leave traces of their economic activity and leadership. As scholars encounter more and more of these so-called anomalies, the need grows for new, more inclusive historical narratives that can account for their existence and impacts. This article responds by studying the involvement of one especially visible married businesswoman in the development of her marital family’s coal mine in Kyushu’s Chikuhō region. More specifically we investigate the networking and management approaches of Hirooka Asako (1849–1919) and contextualize them with those of female business leaders working in male-dominated industries in and beyond Japan. Unexamined or understudied contracts, financial records, speeches, interviews, and editorials as well as private, unofficial sources like correspondence, memoirs, and eulogies reveal that she played a leading role in Uruno Mine’s development and management. Mobilizing determination, familial and professional networks with men, and direct management strategies, she personally oversaw the metamorphosis of this failing project into a successful, profitable enterprise. Her methods and achievements in fact resemble those of prewar modern Japan’s few famous businesswomen, of a few well-known Western women entrepreneurs, and, likely, of many less visible women. If, as we demonstrate, dynamic, enterprising women were active entrepreneurs, respected collaborators of men, and influential contributors to Japan’s economic and industrial development during the Meiji period (1868–1912), the inclusion of their stories can complicate scholars’ understanding of Japanese business and economic history.

  2. The Hearts of People in the World
    Thomas Gaubatz · pp. 360-388
    摘要

    This article explores the economic dimensions of the late fiction of the seventeenth-century writer Ihara Saikaku, asking how the author’s understanding of the marketplace shaped his humanistic studies of “the hearts of people” (hito-gokoro). Applying methods of economic criticism to the posthumous Tying Off Saikaku’s Loose Ends (Saikaku oridome, 1694), I argue that the author’s late writings develop a formal homology between the imperfect rationality of the early market economy and the ineffable dynamism of the human heart.

  3. Grounding Empire Through Burial Grounds
    Yuting Dong · pp. 389-420
    摘要

    This article examines how the Japanese military, notably the Kwantung Army, used the acquisition and maintenance of military burial grounds to challenge Chinese sovereignty in Manchuria (currently Northeast China) and expand Japan’s territorial control in seemingly “peaceful” periods. The burial grounds became contested sites where debates over sovereignty and different groups’ claims of land use played out. With a close focus on the military-use land, this article challenges the previous depiction of Japanese imperialism in Manchuria as informal imperialism and enriches our understanding of how the military reshaped social and political relationships in the empire through the control of land. This article also enriches historical writing on the Kwantung Army, which tends to focus on its military roles. The Kwantung Army in Manchuria not only enacted its power through military actions but also far more extensively penetrated Manchuria’s local communities even before the Manchukuo era (1932–45).

  4. To the Virtuous Go the Spoils
    Jeremy A. Sather · pp. 421-454
    摘要

    This article examines Taiheiki (Chronicle of Great Peace, fourteenth century) as a critical intervention in medieval Japanese historiography. It challenges conventional court-versus-rebel binaries, framing the fragmentation of authority during the Nanbokuchō age through Neo-Confucian concepts of virtue, such as the three bonds and five virtues, the Mandate of Heaven, and revolutionary reign change. Figures like Emperor Go-Daigo and his loyal general Kusunoki Masashige expose the erosion of imperial legitimacy through failed moral governance. Taiheiki offers a flexible, genre-defying model of historical change, challenging myths of imperial inviolability and anticipating early modern re-conceptualizations of virtue, sovereignty, and political order.

  5. Ainu Handicrafts
    Michael Hayata · pp. 455-483
    摘要

    This article examines the mass commercialization of Ainu handicrafts as Hokkaido’s official folk art and signature souvenir during the 1930s. Focusing on the political economy of these works, it analyzes how the Japanese empire constructed a colonial handicraft industry that promoted the popular consumption of Ainu woodcarvings and attus textiles as symbols of Japan’s civilizing mission over the Ainu people. Despite such appropriation, Ainu craftspeople repurposed these works as instruments of political mobilization to confront colonial inequalities. At Asahikawa, Ainu activists from the Third Chikabumi Land Dispute envisioned collective handicraft workshops as a centerpiece of their proposal to dismantle their reservation and construct a new village. In the Saru River basin, Ainu weavers also drew on community-based management of mountain forests to connect attus textile production to Indigenous land rights. By tracing Ainu cultural production across these sites, the article argues that Ainu handicrafts were critical sites for the articulation of Ainu demands for land recovery, local autonomy, and equitable resource use.

  6. Acting Like Female Spirit Mediums
    H. Yumi Kim · pp. 484-510
    摘要

    In 1913, Nakachi Kamado called for a public trial in Naha, Okinawa, to contest the accusation that she had acted as a yuta, or female spirit medium. The trial captivated city residents, drawing hundreds to the courtroom and even more to commercial theaters featuring plays about the trial. This article argues that the trial and plays articulated tensions of class and gender pulsating through the city of Naha. From police surveillance to the dispossession of the former ruling class and an influx of working-class women, tensions wrought by Japanese colonialism and urbanization found powerful expression in the trial and plays.

  1. Popular Nanyang
    Beng Huat Chua · pp. 523-525 · online 2026-08-13
    摘要
