  2. Popular Nanyang: re-thinking Chinese cultures in post-war Singapore and Malaya/Malaysia
    Lanjun Xu, Cheow Thia Chan · pp. 526-538 · online 2026-08-13
    摘要

    This special issue aims to rethink the significance of “popular culture” for Singapore and Malayan/Malaysian Chinese studies, and more broadly, for modern Chinese cultural studies. Located in the heart of Nanyang, Singapore and Malaya/Malaysia are well-known sites of vibrant Chinese cultural production. After World War Two, besides literature written by intellectuals, cultural forms with wider appeal have enriched the everyday lives of Chinese in the region. From large-scale publishing, to theatre, to radio broadcast, to film and television, the mix of old and new media technologies enlivened by multiple languages and Chinese dialects were entangled with complex commercial and ideological concerns. This special issue frames the resultant sociocultural formation as “Popular Nanyang,” offering a conceptual framework as well that analyse how people’s leisure preferences and ideas about regionality associated with Singapore, Malaya/Malaysia—and beyond—were animated by quotidian and translocal practices of cultural production and consumption. Collectively, the articles demonstrate how the “popular” in its varied senses of extensive reach, non-state positionality, substantial profitability, content accessibility, and contemporary resonance enabled “Nanyang” as a regional imaginary and a cultural sensibility to survive, travel, and reinvent itself across shifting political and historical contexts from the 1950s till the 1990s despite the rise of “Southeast Asia” as the favoured alternative. Advocating for a cultural history of Chinese communities composed from a grassroots perspective, the special issue harnesses the internal diversity of “Popular Nanyang” to point towards a future mode of historical analysis that recognizes the non-Chinese and non-Sinophone dimensions of mass-oriented Nanyang culture.

  3. Transborder malleability: examining popular Nanyang through film stardom
    Min Hui Yeo · pp. 539-559 · online 2026-08-05
    摘要

    What does it mean to characterise someone, something or somewhere as “Nanyang”? Whether in studies of Nanyang elite or popular cultures, the term is today frequently invoked as a shorthand for a place-based Southeast Asian Chinese identity formed predominantly through acclimatisation to, and interaction with, local cultures and environments, especially in the context of Singapore. This paper rethinks the notion of “popular Nanyang” beyond this usual paradigm through an examination of Singaporean film star Chong Sit Fong’s regional stardom in the 1950s and 1960s. Uncovering how Chong simultaneously embodied different kinds of Chinese national, local and translocal identities, this paper illustrates how the characteristic of transborder malleability, which refers to the capacity to simultaneously embody seemingly contradictory ideologies across borders, enabled Chong to attain regional film stardom during a time of geopolitical flux. This gestures towards an understanding of Nanyang Chineseness not only as place-based, but also inherently translocal and transnational, suggesting how “Nanyang” indexes a flexible modality of identity that functions across geopolitical borders during the 1950s and 1960s.

  4. Popular crossings: Lin Chen and his bifurcated editorial practice in 1950s Singapore and Malaya
    Cheow Thia Chan · pp. 560-582 · online 2026-08-14
    摘要

    This article reframes the Singapore Chinese dramatist Lin Chen’s cultural practice by explicating discrepant modes of popular cultural production and circulation in 1950s Singapore and Malaya through commercial printing. Today, Lin Chen (1919–2004) is remembered primarily as the pioneering playwright-director who shaped Singapore’s Mandarin theatre scene after the second World War. Alongside his amateur theatrical engagements, he served vocationally as an editor at the World Book Company (Shijie Shuju), one of the five major localised Chinese-language publishing houses. It is now revealed that Lin helmed both Linglong (Smart) and Wenyu Shenghuo (Culture and Amusement), two leisure magazines published contemporaneously around the mid-1950s. Whereas Smart carried salacious content, Culture and Amusement promoted “healthy arts and entertainment,” focusing on collective performing art forms. The two publications’ conflicting stances on nurturing readers’ interests placed them on opposing sides of the anti-yellow culture campaign in Singapore, which criticised the widespread societal access to prurient material. Examining the two periodicals in tandem as well as the content within Smart, this article outlines how Lin negotiated fluid ideas about the landscape of mass market publishing that spans the nature of the content, his non-state positionality as a cultural actor, the journals’ audience constituencies, and the contents’ varying degrees of contemporary resonance with the decolonising ethos in Singapore and Malaya. The inter-journal and intra-journal analyses reveal the intransparency of Chinese culture labelled as “yellow” and how the idea of the “popular”—represented through overlapping vernacular senses in Chinese—index an internally dissonant landscape of grassroots culture.

  5. Yellow panic: Yao Zi, popular literature and gendered modernity in Cold War Malaya
    Ying Xin Show · pp. 583-601 · online 2026-07-22
    摘要

    During the peak of the anti-yellow cultural movement in Singapore (1953–1956), Yao Zi—now recognised as a pioneering figure in postwar Sinophone Malayan literature—was criticised as a pornographic “yellow” writer. This article explores the cultural debates and phenomena of 1950s Malaya, a period of social transformation in the late colonial era shaped by ideological polarisation during the Cold War and anti-colonial nationalism. It focuses on the moral panic surrounding “yellow”—a term in which concerns surrounding sexuality and Chinese ethnicity intersect—through a historical and cultural analysis of the author Yao Zi’s literary and editorial works. It situates them within broader debates about yellow literature, demonstrating the ways sexuality, ethnicity, and womanhood were constructed as moral categories of social engagement within the Cold War-era battlegrounds of modernity and decolonisation. The first section of this article reexamines the anti-yellow cultural movement and its associated moral discourse by introducing Yao and his conceptualisation of popular literature in Nanyang, showing how they challenged ideological binaries and gender norms during the cultural Cold War. The second section shifts to another dimension of yellow panic: its intersections with Chinese identity and gender. Focusing on the romance genre that secured Yao’s literary prominence, it analyses his lesser-known novella The Storm (1954) for its depiction of women's narrative presence and the Chinese experience of gendered modernity in Malaya, an experience conditioned by a cultural nationalism uneasy with hybridity.

  6. Huang Ya’s Wisteria and the Cold War middlebrow: melodrama, Manichean worlds, and Mahua literature
    Li Wen Jessica Tan · pp. 602-617 · online 2026-08-10
    摘要

    As the longest-serving editor of the literary magazine Chao Foon, Malaysian Chinese (Mahua) writer Huang Ya (1932–1992) is widely regarded as a central figure in shaping Mahua modernism during the 1960s. Existing scholarship has largely focused on his editorial advocacy for Malayanisation and modern Mahua literature within a Cold War framework, while less attention has been paid to his fiction, which draws more heavily on melodramatic than modernist aesthetics. This article examines Huang’s best-selling romance Wisteria (1960) through the lens of the “Cold War middlebrow,” understood here as a contingent literary formation that served sociocultural, sentimental, and pedagogical functions during a period of polarised ideologies. By analysing Wisteria’s adaptation of the melodramatic mode for a local readership, this article offers a reassessment of what constitutes the “popular” in Mahua literature, showing how Huang’s fiction engaged questions of taste, value, and affect within a rapidly changing decolonising milieu. Through the subversion of melodrama’s Manichean binaries and the reconfiguration of the relationship between modernism and the middlebrow, Huang demonstrates a self-conscious effort to balance accessibility with aesthetic ambition. Situating Wisteria within the intersecting contexts of Cold War cultural politics and inter-Asian literary networks connecting Hong Kong and Malaya, this article highlights how popular Mahua literature participated in the circulation of cultural influences and the forging of new national identities, thereby expanding critical understandings of both “literary value” and the “popular” within the Mahua canon.

  7. Reading popular Nanyang: literary realism in plurilingual Singapore
    Shuang Shen · pp. 618-632 · online 2026-05-26
    摘要

    This article approaches the topic of Nanyang popular culture from the perspective of language use and language politics. Revisiting a canonical text by Miao Xiu, Singapore under the Roofs (1951), which is set in Singapore on the cusp of decolonisation, this article examines the left-leaning realist camp’s engagement with a plurilingual and transnational public sphere where different forms of popular culture—some profit-driven, others politically oriented—competed for resources and audiences. The story’s incorporation of non-Mandarin Chinese languages and non-standard linguistic registers draws attention to how linguistic variations associated with the subaltern class mediate access to their subjectivity. In seeking to broaden the scope of current discussions of the language question beyond the conventional framing of the state’s ideology of language in Singapore, the analysis here refutes the technological determinism inherent in excluding literature in print from popular culture simply because its medium of production has been overtaken by “newer” technologies.

  8. Nanyang popular culture on Singapore television (1963–1979)
    Guo-Quan Seng · pp. 633-650 · online 2026-08-05
    摘要

    This essay explores the film-to-television evolution of the Nanyang popular cultural imaginary between Hong Kong and Singapore in the 1960s and 1970s. Not having the capacity to produce films or television serials, Singapore was heavily reliant on Hong Kong films to fill its airtime, and in so doing, gave the diasporic imaginary projected by films a second lease of life on television. Television was a more capacious medium than film—a wider range of popular cultural performances were featured on its variety shows. The more literary and artistic (wenyi) songs surprisingly sold as well if not better than their liuxing counterparts. Combined, television and popular music remade the Nanyang popular cultural imaginary for Singapore, as the radical left’s Malayanisation cultural vision became increasingly marginalised from the reality of Singapore’s independence and its development in the capitalistic world.

  9. Popular Nanyang and the construction of a pluralist Sinophone community: the case of Rediffusion Singapore
    E. K. Tan · pp. 651-665 · online 2026-07-24
    摘要

    Originating in the UK in 1928 and introduced to Singapore in 1949, the cable radio service Rediffusion gained popularity for its affordable, multi-ethnic programming. In addition to a channel featuring Tamil, Malay, and English content, Rediffusion offered a dedicated Chinese channel that included not only Mandarin programmes but also broadcasts in various Sinitic dialects such as Hokkien, Cantonese, and Teochew. This article examines Rediffusion’s role from the 1950s to the 1970s in fostering a pluralistic Sinophone community in Singapore, collaborating closely with Chinese clan associations organized around Sinitic dialect affiliations. However, this linguistic diversity diminished with the implementation of the “Speak More Mandarin, Less Dialect” policy in 1979. In compliance with the policy, Rediffusion gradually reduced and eventually phased out its dialect programmes. I interpret this shift in Rediffusion’s programming as indicative of Singapore’s increasing linguistic homogenization, despite the People’s Action Party (PAP) government’s efforts to present the nation as multicultural and multilingual. The post-independence rhetoric of fostering a multicultural society with four official languages—English, Mandarin, Malay, and Tamil—ironically streamlined the diverse Sinophone community into a monolithic one, with Mandarin imposed as the “official” mother tongue for all Sinophone subjects, regardless of their dialect heritage.

  10. Projecting “healthy films”: Chong Gay Theatres, youth and Chinese cinema in late Cold War Singapore
    Lanjun Xu · pp. 666-688 · online 2026-08-13
    摘要

    This article examines Chong Gay Theatres’ role in circulating mainland Chinese cinema in Singapore from the 1970s to the 1980s under the leadership of Lim Djit Sun (1925–1987). Known for importing left-leaning Hong Kong films, Chong Gay shifted in the 1980s to Mandarin-language productions from the People’s Republic of China in collaboration with the Shanghai Film Corporation, a transition shaped by the Speak Mandarin Campaign, changing censorship policies, and generational shifts in audience taste. Emerging from networks around the anti-yellow culture movement and earlier left-leaning cultural and entertainment circles, Chong Gay recast progressive cultural ideals through the discourse of “healthy films” within an increasingly regulated media environment. Drawing on archival sources, media reports, and oral interviews, I trace how “healthy films” were redefined through the interaction of ideological commitment and commercial pressure during a period of cultural transition in Singapore. By examining film delegations and festivals in the 1980s, hybrid genres, and youth reception, I argue that Singapore became a key node in late Cold War transregional exchange, where films from Taiwan, Hong Kong, and mainland China converged and were reinterpreted. Intermediaries such as Lim promoted PRC cinema as an edifying alternative to commercial culture while sustaining cultural ties with reform-era China. Singapore thus functioned as a distribution hub and testing ground where PRC films were evaluated and adapted for Southeast Asian audiences. Although officially-sanctioned healthy films were associated with discipline and social harmony, their ambivalent reception reveals unease over the commercialisation of PRC cinema and the persistence of residual leftist sensibilities.

  11. Popular culture in the time of Greater Nanyang
    Caroline S. Hau · pp. 689-706 · online 2026-07-24
    摘要

    Cognisant of the multivalent, shifting meanings of “Nanyang” as a spatiotemporal, geopolitical, geo-economic, and cultural unit, this article seeks to flesh out the concept of “Popular Nanyang” by bringing in the contributions of popular culture from the Philippines, Indonesia, and Thailand. Historically, ethnic Chinese played an important role in the creation of “the popular,” albeit often dismissed as commercial or of low cultural value, in the plurilingual settings of their respective domiciles. Their networked production, distribution, circulation, consumption, and (e)valuation of popular culture helped substantiate the reach and bounds of “Nanyang” as geography, market, and regional imaginary. This article reclaims and repurposes the term “Greater Nanyang” to give weight to Southeast Asia, Hong Kong, Taiwan, and Macao as hubs for the multi-sited co-creation of the Popular Nanyang from the Cold War era to the end of the twentieth century. The Greater Nanyang concept highlights the historicity, materiality, politics, economies, ecologies, and cultures of localisation, meaning-making, and value creation that underpin place-, identity-, and claim-making in, across, and beyond the region. The temporal depth of Popular Nanyang also raises questions about Sinophone assumptions of the convergence of language, audience, cultural literacy, and ethnicity in the production, circulation, and consumption of popular culture.

  1. Perverted normalcy: on the cultural durability of authoritarianism
    Hilmar Farid · pp. 1-19 · online 2026-10-15
    摘要

    Authoritarianism in Indonesia persists not only through institutions and coercion, but through everyday understandings of what constitutes normal life. This article develops the concept of perverted normalcy to explain that persistence. Following a historical trajectory from Dutch colonialism through the New Order to the post-Reformasi present, it argues that unresolved violence has accumulated within social relations, shaping expectations, habits, and the limits of political imagination. Violence does not simply leave wounds in the past. It helps establish the terms on which the present is lived. The article examines how this condition took shape, survived democratic transition, and found renewed expression through digital governance. It then traces its reproduction across generations through family, education, media, and silence. These are the everyday settings in which domination becomes familiar, and its historical origins disappear from view. Perverted normalcy thus directs attention to a problem that institutional reform alone cannot resolve. Understanding authoritarian persistence across the region requires examining how histories of violence become ordinary social life, and how democratic transformation might interrupt their reproduction within the relationships and practices that organize everyday existence.

  2. “Asia” in the making: friction, relational authorship, and the pedagogy of productive discomfort in the Asia Documentary Co-Production Network: A critical autoethnography of transnational Zoom workshops, 2021–2026
    Chul Heo · pp. 1-16 · online 2026-10-06
    摘要

    This article argues that “Asia,” as a category of regional belonging, is not a precondition of transnational collaboration but a relation produced within it—assembled, incrementally and unevenly, in the friction of cross-border documentary co-production. Drawing on the Asia Documentary Co-Production Network (ADCN), a hybrid pedagogical project linking film schools across East and Southeast Asia, it reads the transcripts of monthly online meetings, rather than the finished films, as its primary text. Through a critical autoethnography written from the position of a faculty mentor and former chair, it traces friction in two directions: outward, where students raised in mutual national hostility come to reference one another laterally and so constitute the region in real time; and inward, where an encounter with another nation’s history estranges students from their own. Building a single conceptual chain from Tsing’s friction through platform mediation and radical listening to Chen’s inter-Asian referencing and critical syncretism, the article shows these concepts being enacted at the smallest scale—by undergraduates, over poor connections—and, crucially, where they fail. Against celebratory accounts of frictionless online collaboration, it insists on asymmetry: of resource, of language, and of the mentor’s power to redirect. “Asia” so understood is closer to a verb than a noun—permanently unfinished, and real in the measure of that unfinishedness.

  3. Epistemological friction in the contact zone: Ethiopian itinerant commerce, socio-cultural articulation, and the unlearning of race in Chinese trade cities
    Fekadu Malede Abitew · pp. 1-20 · online 2026-09-28
    摘要

    This article introduces the concept of racial unlearning to examine how racism is deconstructed within micro-level Afro-Chinese interactions. Drawing on longitudinal data generated among Ethiopian traders who travel between Ethiopia and China, I situate Ethio-Chinese encounters within Bhabha’s [Bhabha, Homi K. [1994] 2004. The Location of Culture. London: Routledge] “in-between spaces”—interstitial zones of fractured hegemony in which African agency produces not merely epistemic articulation but ontological consequence, carving out deracialised social realities. In these spaces, blackness and Chineseness emerge as liminal identities that evolve towards mutual recognition through iterative processes of racial unlearning. I argue that, within localised Afro-Chinese interactions, racialisation and racial unlearning unfold as concomitant processes—a dynamic that dominant global racial discourses have failed to anticipate. This reorientation shifts analytical attention away from macro- and meso-level scholarship preoccupied with the formation of racial hierarchies, and towards blackness and Chineseness as active, lived, and relationally constituted experiences.

South Asia: Journal of South Asian Studies

Vol. 49, No. 4 · 2026-07-04 · 13 篇 · Routledge · South Asia · Peer-reviewed
  1. Matters of Cultivated Leisure: Humility of Things, Rabindranath Tagore and Yogāyog (1929)
    Sagar Das · pp. 861-880 · online 2026-06-05
    摘要

    Rabindranath Tagore’s (1861–1941) critique of the political and urban civilisation of the West as opposed to the spiritually oriented civilisation of the East consolidated the materiality/spirituality binary in the Manichean world of colonial Bengal. This dichotomy is codified in Tagore’s commentary on cultivated leisure, establishing the nebulous idea of taste through negating the ‘thingness’ of subject-object relations. Complicating such absolute discursive divisions, this paper proposes materiality as a method to read Tagore’s 1929 novel, Relationships (originally published in Bengali in 1929 as Yogāyog). Contextualising the ambiguities of ‘thing theory’ within the colonial context of Bengal, this paper examines Tagore’s navigation of modernity and its conflictual materialities beyond an overdetermined rejection of the corporeal. Informed by Tagore’s writings on material culture, it locates interruptions in the novel’s metaphysical mode, disturbing the ‘humility of things’ to reveal implicit materialities that mark the leisured spaces of retreat from the colonial praxis.

  2. The Dark Ecology of Flash Floods and Hydropower Dams in Sikkim
    Prajwal Pakhrin · pp. 881-900 · online 2026-07-16
    摘要

    This article proposes a dark ecology approach to address the ecological crisis compounded by hydropower dams in Sikkim. It argues that the Teesta flash floods magnified the visibility of the ecological mesh, i.e. a network of ecological beings or ‘strange strangers’ composed of human and non-human forms. The flash floods appeared like a strange stranger and problematised riverine communities, state and non-state agencies on multiple scales. The proliferation of hydropower dams and the 2023 flash floods have resulted in an ecological rupture that corresponds to ecognosis, a riddle to the local community, experienced through the changing climate, thereby resulting in changing traditional agrarian patterns. I conclude with the forgotten narrative of the 1968 flash floods and landslide in the region and ruminate whether the end of nature might be closer than expected, unless alternative plural indigenous ecological knowledge systems are acknowledged and practised on a planetary scale.

  3. Bridging the Worlds: Exploring Dialogues, Differences and Perceptions About ‘Others’ in the Upaniṣadic Tradition with a Spotlight on Brahman
    Rohit Kumar · pp. 901-918 · online 2026-07-07
    摘要

    India has a long-standing tradition of engaging with diversity through public discourse and intellectual debates. This openness has been upheld by strong arguments that celebrate variation and emphasise mutual respect through dialogue. This paper explores this dimension of early Indian literary tradition by analysing three significant dialogues, particularly with their pūrvapakṣa–uttarapakṣa dimension, from distinct Upaniṣadic texts: the dialogue between Gārgya Bālāki and King Ajātaśatru in the Bṛhadāraṇyaka Upaniṣad; the interaction between Indra, Virocana and Prajāpati in the Chāndogya Upaniṣad; and the discourse involving Prajāpati devas and a yakṣa in the Kena Upaniṣad. The first two dialogues highlight differences, particularly in terms of philosophical traditions and social identities, while illustrating how these encounters transcend rigid boundaries without reaching definitive conclusions. The third dialogue sheds light on the accommodative spirit underlying these discussions. This paper argues that debate, difference and the accommodation of diversity are enduring themes in early Indian literary traditions, reflecting a deeply rooted ethos of intellectual plurality.

  4. ‘Poetry Is a Flavourful Ball’: Sinhala Poetic Prescriptions in Munidasa Cumaratunga’s Kavi Śikṣāva ( Poetics ), 1939
    Garrett Field · pp. 919-940 · online 2026-07-12
    摘要

    In twentieth century Sri Lanka, one of the most influential philologists of Sinhala literature was Munidasa Cumaratunga (1887–1944). In 1939, he published Kavi Śikṣāva (Poetics) to explain how to appreciate and compose metred Sinhala poetry. But he did so through a fictional dialogue between a groom-to-be and his future bride. In this article, I argue that Cumaratunga wrote these dialogues to reformulate Sinhala poetics according to his prescriptions that both challenged and sustained ties with premodern poetic conventions. Cumaratunga’s poetic prescriptions offer scholars of South Asian literary cultures a window into an understudied aesthetic paradigm that flourished prior to the emergence of Sinhala free verse in the 1950s. It was a literary sensibility that celebrated recited poetry that engaged listeners in a challenging but pleasurable task to decode double meanings.

  5. Ezhava Reform and Hindu Sanghadana in 1930s–40s Travancore
    Mukulika Radhakrishnan · pp. 941-961 · online 2026-07-07
    摘要

    This article examines the intersections between subaltern anti-caste reform and ‘Hindu unity’ in early twentieth century South India, focusing on the princely state of Travancore. Centring the Ezhava reform movement and drawing on underexplored archival sources, the article argues that while some Ezhava elites resisted conservative assimilation, others pursued it, engaging with organisations such as the Travancore Hindu Mahasabha to negotiate status and social mobility. The article introduces a neglected archival document suggesting the existence of a Sree Narayana Dharma Samrakshana Yogam, distinct from the well-known SNDP Yogam, pointing to a more complex range of engagements with Hindu consolidationist projects. Situating these developments within wider anxieties over Christian conversions, princely state politics, and the growing influence of national Hindu organisations in the 1930s and 1940s, the article argues that Ezhava reform encompassed greater ideological heterogeneity than previously acknowledged.

  6. Laughing at Brahmanical Orthodoxy: Dube ji ki Chitthi and the Satirical Hindi Public Letter in Colonial India
    Charu Gupta · pp. 962-983 · online 2026-05-26
    摘要

    This article examines Dube ji ki Chitthi (1924–44), a satirical epistolary column by Vishwambharnath Sharma ‘Kaushik’, to explore the political power of vernacular humour in colonial Hindi print culture. Written in the guise of a rotund, bhang-loving Brahman, Dube ji’s public letters deployed conversational rhythm, earthy idioms and performative satire to critique the rigidities of Sanatani Hinduism and Brahmanical pretensions. These letters blurred the boundaries between entertainment and dissent, offering a form of civic pedagogy rooted in folk logic and cultural embeddedness. At once conservative and progressive, complicit and critical, they reflected the ambivalences of inter-War Hindi literati and their limited radicalism. This essay argues that Kaushik’s use of satire forged a vernacular ethics of dissent, where laughter became a mode of survival and critique within an increasingly censored and polarised public sphere.

  7. Debating Islam, Nation and Belonging: Nigār Magazine and Muslim Publics in Late Colonial India
    Aaqib Javid · pp. 984-1004 · online 2026-06-05
    摘要

  8. ‘Ours Is a Species Adapted to Life on Water’: Land, Livelihood and the Peculiar Geography of Kuttanad, Kerala
    Mohit Mandal · pp. 1005-1025 · online 2026-06-16
    摘要

    Through a historical and ethnographic approach grounded in the rice-growing, low-lying region of Kuttanad in Kerala, this article traces threads of continuity between reclamation projects initiated in the British colonial period and the postcolonial construction of the Thanneermukkom bund in 1974. I argue that ideologies of agrarian settlement and technocratic intervention, set into motion under colonial capitalism, continue to confine the geography and ecology of Kuttanad’s water-saturated landscape, even as farmers and fisherfolk rely upon small-scale, collectivist techniques that emerge from the bottom up.   9. Reading the Representation of Queerness in the Pakistani Film Joyland (2022)
    Sourav Saha, Shyamkiran Kaur · pp. 1026-1047 · online 2026-07-01
    摘要

  10. Development Citizenship in the Age of Terror: The Pashtun Tahafuz Movement in Pakistan
    Naheem Jabbar, Usman Ali · pp. 1048-1069 · online 2026-06-22
    摘要

    Many lessons might be learnt about the borderland as a transnational place in the Global South in our era of paranoid hyper-nationalism.   11. Exploring Gift Exchange Practices in Swat State (1917–69): Insights from the Era of the Walis
    Jalal Uddin · pp. 1070-1090 · online 2026-06-22
    摘要

    This study examines the practices of gifting in the princely state of Swat under the rule of Miangul Abdul Wadud (1917–49) and his successor, Miangul Abdul Haq Jahanzeb (1949–69), highlighting the interplay between personal generosity, political strategy and cultural patronage. Gifts, fines, allowances and material rewards functioned not merely as symbolic gestures of benevolence but as deliberate instruments for cultivating loyalty, securing alliances and reinforcing hierarchical structures within the governance framework. Regular stipends operated as structured quasi-gifts, consolidating authority both within the state and in relations with external actors. Beyond political utility, the Walis’ gifting practices encompassed cultural, educational and recreational domains, including the distribution of books, patronage of literary gatherings and the sharing of hunted game. This study demonstrates that in Swat state, gifting was integral to governance, operating at the intersection of authority, diplomacy and social obligation, thereby illuminating the broader significance of material culture.

  12. ‘We Do Not Want a Farmer Husband’: Analysing the Marriage Drought in Rural Western India
    Akshay Sawant · pp. 1091-1109 · online 2026-07-07
    摘要

    Based on a year’s ethnographic fieldwork in a village in the drought-prone Beed district of Maharashtra, this article explores the emerging ‘marriage drought’ phenomenon among Marathas, a dominant landholding caste.     Sriti Ganguly · pp. 1110-1128 · online 2026-07-12

  1. Pogson’s Tomb: Three Narratives of Life and Death in Banaras
    Michael S. Dodson · pp. 1-21 · online 2026-10-25
    摘要

    This essay examines the life of Lieutenant-Colonel W.R. Pogson (1787–1843), a middle-ranking officer in the East India Company’s army, as well as the socio-cultural significance of his death in contemporary Banaras (Varanasi). Pogson was also a noted orientalist who wrote at length about India’s history and military cultures. His monumental grave, located in a poor neighbourhood of Banaras, now acts as a shrine for local residents, and they consider Pogson himself to be a wish-granting spirit. The tomb thus serves as an alternative religious space in this largely Hindu city. Three different versions of Pogson’s life and death are recounted here as a way of illustrating the different meanings one can ascribe to Pogson’s historical and contemporary significance. This essay also argues that Pogson’s continued relevance to the life of Banaras’ poor highlights the failures of recent attempts to rebrand the city as an aspirational modern Hindu space.

  2. Visual Grammar of Viraha in the Paintings of Select Partition Artists
    Kamayani Kumar · pp. 1-20 · online 2026-10-25
    摘要

    Decades have passed, yet South Asia continues to grapple with cartographic anxieties invoked by the Partition of the Indian subcontinent. This paper addresses Partition as an unresolved cultural trauma, examining how its post-memories have been transgenerationally transmitted. Partition translated into a violent ‘unhoming’ for millions and, in the process, caused a deep sense of viraha—longing for the unruptured past. Art and literature from the subcontinent have represented this viraha—as a persistent longing for the lost home and culture. This paper, with its focus on the work of artists such as S.L. Parasher, Satish Gujral, Zarina Hashmi and Krupa Makhija, explores how artists have represented the memories, post memories and prosthetic memories of Partition trauma. In the process of collective mourning, art serves as a tool for ‘aesthetic wit(h)nessing’.Footnote1 While official historiography has provides ‘systemic’ truths about Partition, artistic discourses translate the profound sense of loss of home.

  4. Shocked But Not Awed: Manipulation and Counter-Manipulation in the 2024 Pakistan Elections
    Hassan Mahmood, Syed Taha Ali · pp. 1-20 · online 2026-10-25
    摘要

    This paper attempts to increase our understanding of the micro-dynamics of the election rigging process in hybrid regimes by presenting a detailed case study of the 2024 general elections in Pakistan. It focuses not only on the ‘menu of manipulation’ employed by the Pakistani military and its collaborators, but the manner in which these interventions were combined and sequenced to yield what initially appeared to be a coherent and effective ‘shock and awe’ strategy, designed to completely beat the out-of-favour Pakistan Tehreek-e-Insaf and its supporters into submission even before the polls began. It describes the reasons why this strategy failed, including the clever use of technology by PTI supporters and the excessive harshness displayed by the military, which hurt rather than helped its cause, forcing it to take ‘corrective’ actions that not only undermined the legitimacy of the poll but the popularity of the military and its collaborators as well.

  5. In Murky Waters: Insights on Doing Feminist Research as Men in India
    Prakash Khundrakpam, Jayanta Krishna Sarmah · pp. 1-17 · online 2026-10-20
    摘要

    Recent feminist scholarship argues that men can and should conduct feminist research, guided by critical epistemological and political principles. Such participation is valued for producing knowledge grounded in strong objectivity in incorporating the male standpoint as another lens, while also enabling men to acknowledge the historico-moral responsibility tied to their gendered position, thereby becoming co-companions in knowledge production and feminist emancipation. In this context, drawing on ethnographic fieldwork in India and collective auto-ethnographic reflection, this article offers three insights. First, men’s resolve to engage in feminist research emerges when ingrained patriarchal mindsets are unsettled by education and, crucially, by lived interactions with women research subjects. Second, we examine the ethical dilemmas in such research, particularly access negotiated through a female associate, which raises questions of agency and epistemological ownership. Finally, we suggest strategies for disseminating findings beyond the field to further the feminist project.

  6. Populist Politics and Law Reform in Sri Lanka: Whither Legal Pluralism?
    Sepalika Welikala · pp. 1-19 · online 2026-10-13
    摘要

    This paper explores the complex interplay between Right-wing populism and law reform, and its impact on legal pluralism, analysed through the ‘One Country, One Law’ (OCOL) law reform campaign in Sri Lanka. Focusing on legal processes as embedded within socio-cultural and political contexts, and understanding law reform as a site where political ideology is articulated and mobilised to advance populist politics, this discussion examines the Right-wing legal mobilisation through the OCOL campaign, which challenges not just Muslim personal law but also the plural legal system of Sri Lanka. Although the reform campaign was abandoned, this paper demonstrates that the reform process was appropriated by Right-wing populist actors to promote a single unified legal system in line with the majoritarian political ideology and the marginalisation of minority communities. Such politicisation has a profound impact on society, particularly one that values diversity and pluralism.

  7. Beneath Khangchendzonga’s Shadow: Division, Belonging and Multiplicity in Lepcha Healing Traditions
    Melissa Zermit Namchu · pp. 1-16 · online 2026-10-05
    摘要

    This article examines indigenous Lepcha health practices within the historical and socio-political context of Máyel Lyáng. Critiquing the paucity of historical sources, it explores how colonialism, missionary encounters and Tibetanisation reshaped Lepcha healing traditions within the sacred and geographical landscape of Máyel Lyáng in the Eastern Himalayan region. These interventions not only altered the territorial integrity but also the epistemological foundations of traditional Lepcha healing, producing a diverse therapeutic field comprising ritual healers, herbalists, Buddhist monks, healers who integrate Christian prayers, bonesetters, and practitioners incorporating biomedical diagnostics. It argues that Lepcha healing traditions remain dynamic sites of negotiation, where resilience and adaptation sustain diverse therapeutic practices, contributing to anthropological debates on health, religion and cultural transformation in South Asia.

  8. A New Legal Order for India’s New Woman: Interrogating the Spectacle of Emancipation in Contemporary Courtroom Dramas of Consent
    Amalie Goul Dueholm · pp. 1-22 · online 2026-10-05
    摘要

    In the latter half of the 2010s, some Hindi courtroom dramas invoked the concept of ‘consent’ to adjudicate sexual violence cases. In these films, consent functions as a tool that ensures that the judiciary no longer relies on moralising judgements about women’s sexual history or their public behaviour to reach its verdicts. This paper draws on close analysis of two Hindi films, Pink and Section 375, to examine this emancipatory promise. By attending to the films’ framing of rape as a legal mystery that can be factually established, the paper asks how the rhetoric of consent shapes what gets recognised as sexual violence, and it draws on feminist legal scholarship to argue that while consent facilitates a powerful spectacle of a new legal order committed to gender justice on screen, it also demonstrates how legal fetishism risks disciplining female desire while foreclosing other possibilities for imagining justice.

  9. Virahi -Masculinity and the (Dis)Contents in Nation-Making: Devdas in Early Twentieth Century India
    Oindri Roy · pp. 1-22 · online 2026-09-29
    摘要

    The article unravels the interplay of viraha, masculinity and early twentieth century nationhood in India through Saratchandra’s Devdas (1917) and Pramathesh Barua’s Hindi Devdas (1936). The representations of male desire, situated in the liminal space of personal and political, foster the close, historicised readings of the texts. This provides the scope to examine the multifarious impingements of nationhood, often India’s pro-Hindu nation-making, in the formation and stylisation of viraha and virahi.   10. Mahadevi’s Viraha : Gender, Longing and the Nation
    Vandana Saxena · pp. 1-19 · online 2026-09-22
    摘要

    This paper explores the theme of viraha and the persona of the virahini in the writings of Mahadevi Varma, a prominent Hindi woman writer of the mid twentieth century. Varma’s poetry focuses on the persona of a virahini—a woman in love, solitary and ready to surrender herself to the intensity of her feelings for her lover. This emotional matrix has earned her the epithet of the ‘modern Mira’. A careful exploration of the indomitable self of Varma’s virahini not only marks her difference from the virahini of the Bhakti poets but also places the persona in the historical moment of India’s anti-colonial struggle, particularly Gandhian nationalism. This connects to Varma’s prose writings, which reflect upon various forms of longing and pain, focusing especially on feminine subjects in a colonised and patriarchal society. Here she records the stories of virahinis around her—the widows, child brides and abandoned women. In her essays and editorials, this pain and longing are transformed into logical and succinct arguments in favour of women and the need for social reform as a part of the movement for freedom. The article ends with deliberations on the persistent strains of viraha in Varma’s writing as the discourse of women and social reform is circumscribed by the benign paternalism of Gandhian thought that celebrates and limits the feminine subject within the folds of domesticity and tradition—the very limits that Varma’s virahini longs to overcome.

  11. The Romantic Nationalist: Aesthetics of Viraha in the Poetry of Ambikagiri Raichoudhury
    Dhurjjati Sarma · pp. 1-18 · online 2026-09-22
    摘要

    The early decades of the twentieth century signified a dual engagement within many Indian poets with the romantic-modernist influence acquired from the West on the one hand, and the patriotic and nationalist sentiments of the anti-colonial freedom struggle on the other. This paper will look into the poetry of Ambikagiri Raichoudhury, published in various collections like Tumi (1915), Beena (1916), Anubhuti (1954) and Bedonar Ulka (1964), in which he skilfully conflates his desire for freedom and liberty with an intimate appreciation of both sambhoga and viyoga as affective principles, enabling him to cultivate an in-depth and integrative spiritual-devotional sensibility. The paper aims to critically engage with this integrative impulse evident in selected poems of Raichoudhury, which reflect a modern refashioning of viraha a mystic–spiritual experience manifested in verses addressed to the beloved yet having deeper implications with the many-layered dynamics of identity and patriotism.

  12. The Re-Invention of the Virahiṇī : Image-Making of a De-Eroticised Nāyikā in Bengal Nationalism
    Gunjan · pp. 1-27 · online 2026-09-16
    摘要

    This paper examines the reconfiguration of the sensuous and spiritually charged image of the virahiṇī, tracing her transformation from early modern paintings of Rajasthan and the Pahari states to a morally idealised and hybridised figure painted by the artists of the Bengal School in the late nineteenth and early twentieth century. It begins with the visualisation of viraha (separation) experienced by the nāyikā (heroine) in early modern paintings based on texts such as Bhāgavata Purāṇa and Rasamañjarī, where separation is articulated as both an emotional and spiritual surrender.   13. Yearning and Its Limits: Viraha and the Gendered Script of Desire in Haryanvi Saang
    Suman Sigroha, Muskan Dhandhi · pp. 1-20 · online 2026-09-06
    摘要

    Saang/Swaang/Suaang or folk drama in Haryana, a state in North India, has been one of the most important forms of entertainment since the eighteenth century. This lesser-known and relatively understudied performative folk tradition comprises music, dance and drama, and its varied content is based on myths, legends and subjects of socio-cultural significance. The author-composer can narrate and simultaneously perform, and in the process enables the intervention and interplay of the socio-cultural landscape. This paper focuses on the textual analysis of two Saangs by Pandit Lakhmi Chand to examine how the male-authored and male-performed traditions project masculine anxieties through stylised depictions of female desire, betrayal and emotional excess.   14. Controlling Multiplicity: Hindu Mythologies in the Works of Amish Tripathi and Devdutt Pattanaik
    Uttara Rangarajan · pp. 1-22 · online 2026-09-06
    摘要

    This essay examines the emerging genre of mythological fiction through the works of two popular Indian authors, Amish Tripathi and Devdutt Pattanaik. Combining Hindu mythology with global speculative genres, mythological fiction instantiates new discursive spaces for the negotiation of Hindu identity and nationalism. Reading Tripathi’s Shiva Trilogy and Pattanaik’s Jaya and Sita, this essay argues that despite producing works that are formally and ideologically distinct, both authors position themselves as interlocutors who interpret Hinduism for common, middle-class citizens.   15. Songs of Separation in Bangladesh: Viraha , Affect and Anti-Colonial Imaginings in Early Twentieth Century Baul Literature and Folk Creative Industries
    Golam Rabbani · pp. 1-21 · online 2026-09-02
    摘要

    This article examines how early twentieth century Baul songs from regions of Bengal now in Bangladesh reworked the classical Indic concept of viraha—the pain of separation—into an affective force moving across devotional expression, folk creative industries and emergent nationalist feelings.   16. Between the State and Market: Subaltern Resilience in Entrepreneurship
    Avienaash E. Poovaragavan · pp. 1-14 · online 2026-08-30
    摘要

    While state support for marginalised entrepreneurship rests on its presumed emancipatory potential, little attention is paid to the exclusionary practices reproduced through state and market agents.   17. The Political Economy of the Rise and Fall of Brassware: A Case Study of Gadaladeniya, Sri Lanka
    Hasini Lecamwasam · pp. 1-18 · online 2026-08-30
    摘要

    Through the case of brassware producers in Gadaladeniya, Sri Lanka, I show that nationalism is not a tide that lifts all boats equally, even when some boats are supposed to be lifted so. Building upon qualitative fieldwork carried out intermittently from February to December 2023, I argue, first, that the complex effects of democratisation may have non-linear offshoots such as the disintegration of a traditionally marginalised caste community, rather than its empowerment; second, the social bases of state power may result in a single institutional apparatus yielding different benefits to different constituencies, depending on where the regime draws its electoral strength from, leading to fluctuations in community fortunes; and third, and last, customised methods of production that both enable traditional crafts to have a distinct presence in the market while also constraining their productive capacities further contribute to their unviability, compounding disintegrative tendencies.

  20. Beyond Uniformity: Bodily Gestures and the Resisting Gaze of Nachni s in Purulia
    Sudatta Ghosh · pp. 1-18 · online 2026-08-10
    摘要

    This paper critically examines the gestures and gaze used by nachnis to resist stigma and dominant regimes of uniformity. Through the case of the nachni, I reconceptualise bodily gestures as a site of labour and embodied resistance.

  21. The Rashtriya Swayamsevak Sangh’s ‘Struggle’ for Democracy: Hindu Nationalism, Propaganda and the Internal Emergency of India

  23. A ‘Swadeshi’ Star Is Born: Locating Visions of Nationalist and Consumerist Utopias in Bal Gandharva’s Acts of Female Impersonation
    Radhika Raghav · pp. 1-27 · online 2026-07-21
    摘要

    This article examines the star text of Bal Gandharva (1888–1967), the celebrated female impersonator of Marathi sangeet natak (musical theatre), as a critical site for the production and commodification of respectable Hindu femininity in colonial India. Through close readings of two iconic roles—Shakuntala in Sangeet Shakuntal and Sindhu in Ekach Pyala—it demonstrates how Gandharva’s performances advanced the tenets of stridharma as the foundational moral grammar of respectable middle-class femininity. Vol. 58, No. 3 · 2026-07-03 · 8 篇 · Routledge · Critical Asian studies · Peer-reviewed
  1. Ideology, Memory, and Silence: Thai Studies under Cold War Historiography in China
    Cui Feng · pp. 369-388 · online 2026-02-04
    摘要

    Between the 1950s and 1970s, the People’s Republic of China (PRC) maintained contact and cooperation with the Communist Party of Thailand (CPT) through multiple channels. The CPT received material support, political training, and ideological guidance from China, particularly from bases in Yunnan Province. Using the development of Thai Studies in China as a case study, this article examines how Chinese academia has engaged with—or avoided—the Cold War history of Thailand. Through a critical review of the field, it identifies structural tensions shaped by political sensitivity and institutional constraints, situating these dynamics within the broader context of Southeast Asian studies. Such constraints influence topic selection, access to sources, and publication practices, resulting in a field characterized by narrowed perspectives, uneven critical engagement, and structurally constrained academic autonomy. In this sense, Thai Studies in China reflects a paradoxical trajectory in which the field has been repeatedly shaped, disrupted, and revived by shifting political imperatives rather than by autonomous scholarly inquiry.

  2. Learning in (Re)Production: Spare-time Learning and the Reproduction of Gendered Labor in Socialist China
    Changwen Chen · pp. 389-414 · online 2026-03-15
    摘要

    This article examines spare-time learning (yèyú xuéxí) in socialist China as a form of labor-mediated pedagogical governance that expanded women’s access to education while simultaneously reproducing gendered divisions of labor. Drawing on archival materials from Shaanxi Province, I trace how spare-time learning was organized across urban industrial and rural agricultural settings, showing that it constituted an ambitious effort to democratize intellectual authority by locating knowledge production within collective labor practice. Yet the capacity of spare-time learning to serve as an equalizing institution was unevenly structured across institutional contexts. I introduce the concept of a “gendered mobility loop” to specify the mechanisms through which the gendered division of labor was continually reconstituted within socialist pedagogy. By tracing shifts from early literacy campaigns to increasingly moralized political training, I argue that women’s double burden was not merely a residual legacy of tradition or an effect of incomplete implementation but was continually reconstituted through pedagogical arrangements embedded in production regimes. Reconceptualizing spare-time learning as a site of ideological reproduction embedded in production, this article contributes to feminist scholarship on social reproduction under socialism and to Marxist cultural studies of the education–labor nexus.

  3. Hidden Smoke: Air Pollution, Agrarian Change, and Governance Gaps in Luang Prabang, Laos
    Danny Marks, Ian G. Baird · pp. 415-437 · online 2026-04-21
    摘要

    Air pollution has emerged as a major public health and economic threat in Southeast Asia, yet Laos has received limited attention in scholarly and policy debates despite significant mortality and economic losses. This article analyses the drivers, lived experiences, and governance of air pollution in Luang Prabang, Laos’ premier tourist destination and a UNESCO World Heritage city. Drawing on semi-structured interviews, field observations, and satellite data, our findings challenge dominant policy narratives that attribute haze primarily to traditional swidden cultivation. Instead, commercial agrarian change, particularly cassava expansion, grass cultivation, and cattle pasture expansion linked to regional and Chinese cattle demand and a prolonged urban landfill fire, drove air pollution in 2023. These sources stem from labor shortages, economic insecurity, poor urban waste planning, and a reliance on fire as a low-cost management tool. Despite significant health, livelihood, and tourism impacts, haze remains underappreciated and underfunded. Weak monitoring infrastructure, fragmented institutional responsibilities, and the absence of disaggregated health data render air pollution uncertain and socially normalized. The article argues that air pollution in Luang Prabang represents a form of slow violence, maintained by epistemic gaps and administrative silence within a development model that prioritizes expanded agriculture and tourism over environmental protection.

  4. Telling What was not Told: Acehnese Women’s Narratives of Conflict and the Tsunami
    Alfi Rahman, Rizanna Rosemary, Safrina, Norma Susanti, Syahrul Ridha, Siti Ghaisani Mastura et al. · pp. 438-458 · online 2026-04-24
    摘要

    This study investigates how Acehnese women experience, remember, and transmit narratives about prolonged armed conflict and the 2004 Indian Ocean tsunami. In-depth interviews were conducted with women across two generational categories: those who had directly experienced these events (experiential narrators) and those who have learned the stories through familial or community transmission (post-memory narrators). The study situates narratives as a social practice, exploring how women’s experiences, survival, and resilience are interpreted, negotiated, and passed on. The study reveals a striking divergence in how these two crises are remembered and narrated. The tsunami, often seen as a divine test, has been spiritualized, moralized, and publicly commemorated through museums, school drills, and religious discourse. This structured and didactic form of storytelling fosters intergenerational compassion and preparedness. In contrast, conflict memories are fraught with moral complexity, silence, and fear. These narratives, often fragmented, emotionally restrained, and selectively told, have been shaped by past surveillance, suspicion, and unresolved trauma. The study underscores the importance of integrating women-centered approaches into post-disaster and post-conflict recovery. It also shows how the recognition and protection of women’s voices is vital to understanding long-term resilience, collective memory, and processes of social repair.

  5. Technocratic Turn: Authoritarianism, Global Capitalism, and Cambodia’s State Transformation since 2013
    Kimhean Hok · pp. 459-479 · online 2026-04-15
    摘要

    Scholars have characterized Cambodia as a weak state, strong party governance system in which administrative institutions are subordinated to the ruling Cambodian People’s Party (CPP). This article argues that since 2013 Cambodian governance has taken a significant technocratic turn, as party rule has become increasingly dependent on a stronger and more capable administrative state. It traces a reform pattern involving selective creation and upgrading of institutional nodes that expand overall state administrative capacity without disrupting the party-political order. Through three cases — the transformation of the National Bank of Cambodia through the Bakong digital payment system, the restructuring of public–private partnership frameworks to fund the Techo International Airport, and the creation of executive-linked agencies such as the Techo Startup Center — the article shows how new centers of administrative capacity have emerged and been positioned at the core of the state’s developmental agenda. These institutions have enhanced the party-state’s capacity to mobilize revenue, coordinate and develop mega infrastructures, and formalize the economy, producing a more capable but still party-embedded state. The Cambodian case illustrates a particular trajectory within broader contemporary developmental state-making under global capitalism, in which strengthened administrative states and political authoritarianism are mutually reinforcing.

  6. The Making of an Indian Company Town: Manufactured Cosmopolitanism, Communal Violence, and Urban Memory in Jamshedpur, India
    Isha Dubey · pp. 480-504 · online 2026-06-13
    摘要

    In 1907, after months of prospecting across India, the little-known and remote hamlet of Sakchi in Singhbhum was chosen by Jamshetji Nusserwanji Tata (1839-1904), founder of the Tata Group, as the location for India’s first indigenously owned iron and steel works. This paper examines the construction of an institutional cultural memory of what would become the planned city of Jamshedpur as an integral part of India’s industrial heritage, steeped in ideas of progress, modernity and cosmopolitanism. The article juxtaposes this cultural memory with the lived experiences and memories of Jamshedpur inhabitants who have witnessed its transformation in response to and beyond the institutional narrative of the ideal steel city. Finally, the article discusses the immediate and long-term impacts of communal riots in 1964 and 1979 and the ways in which this violence is remembered or forgotten. In doing so, the article argues that public and private histories and memories of Jamshedpur intersect, overlap, and negotiate with each other in ways that complicate and contradict a carefully cultivated discourse of model industrial urbanism that has been erected on a foundation of manufactured cosmopolitanism.

  7. Reproducing Patriarchy Through Women’s Proxy Representation in Local Governments: An Ethnographic Perspective from Rural Punjab, Pakistan
    Nadeem Malik · pp. 505-528 · online 2026-04-28
    摘要

    This article examines how Pakistan's local government gender quota policy, aimed at improving women's political representation, has been undermined by entrenched patriarchal structures in rural Punjab. Based on longitudinal ethnographic research, including fieldwork conducted in 2005-2006 and 2019, with follow-up observations in 2024, the study highlights the problem of proxy representation. In this scenario, elected women often act as figurehead councilors while their male relatives wield actual decision-making power. The analysis indicates that the apparent increase in female representation often amounts to mere symbolic inclusion, offering little genuine empowerment for women. By drawing on feminist theories of representation and agency, along with concepts of patriarchal bargains and a governmentality perspective, the article argues that state-led “empowerment” reforms can be co-opted and reshaped by local kinship norms concerning honor and authority. In practice, rather than fundamentally disrupting male dominance, Pakistan’s gender quota has largely been transformed into a new form of patriarchy—one that maintains male control while creating a façade of female representation. These findings contribute to discussions on feminist governance by illustrating that efforts to democratize gender relations must confront deeply entrenched social structures.

  8. Seeing Like a Colonial State: Post-colonial Dossierism, Patriarchal Governance, and Indigenous Status Recognition in Taiwan
    Unang Uday · pp. 529-551 · online 2026-05-18
    摘要

    When I applied for Truku Indigenous status in 2022, I discovered that my mother had never been officially registered as Indigenous in Taiwan and my application was therefore denied. Only through an arduous bureaucratic process was I able to obtain state recognition of my Truku Indigenous identity. This experience led me to ask what epistemic and ideological foundations structure Taiwan’s Indigenous Peoples Status Act (IPSA). Drawing on narrative inquiry and combining autoethnographic reflection with legal and policy analysis, this article identifies two ideologies underlying the Act: post-colonial dossierism and patriarchal governance. Taiwan’s recognition regime privileges colonial archives over Indigenous self-determination while operating through patriarchal norms of descent and identity transmission. This study contributes to the literature on postcolonialism in two ways. First, it identifies dossierism and patriarchal governance as mechanisms through which colonial epistemology is reproduced in law. Second, it shows that once colonially produced documents are codified as mandatory state law, it legitimized the colonial way of seeing and, in turn, shapes how colonized subjects come to understand themselves.

Online first（尚未編入期別）· 11
  1. Indigenous Communities and Development: The Mising People and Dam Construction in Assam, India
    Pranab Kumar Pegu, Vasundhara Jairath · pp. 1-19 · online 2026-10-17
    摘要

    Much of the early literature on dams and Indigenous peoples focused on resistance and organized opposition to the projects, with an emphasis on threats to Indigenous life ways, sustainability, and their roles as custodians of nature. However, more recent scholarship highlights the complex ways in which affected populations respond to such projects. This article examines how Indigenous people in one community in Northeast India engage with a development project that dispossesses them, provoking opposition to the project, while also offering opportunities to enter the market economy and earn higher incomes. It discusses the case of the Subansiri Lower Hydro Electric Project (SLHEP) in Assam. The article argues that what appears to be a contradiction in how people respond to this project reflects a more complex response to the set of changes brought about by a growing neoliberal order and the place that Indigenous working people find themselves in. This is marked by the rapid erosion of the material life worlds of Indigenous peoples and a growing dependence on market interactions for meeting most basic needs, alongside growing aspirations and the precarity brought on by a crisis of social reproduction.

  2. Under Construction: Frontier Infrastructure in Northeast India
    Debasish Hazarika · pp. 1-21 · online 2026-10-17
    摘要

    This article investigates encounters between infrastructure projects and Indigenous communities in Northeast India. Focusing on two projects by the National Hydroelectric Power Corporation in the Dollungmukh region of Arunachal Pradesh, namely the Subansiri Lower Hydroelectric Project (SLHEP) and a Nyder Namlo (prayer hall), it shows how a large-scale, long-drawn-out construction process has become the turf for intense encounters between local communities, the state, and infrastructural technologies. Projects such as these function as a form of infrastructural optics, through which the state materially and discursively manifests itself and thereby constantly reaffirms and adjusts the material and discursive scaffolding of this frontier region. Based on ethnographic fieldwork, the article demonstrates how the construction of the SLHEP, one of India’s largest dams, has reshaped the local political economy, aspirations, and social relations. The article also highlights how the Nyder Namlo serves as a critical convergence point where state-making and efforts to undergird the collective life of local communities blend through the construction regime across multiple material and discursive realms.

  3. Governing Social Change in Amdo: Deficit Ideologies, Educational Desire, and the Depoliticization of Tibetan Education
    Stuart J. Wright · pp. 1-27 · online 2026-09-26
    摘要

    Tibetan education in the People’s Republic of China (PRC) has received international attention and criticism in recent years as an issue of colonial boarding schools that aim to assimilate young Tibetans. The proliferation of state-run boarding schools was the outcome of a central government policy of school consolidation, presented as a new panacea for the problems of rural education, and which resulted in the closure of hundreds of thousands of schools across the PRC. Drawing on ethnographic fieldwork in 2012–2013 and 2024 in parts of the Amdo region of Tibet (now part of Qinghai and Gansu Provinces), this article examines how education policies and practices are mobilized discursively as technical, practical, and non-political, while simultaneously extending state power into the everyday lives of rural Tibetans with cross-generational disciplinary effects. Contestation has been mitigated by existing and new forms of educational desire, while education has been “depoliticized” by quality discourses and deficit ideologies – including by some Tibetan educators – in the context of rapid socio-economic change and precarity.

  9. Green Star over Thailand: Esperanto and Leftist Internationalism in the Thai Chinese Community during the 1930s
    Peng Ai · pp. 1-27 · online 2026-09-07
    摘要

    This article examines the Esperanto movement within the Thai Chinese community during the 1930s as a prism through which to explore the early formation of leftist internationalism in Thailand. Drawing on a diverse corpus of primary sources, this study reconstructs how a small but committed group of intellectuals mobilized Esperanto as both a practical tool for transnational communication and a symbolic vehicle for articulating a vision of leftist internationalism grounded in the specific realities of China’s anti-Japanese resistance. The article argues that the Esperanto movement was a transnational leftist cultural network that mediated the interplay between internationalism, nationalism, and communism—three forces that collectively shaped the political consciousness of Thai Chinese. The movement’s trajectory reveals both the possibilities and vulnerabilities of such transnational projects. While ultimately crushed by the rise of Thai state nationalism under the Phibun Songkhram regime, the movement’s radical ideas and networks survived through clandestine channels. By tracing how Esperantists adapted internationalist discourse to serve China’s national salvation, this article illuminates a neglected chapter in Thai radicalism and contributes to broader efforts to decenter Eurocentric narratives of leftist internationalism by attending to the experiences of Southeast Asian diasporic communities.

  10. Fear Politics and the Construction of Political Enemies: The Democratic Action Party and Malay-Islamic Narratives in Post-2018 Malaysia
    Mohamad Muzammil · pp. 1-26 · online 2026-08-10
    摘要

    This article examines how Malaysia’s Democratic Action Party (DAP) has been discursively constructed as a political adversary by rival Malay-Muslim political parties and nationalist actors in post-2018 Malaysia. Moving beyond static narratives of ethnic politics, it argues that portrayals of the DAP as a threat to Malay-Muslim interests do not reflect a fixed ideological order, but rather emerge from ongoing struggles over meaning, legitimacy, and political power. Drawing on a qualitative analysis of political speeches, media narratives, and digital discourse between 2018 and 2025, the article demonstrates how fear operates as a central mechanism in shaping political perceptions and mobilizing support. Rather than indicating a unified Malay response, these narratives are received unevenly across class, generational, and ideological lines, thereby revealing significant internal contestations. By focusing on political discourse, the article examines how political fear operates in practice. It contends that contemporary Malay-centric party politics are more accurately understood through a competitive and fragmented field of competing narratives.

  11. Lee Siew Choh and His Discontents: Maoism and the Factionalization of Singapore’s Progressive Left after Operation Coldstore
    Chien-Wen Kung · pp. 1-24 · online 2026-07-23
    摘要

    This article explores how Maoism shaped, and was shaped by, the factionalization of Singapore’s progressive left and its flagship political party, the Barisan Sosialis (Socialist Front) after Operation Coldstore on February 2, 1963. Focusing especially on the Cultural Revolution period, it traces the emergence of Maoism in the city-state to texts and ideas from the People’s Republic of China that circulated widely within left-wing circles in Singapore starting in the 1950s, as well as to the three years after Coldstore, when Maoism came to dominate public leftist discourse and practice in Singapore. The article intervenes in the historiography of global Maoism by triangulating between the work of orthodox scholars such as Yinghong Cheng and recent, left-historical interpretations by the likes of Matthew Galway and Fabio Lanza. By focusing on the increasingly extremist Barisan and its Maoist critics such as the student activist Li Xiquan, it shows, contrary to orthodox analyses, that Maoism in Singapore cannot be reduced to a purely foreign ideology, and was, rather, underpinned by the Barisan and its critics’ shared commitment to Malaya as an anticolonial project and adaptation of Maoism to fit local circumstances. Maoists were likewise capable of critical self-reflection, as left-historical scholarship contends and the example of Li Xiquan suggests. Yet, such scholarship fails to capture how fanaticism, mimicry, and emotion were constitutive of Maoist experiences in Singapore, and indeed how ideological commitments evolved over time in ways that underscore these aspects of Singapore Maoism.

Pacific Affairs

Vol. 99, No. 3 · 2026-07-03 · 48 篇 · U British Columbia · Asia & Pacific · Peer-reviewed
  1. Sacralizing, Materializing, Normalizing China: Monarchy, Merchants, and Media in Contemporary Thailand
    Chaiyaporn Phayakhrut, Ying-Kit Chan · pp. 401-423 · online 2026-08-14
    摘要

    Thailand’s relations with China have expanded in recent decades through diplomacy, cultural exchange, and economic integration. Scholars have explained such expansion through economic interdependence and geopolitical alignment. This article suggests that the durability of Thailand-China relations also depends on mechanisms of social legitimation operating in Thai society; we conceptualize these mechanisms as a monarchy-merchant-media (3M) nexus. Royal diplomacy sacralizes bilateral ties through symbolic authority, corporate networks materialize them through investment and commercial integration, and popular media normalizes them through cultural representation and everyday consumption. Rather than operating sequentially, these forces interact to embed China’s presence in Thailand’s cultural landscape and political economy. While tensions surrounding Chinese influence periodically emerge in Thailand, the configuration of symbolic authority, capital networks, and cultural narratives helps stabilize bilateral relations. By highlighting these processes, the article contributes to debates on how asymmetrical regional power can be reproduced not only through state coercion but also through socially embedded forms of legitimacy.

  2. Differentiated Alliance Management: The Generative Logic of Institutional Divergence in “US-Japan+” Trilateral Security Cooperation
    Xingchuan Jiang · pp. 424-451 · online 2026-08-26
    摘要

    Since 2022, three trilateral mechanisms anchored on the US-Japan alliance have advanced in parallel: US-Japan-Australia, US-Japan-Republic of Korea (ROK), and US-Japan-Philippines. Yet they have diverged sharply across four dimensions: organization, process, capability, and network. The three share the same US-Japan axis and converge on the China challenge, so one might expect their institutional forms to converge as well. They have not. This divergence is the puzzle the article addresses. To explain it, the article proposes “differentiated alliance management.” Washington and Tokyo, the argument runs, do not apply a uniform template. They adjust commitment form, organization, capability integration, and network connectivity to fit each third party’s domestic-political, capability, and diplomatic-risk constraints. Process tracing across the three cases shows that the same axis, filtered through different constraints, produces three stable but distinct forms: a non-alliance yet steadily deepening high-level integration with Australia, a legally non-binding political commitment with the ROK, and a selectively activatable, capability-building arrangement with the Philippines. It argues that Washington and Tokyo have not applied a uniform template when building trilateral cooperation with third parties; rather, they have calibrated the institutional configuration of each mechanism according to the domestic political, capability-structural, and diplomatic-risk constraints that each partner faces.

  3. Beyond Hedging: A Dynamic Model of Equidistant Diplomacy in Indonesia
    Shuqi Wang · pp. 452-472 · online 2026-08-11
    摘要

    Research on middle-power strategy often subsumes diverse practices under hedging, obscuring how governments operationalize neutrality over time. Equidistant diplomacy specifies three perception-management tools—paralleling, downplaying, and broadcasting—but existing accounts treat them as a static menu and say less about when and why states reweight them. This article reconceptualizes equidistant diplomacy as a stage-based process consisting of three core phases—initiation, navigation, and recalibration—and one cross-cutting mode of episodic adjustment triggered by shocks. It argues that tool choice and phase movement reflect the interaction between external pressures and domestic political factors, especially leadership change and electoral incentives. The framework is applied to Indonesia’s management of US–China rivalry from 2014 to 2025. Under Joko Widodo, Indonesia established a neutral baseline through the Global Maritime Fulcrum and symbolic paralleling, then institutionalized equidistance by pairing deeper economic engagement with China with diversification toward the United States and its partners. Natuna maritime frictions, the 2019 election, and COVID-19 prompted adjustment episodes dominated by downplaying and defensive broadcasting. Prabowo Subianto’s early diplomacy is interpreted as recalibration, combining paired high-level diplomacy, new security dialogues, and intensified broadcasting to reset neutrality in a more assertive form. The article adds temporal structure and mechanism-based specificity to equidistant diplomacy, clarifying an operational question often left underspecified in hedging: how sequenced, domain-specific signalling can help preserve autonomy while managing perceptions of alignment as great power competition intensifies.

  4. Institutional Decay and Electoral Upheaval: Party System Fragility and Nepal’s 2026 Political Transformation
    Suvexa Pradhan Tuladhar · pp. 473-486 · online 2026-06-22
    摘要

    When the 2025 Gen Z movement toppled the incumbent government and dissolved the House of Representatives, Nepal’s democracy appeared on the brink. Yet six months later Nepal successfully conducted a midterm election. The result was a near supermajority for four-year-old Rastriya Swatantriya Party (RSP), which secured 182 of 275 parliamentary seats, delivering a historic defeat to Nepal’s long-dominant establishment parties. Prevailing explanations attribute these outcomes to the Gen Z movement and the populist appeal of RSP’s prime ministerial candidate, Balendra Shah. While these factors contributed to the transformation, they do not fully explain it. Drawing on Mainwaring and Scully’s Party System Institutionalization theory, I contend that both the protest movement and RSP’s breakthrough are best understood as the culmination of a structural party system crisis decades in the making. Analysis of electoral volatility data, public trust surveys, and party organizational patterns reveals a system characterized by unstable competition, eroding party-voter linkages, persistent legitimacy deficits, and weak internal party democracy. Yet RSP’s rise reproduces the same personality-driven, weakly institutionalized pattern it sought to dismantle. Its durability will depend on whether it can achieve what Nepal’s party system has historically failed to deliver: institutionalized, programmatically grounded, and organizationally coherent party politics.

  5. Neither Democratic Breakthrough nor Conservative Consolidation: Thailand’s 2026 General Election in a Contested Political Order
    Prajak Kongkirati · pp. 487-498 · online 2026-08-05
    摘要

    Thailand’s general election of February 8, 2026 produced a fragmented outcome that did not satisfy expectations for either decisive reform or for outright conservative restoration. Bhumjaithai (BJT) emerged as the largest party with 192 seats, yet its victory rested not on ideological appeal so much as on an extraordinary convergence of provincial patronage networks, bureaucratic resources, and elite endorsement. This article analyzes the principal dynamics at play in the election, examining BJT’s rise, the continued decline of military-backed parties, and the setbacks suffered by both the reformist People’s Party and the Thaksin-aligned Pheu Thai. Drawing on electoral data and qualitative evidence, it argues that Thai electoral politics remains more competitive and institutionally complex than conventional accounts suggest, even as informal power structures continue to shape its outcomes.

  6. “One Step Forward, Two Steps Back”: Unpacking Pakistan’s Post-9/11 Policies of Madrassah Reforms
    Abdur Rehman Shah · pp. 499-510 · online 2026-06-02
    摘要

    Despite numerous initiatives by the Pakistani government to reform religious seminaries, or madrassahs, results have been constrained by institutional resistance and flawed state policies. Drawing on madrassah literature, as well as interviews with government officials, madrassah administrators, and analysts, this article offers a fresh perspective on post-9/11 reform efforts. It argues that the reform process has been hampered by policy inconsistency, lack of a compelling narrative, and counterproductive securitization of a complex socioeconomic issue. This article advocates for a transition away from the current situation of ambitious, holistic strategies towards a more nuanced, long-term approach focused on incremental goals. The article argues that in addition to adopting this strategy of refinement, the state must also desecuritize the agenda of madrassah reform. This shift from reactive securitization to a consistent and comprehensive agenda offers a more viable path for reform in Pakistani madrassahs.
