Modern Asian Studies

Vol. 60, No. 2 · 2026-03 · 12 篇 · Cambridge UP · Asian history & society · Peer-reviewed (double-blind verified)
  1. Political notes of nauras : Emergence of an aesthetically embodied kingship practice in Adil Shahi Bijapur
    Namrata B. Kanchan · pp. 263-289 · online 2026-07-17
    摘要

    From late sixteenth century, Bijapur’s sultan, Ibrahim Adil Shah II (r. 1580–1627 ce ), used nauras —a polyvalent term used extensively within the Sanskrit aesthetic realm—on his kingdom’s cultural artefacts as well as political and administrative insignia. An avid music lover, he is renowned for his Dakani work on music titled Kitāb-i Nauras ( circa 1580s). Portrayed by previous scholars as religiously unorthodox, Ibrahim Adil II and nauras are located chiefly within narratives of Bijapur’s political instability and the sultan’s indulgence in leisure over statecraft. Recent scholarship has challenged such notions but no study focuses on the reasons for nauras ’ crossover to the political domain or its appearance on state insignia. This article offers new insights by locating the sultan within a matrix of Indic, Turco-Mongol, and Islamicate sovereignty practices. In doing so, it argues that Ibrahim Adil II strategically employed the corporeal, religious, aesthetic, and polyvalent dimensions of nauras to forge a uniquely personalized embodied cosmic kingship practice to govern a multicultural populace. In particular, this article elucidates that the realms of art, aesthetics, and kingship were not mutually exclusive in the Indic or Islamicate worlds and that nauras was a rational manoeuvre to elevate the ruler above courtly factions and religious differences.

  2. Whose women and whose honour? A fragile imperial discourse on the Baluch-Punjab frontier, late nineteenth century
    Gagan Kumar · pp. 290-322 · online 2026-07-06
    摘要

    This article focuses on the Punjab-Baluch borderland region during the last decade of the nineteenth century. It shows how this region was a conflict zone, not only between local communities across borders but also between the two colonial frontier governments of Punjab and Baluchistan. The article explores the nature of these conflicts by examining a cross-border dispute involving women. It analyses how different competing legal systems—Shariat, customs, and colonial law—operated on the ground and were used by the disputants to resolve the conflict. Additionally, it discusses how, during this process, the colonial state documented women’s presence and used their voices and actions to serve the objectives of state-making. Through this case study, the article demonstrates that in local feuds involving women, colonial authorities emerged chiefly as disputants rather than just arbitrators. By claiming to be the legitimate custodians of the ‘abducted’ women, the two frontier governments competed against each other to fulfil the aspirations of the tribal communities they represented. This intra-tribal and intra-colonial dispute reveals deep fissures in colonial understanding of questions of state intervention, sovereignty, and colonial subjecthood—rendering the imperial project inconsistent, fragile, and contradictory in the Punjab-Baluch borderlands.

  3. Typhoid, milk, and the military: India’s first dairying revolution, 1880–1925
    David Arnold · pp. 323-346 · online 2026-09-04
    摘要

    Government alarm over the extent of enteric fever (typhoid) among British troops in India in the late nineteenth century focused concern on adulterated milk as a likely medium for the transmission of disease and incentivized the creation of cantonment dairies, of which there were more than 30 by 1914. Aided by the introduction of modern milking techniques and butter-making equipment, military farms and government-sponsored dairies helped create a new market for dairy products in India, fostered the growth of privately run dairies, and encouraged the idea of milk consumption as an aid to health. The new dairies, with their intensive cattle-breeding programmes, were further seen as model institutions and centres for the dissemination of scientific dairying across India. The cantonment dairies drew in part on India’s own cattle culture and anticipated the ‘white revolution’ of the 1970s; but the uniqueness of the cantonment environment, the high costs of intensive dairying, and the reliance on specialized breeding inhibited the wider application of the military dairy model while intensifying Hindu opposition to cantonments as cow-killing sites.

  4. Borderland extraterritoriality: The Yunnan-Burma frontier meetings (1902–1949)
    Li Wan · pp. 347-372 · online 2026-09-22
    摘要

    Britain exercised a distinctive form of extraterritorial jurisdiction around the Yunnan-Burma border before 1949. In 1902, Chinese and British officials established a joint trial system under the 1902 Manai Agreement. From then on, Chinese, British, and Burmese officials convened temporary frontier meetings each winter in border areas, including in Manai, Nawngma, and Sima. These meetings adjudicated disputes involving theft, injuries, and trans-frontier cultivation pursuant to tribal customary law. The frontier meetings normally issued four distinct types of judgments and awarded a uniform remedy in the form of damages. This system served the practical needs of border tribes by delivering swift justice to prevent blood feuds and maintain law and order. These meetings developed a case law for resolving borderland cases that diverged from the national laws of both China and Britain. This system was jointly administered by Chinese, British, and Burmese officials under ostensibly reciprocal terms, though Britain’s exercise of extraterritorial jurisdiction in China ultimately placed a heavier burden on the Chinese side. The joint trial system came to an end after Burma officially gained independence in 1948. British extraterritorial jurisdiction in China was not absolute but operated through distinct jurisdictional systems in the interior and in the borderlands.

  5. The brokerage of order in Karachi
    Adeem Suhail · pp. 373-392 · online 2026-08-05
    摘要

    This article reframes Lyari’s gangs in Karachi as brokers of political order rather than merely criminal entities. Drawing on five years of ethnographic and archival research, it argues that the figure of the ‘gangster’ functioned as a political intermediary, stabilizing contradictions between military regimes, capital accumulation, and working-class urban life. Through portraits of three interlocutors—a tragic and unlikely ‘gangster’, a gang underboss who mediated between state projects and the denizens of his neighbourhood, and a socialist activist targeted for elimination by the gangs on behalf of the state—the article demonstrates how state, civil society, and scholarly discourses have unwittingly colluded to produce ‘Lyari-as-gangland’ without attending to what the gangs actually did in Lyari apart from the violence that overdetermines their era of dominance. It argues that coding brokerage as criminological masks the gang’s everyday functions in projects of public health, housing, and bureaucratic mediation. Furthermore, the rise of gangs to this role is historically situated in the conjuncture of the role of Pakistan’s military dictatorship in the global war on terror, where violent non-state actors were instrumentalized to produce a highly unequal order in the city.

  6. Waves of feeling: Emotions, media, and the Chinese Communist Revolution
    Dayton Lekner · pp. 393-415 · online 2026-08-11
    摘要

    How did the Chinese Communist Party (CCP) foster emotional engagement with its revolution? How did the advent of new media transform this engagement? Building on research that has noted the importance of emotions in the CCP’s revolution, this article explores these questions through a study of CCP radio and the person of the announcer. Using broadcast circulars and directives, memoirs, and diaries, it explores how broadcasters found an appropriate emotional tone for political texts, and how individual broadcasters functioned as nodes in the diffusion of emotions over an expanding network of radio stations. I make three arguments: first, that along with the well-documented normative forces of Party organization, announcers relied on reason and introspection to find their own emotional expression of a text and, with that, an emotional engagement with the revolution; second, that announcers were not anomalous in their reasoned engagement, but represent a consistent strain of community-building emotional practice within the ranks of the CCP; and third, that the advent of radio empowered the CCP to scale up its subject-oriented emotion work, developed at Yan’an but locally bound, to the regional and, eventually, the national scale. In this scaling up, extant CCP work-practice interacted with the promises and limits of the new media to create a distinct broadcasting culture that would shape the use of radio throughout the Mao era.

  7. Managing culture: The role of music in state-led cultural reproduction in post-colonial Pakistan
    Ghulam Ali Shair · pp. 416-448 · online 2026-09-17
    摘要

    If music’s place remains ideologically contested in the national cultural formation of post-colonial Pakistan, what are the terms, if any, by which the state engages with this art form? Answering this question, along with others, this article advances the term ‘logic of managing culture’ to explore the Pakistani state’s relationship with music. Through contesting India-centric scholarship on music and cultural politics in the South Asian context and relying on an eclectic array of empirical evidence, I argue that the post-colonial state’s engagement with music is marked by a set of priorities that entails a logic of managing music rather than reproducing it. This orchestration plays out at two major levels: the international, involving strategically projecting selected elements of Pakistan’s musical-cultural identity internationally, and the national, relying on language (Urdu) and religion (Islam) for national integration, while bypassing the role of music. I suggest that this amounts to a logic of cultural management, wherein the state, through a set of cultural institutions, ‘conducts’ music and its practitioners while presupposing the ideologically contested place of this art form in the national cultural formation of Pakistan.

  8. ‘The state won’t stop us’—Hate-crime adjudication under the SC/ST Atrocities Act in India
    Prashant Iyengar · pp. 449-518 · online 2026-07-06
    摘要

    This article presents conclusions from a study of 1,400 criminal trials in cases involving anti-caste and tribe violence, filed in the trial court of Aurangabad District (Sambhajinagar) in western India. Employing computational analysis upon the metadata and text of decisions filed under the Scheduled Castes and Scheduled Tribes (Prevention of Atrocities) (POA) Act 1989 during a 20-year period (2002–2023), I offer an exploratory socio-legal analysis of hate-crime adjudication in India. This article sheds light on hitherto unexplored questions about criminal adjudication in India such as: Which cases are most likely to result in acquittals and convictions? What kind of evidence does the court consider and in what manner is evidence read? How frequently are cases dismissed due to witnesses turning hostile? What kinds of punishments do Indian courts hand out? How long do criminal trials typically last? What kind of charges are most commonly brought against the accused in these cases? How easy is it to get bail and what conditions do courts typically impose? How long do POA-accused spend in jail before they get bail?. In doing so, this article offers a first-of-its kind phenomenology of ‘hate-crime’ adjudication and, more broadly, of criminal adjudication in India.

  9. The dynamics and ambiguities of ‘Chineseness’: Interethnic marriage practices in the Dutch Batavia before 1850
    Victoria S. Zhou, Tak-Wai Hung · pp. 519-544 · online 2026-09-16
    摘要

    This article explores how interethnic marriage produces and governs ambiguous identities within legally pluralist port-city settings. Taking the construction of ‘Chineseness’ in the Dutch Batavia before 1850 as a case study, this article analyses how identity was contested, negotiated, and reconfigured in a colonial legal landscape. Drawing on archival records from the Batavia Kong Koan (the Chinese Council), this article argues that normative orders from the homeland, such as Confucian ethical distinctions between the ‘civilized’ and the ‘uncivilized’ and gendered prescriptions governing family life and female conduct, were not merely adapted to local conditions but were frequently disrupted by diasporic realities. Through case studies of interethnic marriages and female-initiated divorces, the article highlights how female agency within the Kong Koan’s hybrid legal regime fractured the ideological coherence of the Confucian moral order and exposed the instability of ‘Chineseness’ under colonial rule. Situated at the intersection of claimed Chinese custom, Dutch colonial statutes, and the everyday realities of interethnic co-residence, the Kong Koan did not preserve tradition so much as mediate and fragment it. Rather than reinforcing a fixed notion of identity, the institution became a mechanism for its continuous remaking. By locating identity within everyday social practice and the institutional logics of colonial governance, this article challenges essentialist conceptions of diaspora. It offers a historically grounded account of how an ethnic identity like ‘Chineseness’ was continuously unmade and remade at the intersection of multiple legal and cultural systems within colonial jurisdictions.

  10. Inscribing chölkha : Census, election, and the making of modern Tibetan diaspora identity
    Ugyan Choedup · pp. 545-584 · online 2026-09-21
    摘要

    This article examines the historical formation of modern chölkha identity, a cornerstone of contemporary Tibetan nationalist thought. Rather than a direct continuation of medieval Tibetan identity categories, this article argues that modern chölkha identity is a contingent product of the Tibetan encounter with colonial modernity, especially in exile. By analysing the introduction of the modern census and electoral representation in exile, this article demonstrates how institutional logics of governmentality reconstituted once-fluid, elite-defined spatial categories into standardized, mutually exclusive popular identities. This is not to suggest that chölkha identity was invented ex nihilo by exiled Tibetan nationalist elites. On the contrary, and well known among scholars, the chölkha , as a spatial category among the literate class, has existed since the thirteenth century. Yet, as this article argues, its form, scope, and semantic load were different from those of its modern counterpart. Drawing on this history, the article proposes a ‘sequential thesis’ as a way of reframing Benedict Anderson’s concept of seriality. This article argues that ‘unbound’ and ‘bound’ serialities are best understood not as opposing types but as historical sequences, a process wherein fluid imaginaries are contingently captured by the state’s institutional grid, generating the rigid collective subjectivities that define the modern world.

  11. Hybridity, glocalization, and the negotiation of contemporary Chineseness in guoman (national animation): White Snake: Afloat (2024)
    Chenglong Zhou · pp. 585-612 · online 2026-09-22
    摘要

    This article examines Light Chaser’s animation White Snake: Afloat (2024) within the context of the revival of Chinese guoman 國漫 (national animation) since the 2010s. It reflects on a key gap in existing literature: a failure to analyse the dynamic interplay of state ideology, market demands, and animators’ agency in the construction of specific guoman texts. Drawing on an interdisciplinary framework comprising Homi Bhabha’s ‘hybridity’, Roland Robertson’s ‘glocalization’, and Weiyu Zhang’s ‘Chineseness as identity work’, this article explores how the tripartite negotiation leads to distinct forms of hybridity across three aspects of the film—techno-artistic, narrative, and structural—against the backdrop of media glocalization. The analysis also examines how the film’s hybrid forms embody competing versions of Chineseness. The overall argument is that the hybridity in Afloat is a product of the triad’s complex negotiation; it illuminates the contentious status quo of contemporary Chineseness as a globalized ‘identity work’—a dynamic, ongoing, and contested process of constructing identity, encompassing not only a subject’s self-identification but also how others identify that subject.

  12. Allies apart: China, Russia, and the limits of mnemonic friendship
    Vincent K. L. Chang, Eric S. Zhang · pp. 613-641 · online 2026-09-17
    摘要

    Addressing a gap in memory studies and international relations, this article examines the construction and limits of Sino-Russian mnemonic friendship through historical narratives of the Second World War. Drawing on extensive multilingual datasets of Chinese and Russian state-sponsored media output, supplemented by additional primary sources and fieldwork in China, it identifies a complex pattern of selective remembering and strategic forgetting across four key wartime themes: the wartime Kuomintang (KMT)–Chinese Communist Party (CCP) united front, early Soviet aid, bilateral tensions in Xinjiang, and the Soviet offensive in Manchuria. While Beijing and Moscow’s official discourses converge on the moral lessons of the war, alignment on historical events remains largely confined to strategic silences. There is little substantive narrative convergence, and personal wartime stories and Sino-Soviet cooperation remain largely untapped. This limited convergence is explained by domestic legitimation imperatives, ethnonationalist impulses, enduring mutual distrust, and divergent visions for regional and global order. The article argues that mnemonic alliances are inherently fragile, dependent on bottom-up engagement and broader social resonance, even in authoritarian contexts. Drawing attention to the mnemonic dimension of China–Russia relations illuminates the fragility of mutual trust and the long-term vulnerability of this relationship, as narrative silences can be easily undone.

  1. Experience, person, and myth: A socio-cultural history of the ‘oily man’ in post-colonial Malaysia and Singapore
    Esmond Chuah Meng Soh, Jin Rong Aw · pp. 1-29 · online 2026-10-26
    摘要

    Drawing on English, Chinese, and Malay newspapers, this article examines the ‘oily man’, a figure who shifted between sexual assailant, ghost, and intruder across twentieth-century Singapore and urban and semi-urban peninsular Malaya/Malaysia. It situates him not as a timeless ‘Malay’ entity but as an elastic label and repertoire, assembled and reassembled through rumours, retellings, and recurring events. Departing from most studies of Malay-world monsters, which reduce such figures to allegories or cinematic symbols of gender, modernity, or social change, this article demonstrates how the oily man’s historical force lay in intrusions, panics, and community responses—events mediated, disputed, and reworked across time and place. His history is framed through three registers: experience, person, and myth, which trace how this elastic label took shape through encounter, attribution, and retelling. Experience captures sightings, rumours, and assaults that animated fear; person traces how individuals were accused or apprehended as oily men; myth designates the narratives and repertoires that amplified these encounters in film, fiction, and folklore. Across these registers, the article shows how monsters emerge through lived circulations of testimony, attribution, and storytelling. It highlights the value of historical research that privileges experience and offers a framework for future scholarship: monsters as unstable, context-specific composites of encounter, narrative, and transmission that illuminate the entanglements of state, society, culture, and religion in post-colonial Malaysia and Singapore, with resonance for the study of rumour and monstrosity elsewhere in Asia.

  2. Where is Rama in Bengali folk songs? A case of devapolitik, mythopoetic dissonance, or performative abstention in bhakti federalism of Bengal
    Sayantan Thakur · pp. 1-16 · online 2026-10-26
    摘要

    This article examines the striking marginality of Lord Rama in Bengali-language folk songs and performance-based devotional repertoires, reading this absence not as a simple omission but as a culturally patterned silence shaped by regional aesthetics, vernacular religiosity, and historical power formations. Rather than presuming a uniform pan-Indian bhakti field, the article focuses on Hindu devotional song-worlds in Bengal—especially Vaishnava-inflected oral and performative contexts—where Krishna emerges as the dominant affective deity: intimate, plural, and theatrically adaptable. Rama, by contrast, appears as comparatively distant, associated with moral restraint and epic discipline that often resist the emotional grammar of folk performance. Drawing on folkloristics, performance theory, and critical approaches to discourse and cultural hegemony, the article argues that devotional absence can function as a mode of cultural agency: what is not sung may still carry ideological weight. Concepts such as devapolitik, mythopoetic dissonance, bhakti federalism, performative abstention, and scriptural decentring are deployed as interpretive tools to illuminate how regional devotional cultures selectively remember, transform, or sideline divine figures, thereby producing a distinctive devotional geography within Bengal’s plural religious landscape.

International Journal of Taiwan Studies

Vol. 9, No. 2 · 2026-02-04 · 11 篇 · Brill · Taiwan · Peer-reviewed
  1. Why Do We Need Taiwan Studies in the Twenty-First Century?
    Wen-chi Li · pp. 255-283
    摘要

    This article reviews scholarly literature related to Taiwan from the 1950s to the present to depict the development of Taiwan studies, its fraught relationship with China studies, and Taiwan scholars’ search for a home of their own. I explore the expansion in research focus from anthropology in the 1950s and 1960s, to political science in the 1970s, to economics in the 1980s, and to literary and culture studies in the 1990s and describe how scholars established associations, research centres, programmes, and a journal devoted to Taiwan studies. This institutionalisation has created a feeling among scholars of being ‘at home’ and led me to the question of which routes should be taken for the future development of the field: the ‘ Sinocentric ’ (strikethrough intentional) route of working with China studies, or the alternative, ‘oceanic’ route of seeking alliances with other marginalised countries that also believe in freedom, democracy, and resistance to hegemony.

  2. Going Weigen: Animal Advocacy and Sustainability Intersections under Taiwan’s New Vegan Movement
    Gina Song Lopez · pp. 284-317
    摘要

    From compassion to animal liberation, health, and sustainability, veganism is a concept in flux in Taiwan as a new generation of actors are bringing a fresh face and tactics to a novel yet familiar practice. This article examines the emergence of Taiwan’s new vegan movement during the socially receptive period that followed the Sunflower Movement. It introduces the actors involved in vegan advocacy, noting its distinct routes of diffusion, and where new vegan identities and alternative advocacy spaces play a key role. It shows that while animal rights rationales informed the inception of the contemporary vegan movement, some actors have shifted their attention to policy processes by reconnecting their discourses to matters of dietary sustainability, thus bringing the local movement into closer alignment with global vegan mainstreaming through engagement with sustainability regimes.

  3. Save the Children: Youth Narratives and Historical Learning in Three Taiwanese Horror Video Games
    Chia-rong Wu · pp. 318-336
    摘要

    This article examines the process of historical learning in video gaming and broadens the scope of Taiwanese game culture through an in-depth analysis of three horror video games: Detention (2017), pagui (2019), and Halflight (2020). It offers a comparative study of these games, focusing on their shared ‘save the children’ theme, which reimagines Taiwan’s traumatic past through nightmarish settings tied to the February 28 Incident and the White Terror. Additionally, the article explores how youth narratives shape and mobilise players through interactive digital gameplay. By blending historical memory with horror aesthetics, these meticulously crafted games serve as immersive portals, allowing players to engage with Taiwan’s past in a deeply interactive and affective manner.

  4. Entanglement in an Endangered Situation: Paiwan Society and the Conservation of the Mountain Hawk-Eagle
    Agathe Lemaitre · pp. 337-362
    摘要

    The mountain hawk-eagle has a special status among the indigenous Paiwan people of Taiwan. This article examines the historical evolution of the use of this eagle’s feathers amid social transformations within Paiwan culture. It also addresses the bird’s endangered status and proposed conservation strategies to protect it. Wildlife management collaborations between indigenous communities and conservationists have sparked conflicts in values and worldviews that are challenging to reconcile. While the Paiwan wish to continue using the eagle’s feathers in important cultural rituals, this increased demand exacerbates hunting pressures on the species. This article seeks to find a better balance between preserving cultural practices and ensuring species survival. Drawing on four years of fieldwork among the Paiwan, it demonstrates the complexities of navigating entangled human–animal relationships in the context of species endangerment.

  5. Contextualising Fragments Containing Fresh Perspectives about the Spanish Settlements in Taiwan (1636–1638)
    Simon Green · pp. 363-391
    摘要

    This article examines extracts relating to the period 1636–1638 in Taiwan, when the Spanish occupied two settlements in northern Taiwan, one of them being abandoned in 1637. These fragments reveal fresh perspectives on Taiwan as a stepping stone between the Philippines, the Ryukyu islands, and mainland China, giving rare first-hand accounts of the perils of journeys to these places from Taiwan’s Spanish settlements in the 1630s. The extracts also reveal fresh aspects to Spanish relationships with Taiwan’s Indigenous people. These descriptions add to arguments that the Indigenous people had a significant influence on the departure of the Spanish from the island. The fragmentary details in these texts give further evidence of the multinational exchange that took place during those years. To analyse these fragments, a thematic analysis places them at the centre of discussions, to explore what they add to existing knowledge about the settlements during those years.

  6. Revelations from the Taiwanese Netflix Series Wave Makers: #MeToo, Sexual Violence, and the Lesbian Continuum
    XinLing Li · pp. 392-421
    摘要

    This article discusses critical issues explored in the Taiwanese ‘Netflix Originals’ series, Wave Makers (dir. Lin Chun-yang, 2023) and highlights their real-life implications in Taiwan and other societies of Chinese heritage. The fact that the series has triggered Taiwan’s belated #MeToo movement showcases the profound impact it has had on Taiwanese audiences, especially regarding conventional narratives surrounding power-based sexual violence, gender inequalities at the workplace, and attitudes towards homosexuality. Using both narrative and discourse analyses, this article unpacks such narratives by providing theoretical readings – both feminist and constructionist – for the series’ various plots and character transformations. Divulged in this process are the hidden social and ideological mechanisms within the Taiwanese context that have perpetuated gender inequalities at the workplace and in the family.

  1. Crisis Communication in a Public Health Emergency: Examining Taiwan and Singapore Government Press Releases through Latent Topic Modelling
    Raphael Duerr, Honeylet Santos, Yvan Ysmael Yonaha · pp. 1-42
    摘要

    The COVID-19 pandemic was an unprecedented public health emergency requiring concerted government action. Government responsibilities included communicating risks, informing the public about hygiene practices, and combating disinformation. Examining government communication strategies is important for improving crisis communication in future emergencies. Using quantitative text analyses, this article examines approximately 3,000 press releases issued by the Taiwanese and Singaporean governments. Topic prevalence was examined in relation to the timing of press releases and COVID-19 case and death counts. We find that both governments employed high-frequency, highly standardised, and detail-rich communication. Their communication strategies correspond closely with the model proposed by the Crisis and Emergency Risk Communication (CERC) framework, with their focus shifting from the initial shock of the pandemic through to the resolution stage, when vaccines were deployed. Their communication demonstrated a commitment to being first, being right, and being credible, while their guidelines promoted action by providing the public with concrete measures to reduce risk. Nevertheless, empathy was not prominent in the press releases, which may reflect both the limitations of the format and a division of communicative roles between bureaucratic and executive institutions.

  2. Taiwan and the Global Cold War: Histories, Legacies, and New Directions
    James Lin, Bi-yu Chang, Evan Dawley, Mei-fen Kuo, Chih-Ming Wang, Dominic Meng-Hsuan Yang · pp. 1-30

  6. Performing ‘Two Sides, One Family’ in the Cloud: Cross-Strait Popular Religious Exchanges During the COVID-19 Pandemic
    Kuei-min Chang · pp. 1-27
    摘要

    This article investigates the adaptations made across the Taiwan Strait to sustain popular religious exchanges during the COVID-19 pandemic. Drawing on observations of cross-Strait virtual events and in-depth interviews, it examines the major exchange formats that emerged during this period and assesses their broader implications. The findings reveal that the materiality of divine efficacy in popular religions rendered pilgrimage activities impossible, while formats driven by the ‘United Front’ strategy of the Chinese Communist Party proved more resilient. Previously institutionalised exchange platforms migrated online, and virtual settings facilitated new connections. However, these virtual events also brought unscripted and diverse Taiwanese expressions into direct view, unsettling the carefully choreographed narrative of cross-Strait unity that had previously structured these exchanges. The immediate post-pandemic recovery has been primarily facilitated by the party-state’s United Front objectives. While the pandemic disruptions were temporary, they have ultimately reinforced the political and economic foundations upon which cross-Strait religious networks now rest.

  7. Seeking the koko’ ta’ay: Investigating the Origins of Little People Myths in Taiwan and Beyond, edited by Tobie Openshaw and Dean Karalekas
    Elizabeth Zeitoun · pp. 1-4

  9. Rebirth as Redemptive Spectrality: Hauntological Narratives in Li Ang’s Paranormal Trilogy
    Ching Kuo Hsieh · pp. 1-30
    摘要

    Li Ang’s paranormal trilogy— Visible Ghosts , Possession , and The River Woman Beyond —employs ghostly narratives to stage the intersection of historical trauma, gendered violence, and ethical reckoning. Drawing on Jacques Derrida’s hauntology and Judith Butler’s theory of grievability, this article argues that the ghost in Li Ang’s fiction functions not merely as a motif but as a structural condition of perception and narration. Across the trilogy, spectrality becomes the medium through which histories are re-entered, not to restore coherence but to expose absence, injury, and unresolved memory. Li Ang does not simply represent the paranormal; she inhabits it. Through increasingly self-referential writing, she stages haunting as a feminist modality of critique and redemption. The paranormal, in this context, is neither superstition nor metaphor—it is the very terrain through which the unspeakable is rendered thinkable, and the unreadable, legible.

  10. Consuming Democracy: Geopolitics, Human Rights, and Tourism in Taiwan
    Hardina Ohlendorf, J. J. Zhang · pp. 1-26
    摘要

    International tourism has been an area where Taiwan has projected its soft power. Tourism involves constructing an identity for the outside world to see, and tourism as ‘placemaking’ requires the deliberate selection of imagery and rhetoric that will represent and synopsise the history, culture, and environment of a location. This article examines democracy and human rights in Taiwan’s tourism landscape and argues that Taiwan’s experience of political transformation is becoming a vital component of its tourism identity under the Democratic Progressive Party government. Emphasising Taiwan’s democratic achievements helps it to build a collective memory of its democratic citizenry, distinguish itself from the People’s Republic of China, and garner support among Western liberal democracies. At the same time, it may reduce the complexity of Taiwan’s political transformations by integrating them into a linear narrative of success and closure.

  2. Cultivating Relationalities: On Refuge/Refuse
    Heidi Amin-Hong, Evyn Lê Espiritu Gandhi, Emily Hue · pp. 292-309 · online 2026-09-09
    摘要

    This introduction elaborates on the key terms of our special issue: refuge, refuse (verb), and refuse (noun). First, we define refuge in relation to critical refugee studies and critical militarism studies to understand how refuge for humans and nonhuman species can be shaped by militarized goals and processes. Secondly, we follow refuse (verb) through genealogies of refusal at the intersection of critical refugee studies and Indigenous studies to highlight the generative potential of refusal and Indigenous-refugee relationality. Finally, routing through new materialisms and transpacific Asian American studies, we track refuse (noun) and its entanglements with refugee environments, as well as its ideological and material implications.

  3. The Water Remembers: The Eco-Occupation of the Jordan River and Palestinian Refusal
    Megan Awwad · pp. 310-324 · online 2026-09-09
    摘要

    This article examines the transformation of the Jordan River from a life-giving source central to Palestinian ecological and spiritual practices to a militarized border shaped by Zionist infrastructure. Once vital to Indigenous Palestinians, the River embodies a broader colonial strategy, severing connections to land and water. Drawing on archival research, Palestinian poetry, and oral histories, I trace how Zionist projects such as marsh draining, the National Water Carrier, and dam construction produced a decaying landscape. The analysis highlights how manipulations of the River fuel Palestinian dispossession and underscores the enduring significance of memory, resilience, and resistance in the struggle for return and liberation.

  4. Cans, Crates, and Concrete: Refuse Archaeology
    Koji Lau-Ozawa · pp. 325-348 · online 2026-09-09
    摘要

    The remains of the Gila River Incarceration Camp, which confined over 16,000 Japanese Americans during World War II, lay within Gila River Indian Community land. Japanese Americans incarcerees made homes and continued to care for one another through the exchange of goods, practices of comforting, and the making of homespaces. In this article, I discuss multiple modes of refuse archaeology – the reappropriation of objects by incarcerees, the afterlives of the camp on the landscape, and the methods of analyzing these materials. Throughout, I look to not only the refuse itself, but the acts of refusal which suffuse these histories.

  5. Coral Refugee Refusals in the Shadow of Climate Resilience Finance
    Aimee Bahng · pp. 349-372 · online 2026-09-09
    摘要

    Speaking to this special issue on “Between Refuge and Refuse,” this essay analyzes how resilience and refugee narratives prime corals for recruitment into global racial capitalism as nonhuman climate disaster workers. Coral resilience finance underwrites the development of “super corals” through selective breeding (which produces “refuse” by discarding species deemed to be noncompetitive) and the rescuing of “coral refugees” (through coral arks that offer “refuge” to endangered lifeforms). The essay draws on critical refugee, finance, and disability studies to argue for coral futures that prioritize survivance over maximum productivity and radical interdependency over liberal independence.

  6. “This Poison Broke the World”: Ri-Ṃajeļ (Re)mappings of Colonial Fallout on Hawai‘i Island
    Aanchal Saraf · pp. 373-387 · online 2026-09-09
    摘要

Through poetic narration, ri-Ṃajeļ refuse colonial fallout’s disintegrative logics, connecting across time and space to trouble what is considered a consequence of fallout and where these consequences are taking place. In the face of nuclear colonialism and settler militarism’s mutual reinforcement, these ri-Ṃajeļ (re)mappings also demonstrate how Hawai‘i and the Marshall Islands are imbricated in each other’s struggles, and how refuge can be found with or without return.

  9. Palm Oil Geographies: Storying Refusal Amid Deforested Futures in the Poetics of Barokka, Gebze, and Musa

  12. More Work Than Can Be Accomplished in One Lifetime: Refuge and Refusal at Kaloko Inlet
    Mariko Chin Whitenack · pp. 452-465 · online 2026-09-09

  15. sunny day flooding fund: A Process Piece for HANDS IN THE DIRT, an Eco-Storytelling Project
    Simi Kang, Lawrence-Minh Bùi Davis · pp. 505-523 · online 2026-09-09
    摘要

    Decades of environmental catastrophe have radically transformed coastal Southeast Louisiana, a region once known as Bayouk, still called Bvlbancha. State-designed restoration projects meant to build land and slow storms have been unevenly vetted and implemented, deepening uncertainty in a region already referred to as the nation’s environmental sacrifice zone. Lost in the churn are coast-interdependent peoples who know the coast best. HANDS IN THE DIRT (HID) is an ongoing digital mapping and oral history project that partners with the area's Southeast Asian refugee fisherfolk to archive knowledges about an ecosystem being disappeared from view. This process piece about HID reflects on its fraught development and the possible shapes the project might take as it works to illuminate the violences of western environmental expertise and the potentialities of fisherfolk agency, memory, and placemaking.

  19. Sight, Sound, Movement: Aesthetic Departures from the Migrant Documentary in Shelter: Farewell to Eden (2019)

    摘要

    In Shelter: Farewell to Eden, an undocumented transgender woman named Pepsi left the militarized landscapes of Mindanao, found work as a caregiver in Libya, and applied for asylum in Italy.   20. The Eco-Politics of Voice and Refugee Environments in Ritwik Ghatak’s The Cloud-Capped Star
    Somak Mukherjee · pp. 563-576 · online 2026-09-09
    摘要

  21. Gut Displacements: Race, Migration, and Settler Colonialism in Refugee Health Discourse on the Microbiome
    Neel Ahuja · pp. 577-594 · online 2026-09-09
    摘要

    The reported rise in metabolic disorders among South and Southeast Asian refugees in North America is increasingly described in medical literature as a transition within the refugee body from a capacious to a sanitized, “Westernized” microbiome. Drawing from critical theories in food studies, fat studies, environmental studies, and disability studies, as well as from historical consideration of how wars and U.S. empire in Asia have transformed food systems, the article argues that a focus on obesity and mobility causes researchers to miss a larger set of structural contexts in the politics of refugee health and foodways.     摘要

  3. California’s Historical Alien Land Law: Racial Xenophobia and Home Ownership
    Paul Ong, Jonathan Ong, Chhandara Pech · pp. 1-15 · online 2026-10-27
    摘要

    This article uses a racial xenophobia framework to examine the impact of California’s Alien Land Law on home ownership of Asians during the early part of the twentieth century. Racial xenophobia is a compounding of two forms of marginalization, which led to the enactment of a law targeting Asian immigrants. Drawing on historical micro-level data, the analysis finds that the law adversely hurt all Asian groups, although the motivation for the prohibition was rooted in the anti-Japanese movement. Moreover, the law created differences in home ownership among people of color, with Asians being at the bottom during the study period.

  4. The Uses and Abuses of Asian American History in Responses to Anti-Asian Violence
    Sonya G. Chen, Alexander Jin · pp. 1-17 · online 2026-10-21
    摘要

    This article examines how historical narratives were deployed by Asian American advocates in responses to anti-Asian hate and violence during the COVID-19 pandemic. We identify and critically analyze three commonly-used historical narratives: “racial injury” accounts that posit anti-Asian racism as singular and unchanging; “contribution” narratives that glorify the role Asian migrants played in American history; “solidarity” narratives that elevate racial figureheads while sanitizing their politics. We argue that the continual use of these narratives is more so about political scripts than about the history itself, which may ultimately narrow the possibilities for more capacious understandings of Asian American identity and solidarity.

    摘要

Prism: Theory and Modern Chinese Literature

  1. Transwriting as Method
    Ursula Deser Friedman · pp. 223-245 · online 2025-09-01
    摘要

    This article takes up the recent discovery of an unpublished draft of Eileen Chang's short story “She Said Smiling.” The thirty-one-year period, from 1952 until 1983, in which Chang translated this story into Chinese as “Xiangjian huan” (1978) coincided with her own double exile to Hong Kong (1952) and the United States (1955). In writing back and forth between English and Chinese, Chang harnessed transwriting as an extended textual performance. When Chang's bilingual personae converged, a third—a transwritten voice—emerged from the multilingual cacophony. The article first compares the bare-bones English-language source text with the embellished Chinese-language translated version, tracing changes in characterization, temporality, plot, and narration. Chang's process of creative retelling concretizes wartime trauma and invites readers to serve as collective memory repositories. Next, the article decodes the queer subtext embedded in the subversive smiles exchanged between the story's pair of estranged female cousins. While the matter-of-fact English-language narrator overtly queers the smiling cousins’ relationship, the Chinese-language narrator leaves their ambiguous desires up to readers’ interpretation by showing rather than telling. Finally, the article examines the intertextual resonance between Katherine Mansfield's homoerotic short story “Bliss” and “She Said Smiling” as a window into Chang's attempts to write herself into the World Republic of Letters.

  2. Feminist Politics in Du Pan Fangge's Translingual Poetics
    Jiaxin Yan · pp. 246-269 · online 2025-09-01
    摘要

    This article reevaluates Taiwanese Hakka poet Du Pan Fangge's 杜潘芳格 (1927–2016) contributions to postwar Taiwan poetry to shed light on the underlying connections between translingual practices and gendered forms of (in)visibility. Considered one of the leading figures in “the translingual generation,” Du Pan has long been both politically and theoretically neglected despite the rich complexity of her identity and poetic interventions. Probing into her personal trajectories and the multilingual interspecies tropes she creates, the article takes a feminist approach to bring the issue of gender into conversation with her translingual poetic practices. It traces the creative interplay between minor languages and gender and destabilizes analytical frameworks that cast and undervalue certain writers as minoritarian in Taiwan literature studies. Du Pan depicts three inherently fluid imageries that straddle land and water: nonfish, groundwater veins, and whale potatoes. Her works inspire a practice of intertextual reading that not only takes into account the precarious existence of Taiwan between competing empires, but allows feminist potentials to thrive in moments of extreme precarity. Her translingual poetics conjures up a liberating space, an inherently multivocal, trans-affective habitat outside of domestic and national enclosure.

  3. Toward an Archive Fever around the Last Monarch
    Chang-Yen Lo · pp. 270-290 · online 2025-09-01
    摘要

    This article examines how three distinct twentieth-century works, Pu Yi's From Emperor to Citizen, Bernardo Bertolucci's The Last Emperor, and Su Tong's My Life as an Emperor, not only offer varied literary and artistic representations of the last monarch but also interrogate the concepts of history and the archive. By interweaving these works with Jacques Derrida's Mal d'archive (Archive Fever), the article also seeks to elucidate how an archive fever around the last monarch emerges, and how the ontologically fragmented and violent processes of archiving historical figures are challenged, deconstructed, and reconfigured within these texts. In these works, the past is either acknowledged and harnessed in pursuit of truth, or questioned and reconfigured to unearth suppressed narratives, or entirely fabricated and toyed with to disrupt the conventional link between history and the archive. Through the examination of these three works, I argue that the archive around the last monarch undergoes an endless process of self-deconstruction and reconstruction. This process invites readers to actively engage in reevaluating and challenging historical accounts and narratives, thereby contributing to the de-archiving and re-archiving of the very concept of the last monarch.

  4. Acting Modern China
    Xiaomei Chen, Barbara Mittler · pp. 291-295 · online 2025-09-01
  5. The Jiang'an Years of the National Academy of Drama
    Xiaomei Chen · pp. 296-325 · online 2025-09-01
    摘要

    This essay examines The National Academy of Drama (1936–49), which had trained around a thousand theater, film, opera artists, and leaders in the PRC. During the Sino-Japanese War period, the drama school moved five times and gave rise to the drama school's most productive years with its highest achievements. This essay examines the school's multicultural traditions stemmed from its faculty's American and European training in theater and the hybrid style of Western plays and traditional Chinese theater that nurtured a generation of new theater students. In all aspects of theater, such as theater building, management, advertising, stage design, lighting, custom, make-up, the drama school laid the foundations for the future theater development that culminated in the PRC period. This essay asks how one of the most productive periods of a drama institution could occur in the most challenging years of war-torn China—a time of campus relocations, artist migrations, poverty, and lack of resources. In the larger scheme of things, this essay weaves institutional history, theater formation, repertory building, war and regional cultures, economic conditions, and aesthetic movements both in and out of China for a more complex history of theater art across ideological and political boundaries.

  6. Chinese Socialist Discourse of Theatrical Performativity
    Tam Kwok-kan · pp. 326-348 · online 2025-09-01
    摘要

    A 1956 stage production of Ibsen's A Doll's House by the China Youth Art Theater offers an excellent example for the study of Chinese socialist theater performativity. The staging had sophisticated scenographic designs and acting details aimed at producing ideological messages, combined with visual effects intended to provoke the audiences and lead them to action. The performance had lasting impact in China, with another production of A Doll's House in 1962 by the Shanghai Theater Academy following similar scenic designs. It also served to substantiate Chinese socialist theory of drama with a practical example of theater performativity, which impacted subsequent theater theory and practice in the People's Republic. This cross-cultural experiment in theater performance illustrates artists’ effort to elevate art as art under stringent political conditions in observance of Mao's doctrine defining art as service to the Communist cause, which he put forth in his 1942 speech in the Yan'an Talks.

  7. Black Liberation on the Chinese Stage
    Emily Wilcox · pp. 349-370 · online 2025-09-01
    摘要

    In 1963, in response to a request from US Black radicals Mabel and Robert F. Williams, Mao Zedong issued a declaration announcing China's support for the African American struggle against racial discrimination and US imperialism. This marked the beginning of a nearly decade-long dialogue between Chinese leaders and the Williamses that shaped the Black power movement in the United States and beyond. The Chinese military dance drama Fires of Fury Are Burning (Nuhuo zai ranshao 怒火在燃燒), premiered in Beijing in 1964, used dance choreography to promote Mao's statement, as well as the Williamses’ philosophy and practice of armed self-defense for African Americans. With Chinese dancers portraying Black and white characters in the dance, Fires of Fury Are Burning built upon a tradition in twentieth-century China of employing racial impersonation to promote interracial solidarity, which differs in form and content from US blackface minstrelsy. Though dealing with similar themes, Fires of Fury Are Burning differed from African American dancer Katherine Dunham's Southland and Cuban filmmaker Santiago Álvarez's newsreel Now! because it offered an embodiment of Black empowerment and a vision of the future in which the struggle for racial justice emerged victorious, by directly combating anti-Black police brutality and KKK violence.

  8. Introduction
    Carlos Rojas · pp. 371-380 · online 2025-09-01
    摘要

    Introducing a set of three essays commissioned for a Theory Forum on digital labor, this essay begins by summarizing some questions raised by recent developments in advanced AI, which mirror a larger set of issues raised by the emergence of digital technologies since the mid-twentieth century—including technologies that enable practices of computation, communication, recording, and data storage that would have been difficult or impossible with analog technologies. The essay then reflects more specifically on how digital technologies not only have the potential to make some forms of labor more efficient, but they also create a new category of what is called digital labor. The essay proceeds to outlines several different ways of understanding the concept of digital labor, and some of the specific ways in which this category is relevant to economies, technologies, and cultures associated with East Asia.

  9. Digital Labor, Care Work, and the Production of Value in Japan and Beyond
    Viren Murthy · pp. 381-395 · online 2025-09-01
  10. Writing in Real Time
    Renren Yang · pp. 396-419 · online 2025-09-01
  11. Asianness in an Animated World
    Zachary Gottesman · pp. 420-437 · online 2025-09-01
    摘要

    Nickelodeon's Avatar: The Last Airbender (2005–2008) has endured as a beloved series across decades and spawned a massive media franchise. Though cocreated by two white Americans, a fantasy Asianness is an essential element of the setting, the characters, the martial arts, and a sense of authenticity. The show was also animated in a specific Asian country, South Korea, which served as both a source of cultural Asianness and a source of cheap animation labor. Empowered by the fallout from a live action film version of the story, which cast white actors in roles that had been coded by fans as Asian in the animated show, the original creators returned to create a sequel for an older audience, The Legend of Korra (2012–14) and encouraged its Korean animators to contribute creatively to the show. Investigating the failed effort to use Japanese anime studio Pierrot for outsourcing labor in season 2, the backlash that followed created awareness of the previously invisible Korean elements of the show's success. This coincided with the greatest Korean cultural input into the show itself, with Korean background art, historical and political references in the plot, and a highly stylized two-part special in an East Asian art style. Korra, which evoked East Asian modernity to critique the political limitations of Avatar, brought an American multicultural vision of Asianness into conflict with the Asianness of Koreans themselves rooted in nationalism and historical conflict. This essay analyzes the contradiction between these two forms of Asianness, their manifestation at the level of Korra's production and plot, and failed attempts at resolution. Ultimately, Korra reveals the limits of Korean cultural input in the global hierarchy of animation production.

Journal of Southeast Asian Studies

Vol. 56, No. 1-2 · 2025-06 · 21 篇 · Cambridge UP · Southeast Asia · Peer-reviewed
  1. Editorial Foreword
    Maitrii Aung-Thwin · pp. 1-4 · online 2026-09-30
    摘要

    Transregional interaction, dynamic relationality and local variation are consistent themes that bridge the research articles in this extended double issue.In many respects, the authors' shared interest in mobilities, fluidities and pluralities represents the intellectual currency of our times, establishing a premium on research that draws attention to historical and contemporary experiences of tension, difference and contestation.For nearly four decades, efforts to transcend the epistemological borders, heuristics, and categories associated with an earlier imagining of Southeast Asia (that sought to delineate it as a distinctive, coherent and legitimate unit of analysis) continues to exemplify emergent research.Reconfiguring Southeast Asia by problematising and disaggregating resilient tropes has both thickened and reconstituted how we delineate 'region'.As the articles demonstrate, our parameters for defining the communities, cultures, languages, ecologies, lifeways, worldviews, histories and practices of the regiona legacy of the broader intellectual construction of Southeast Asia-provide opportunities for reevaluating the promise of area as a unit of analysis.While earlier institutional renderings of these ambiguously defined areas were transformed into bordered regions (arguably for administrative/bureaucratic reasons), this has not prevented scholars from recognising the role of trans-Asian dynamics in the study of Southeast Asia over the generations.Who we include as Southeast Asian, what we identify as indigenous experiences, or what we designate as local are arguably legacy issues that have always motivated the field.At the same time and as several of the articles on overseas Chinese experiences will demonstrate, we have become more spatially accommodating, theoretically nuanced, and methodologically tolerant to promote a more elastic notion of area.Read together, the articles by Goh, Soh, and Yan draw attention to the myriad ways diasporic communities both reify and challenge conventional understandings of what constitutes Southeast Asian Studies.The question of how we define Southeast Asian Studies today is consequential as the field's infrastructure endures significant changes.Legacy area-studies programmes around the world are being forced to reposition or redefine themselves in the wake of increasingly shrinking institutional, financial and political support.One pathway recently discussed amongst area-studies leadership at the recent Association for Asian Studies (AAS) conference (Vancouver 2026) was to reconceptualise area-studies as Global Asian Studies, both as an attempt to prescribe greater relevance to stakeholders but also to highlight the way Asian studies research was increasingly becoming multi-sited, multi-lingual and multi-scalar in orientation.Another pathway, promoted by this Journal as an AAS panel in 2024, was to think about the possibility of a Public Southeast Asian Studies as a way of recognising the range of practitioners, processes and settings that produce knowledge on the region, issues that intersect with https://www.

    pp. b1-b2 · online 2026-09-30

  4. Intimate strangers: Shifting images and knowledge gap of Southeast Asia in Japan
    Pattajit Tangsinmunkong, Jin Sato · pp. 5-19 · online 2026-09-30
    摘要

    Japanese people have a strong affinity for Southeast Asia as a region, yet they often know very little about it. This is particularly true for students in Japanese universities. The issue lies in the gap between what students believe they know and what they actually understand about Southeast Asia. This is concerning because perceptions based on a superficial understanding of the facts are vulnerable to political mood swings and propaganda, which can undermine sound judgements in international relations. It is not unreasonable to expect students at top schools, who are interested enough in the region to take related courses, to develop a deeper understanding. This paper approaches the problem from two angles: first, it reviews the evolution of scholarly literature on Southeast Asia in Japan, analysing the characteristics of the Japanese approach to Southeast Asian studies. Second, it traces the educational background of Japanese youth to examine how scholarly culture has influenced education from high school to college level. In doing so, we problematise the global perceptions of ordinary Japanese citizens and attempt to explain the disconnect between poor knowledge and rich awareness. We argue that a structural issue between the production and consumption of knowledge must be addressed.

  5. HongKar experiences: Rethinking localisation in Southeast Asia through Teochew-Catholics in Singapore
    Bryan Goh · pp. 20-47 · online 2026-09-30
    摘要

    The microhistories of three Teochew-Catholic parishes in Singapore challenge notions of localisation as conceived of in the studies of overseas Chinese diasporas. Despite hailing from the Guangdong province of China, these Catholic migrants reached Singapore under a myriad of circumstances, and the Catholic Church played a pivotal role in their coping with the experiences of migration. Triangulating between ecclesiastical archives, oral history interviews, and commemorative artefacts, this paper highlights three separate experiences of mobility between China and Singapore between the nineteenth and twentieth centuries: of sojourning, long-term migration, and refugees. Differing experiences hence prompted divergent modes of localisation, resulting in disassociation amongst fellow Teochew-Catholics. Even though Catholicism and similar geographical origins in China provided commonalities amongst these Teochew-Catholics, the dynamics of migrant-mission interactions contrasted drastically across the three parishes, resulting in distinctive traditions and communal identities. Rather than a single Teochew-Catholic community, these parishes evidence three localised overseas Chinese communities within the island of Singapore. The paper hence problematises understanding localisation across national boundaries and cultural assimilation, advocating for an experience-based model of analysis and inviting further comparisons across communities in Southeast Asia.

  6. Mediums, mobility, migration: The religion of compassion in Southeast Asia
    Esmond Chuah Meng Soh · pp. 48-74 · online 2026-09-30
    摘要

    The Cijiao is a Chinese sectarian religion founded during the Malayan Emergency (1948–1960). Contrary to the common perception of East Asia as the cradle of Chinese religious movements, this study examines a movement that originated in colonial Malaya and subsequently spread to North America and China since the latter half of the twentieth century. This article proposes that different forms of migration and mobility among Malaysians and Singaporeans were quintessential to the Cijiao’s development within Southeast Asia and abroad. It argues that these diverse channels, methods, and periods of travel not only offered new avenues for the movement’s expansion but also generated novel dynamics and reshaped this religious network. As such, the extension of the Cijiao’s global reach cannot be solely understood through the lens of a single locality. Instead, it must be seen as arising from prolonged engagement with cultural currents and competing interests spanning diverse global regions.

  7. Hà Tiên, the Dutch East India Company, and the politics of piracy in eighteenth-century maritime Southeast Asia
    Xing Hang · pp. 75-97 · online 2026-09-30
    摘要

    This article reconstructs an eighteenth-century legal dispute involving piracy between two Chinese captains in the Gulf of Siam. In an unprecedented move, Mo Tianci (Mạc Thiên Tứ), the ruler of Hà Tiên, delegated judicial authority over the case to the Dutch East India Company (VOC) in Batavia. This decision reveals his deliberate strategy to integrate into the Company’s sphere of influence to bolster security and trade. Hà Tiên’s agility in navigating multiple sovereignties was essential to its continued prosperity as a commercial crossroads. The Mo’s historical trajectory also illustrates the growing influence of Western power in maritime Asia’s geopolitical order.

  8. Colonisation and cultivation: Nutmeg production, labour and ecology on the Banda Islands before and after the conquest of 1621
    S.J. Miske · pp. 98-123 · online 2026-09-30
    摘要

    In 1621, the Dutch East India Company (VOC) conquered the Banda Islands (Maluku, Indonesia) and killed, expelled and enslaved its indigenous population. The motivation behind this genocide was to establish control over the production of nutmeg and mace, two valuable spices that were grown almost exclusively in Banda. According to most historians, the VOC was successful in radically transforming, intensifying and, rationalising spice production. This paper, by contrast, argues that there was a significant degree of continuity in cultivation methods between the precolonial and (early) colonial periods and that productivity levels stagnated or declined following colonisation.

  9. Growing your own: Vegetables and food scarcity in colonial Malaya, c. 1900–1950
    Fiona Williamson · pp. 124-143 · online 2026-09-30
    摘要

    Between 1900 and 1950, colonial Malaya suffered a series of difficulties including trade supply disruptions, economic downturns, warfare and harvest failures. These resulted in short to long-term impacts on the availability of locally produced or imported food, especially rice. Small-scale market gardening and home vegetable growing became critical avenues to ameliorating shortfalls. The subject of vegetable gardening as a means of everyday or crisis subsistence has been little attended to in the Asian context, yet, local vegetable growing played an important role in managing food insecurity, even making the difference between life and death. Taking colonial Malaya as a case-study, this article investigates how small-scale vegetable farming and market gardening were methods of subsistence during periods of minor to acute food insecurity and a means of decreasing reliance on imports. This relatively untapped history reveals government-led strategies to increase food production, including ones that created conflict with communities or individuals already producing their own food. It argues that colonialism had in fact created many of the insecurities that the government now sought to address, and that the production of food was closely connected to — and reinforced — the colonial governments perceptions and stereotypes of different Malayan peoples.

  10. Unreading the literary: The Hikayat Anggun Cik Tunggal and Malay oral historical tradition in an age of print
    Nicole Yow Wei · pp. 144-168 · online 2026-09-30
    摘要

    While Malay Hikayat texts are composed along oral-oriented sensibilities or otherwise originate in the oral medium, what survives of their past recitations are but scant written records of them. Scholars working under these evidentiary limits have often seized on these texts’ fantastical elements to interpret them as myths. I suggest that such flawed interpretations are found on visual readings that ignore Hikayat texts’ oral poetics; and consider instead how a text is historical insofar as its medium is germane to its historical poetics. I demonstrate a methodology to unread Hikaya and re-situate them in their oral medium in which texts were liable to change over time, to appreciate their historicity. In unreading the Hikayat Anggun Cik Tunggal, this article recovers a history which retold Minangkabau experiences of colonialism through the memory of the 1511 Portuguese invasion of Melaka. Finally, I trace the Hikayat into an age of print colonialism, in which print formed a key technology by which European colonists re-moulded meaning-making and intellectual life in the Malay world. By these mores of media consumption, Malay readers came to interpret Hikayat as fictions, and were wrested from a long tradition of history-telling that enabled them to process colonial trauma.

  11. Where civilisations converged, where culture languished: A Chinese artist’s wartime travels across South and Southeast Asia, 1939–42
    Yu Yan · pp. 169-194 · online 2026-09-30
    摘要

    Amidst the upheaval of the Second World War between 1939 and 1942, renowned Chinese artist Xu Beihong embarked on a journey across Singapore, India, and Malaya, one which would leave an indelible mark on his artistic career. While existing studies typically frame this phase as Xu Beihong’s Nanyang period and foreground his patriotic fundraising endeavours and contributions to China’s War of Resistance Against Japan, his nearly year-long sojourn in India is overshadowed by these fundraising accounts. Drawing on newly discovered primary sources, this study conducts a comparative analysis to explore overlooked aspects such as his everyday linguistic practices, social networks and markedly divergent artistic outputs across the two regions. By examining Xu’s different attitudes towards South and Southeast Asia, this research highlights the limitations of nationalist or bilateral frameworks for interpreting intra-Asian artistic interaction. It also reveals the intricate power dynamics within overseas Chinese communities during the Second World War, which not only shaped the diverse cultural landscape of both regions but also consolidated hierarchies beneath a rhetoric of resistance and solidarity. Through this comparative lens, the study exposes a deeper layer of inequality that foreshadowed regional divergences on the eve of decolonisation.

  12. The end of nature in Borneo: A concise contemporary history
    David Henley, David Gaveau, Erik Meijaard · pp. 195-231 · online 2026-09-30
    摘要

    This article provides a concise overview of the history of environmental change in Borneo since 1970, with particular attention to human institutions, actors, and choices, and to the reasons for those choices. It is a political narrative, with an eye for vested interests and for the forces resisting environmental change as well as those driving it. The article deals with the whole of Borneo, including Indonesian Kalimantan, the Malaysian states of Sarawak and Sabah, and the sultanate of Brunei, highlighting parallels and contrasts between their trajectories. In the late twentieth century the appearance of large international markets for tropical timber and palm oil changed the ecological fate of Borneo, leading to an almost uniquely rapid process of environmental destruction. In the early twenty-first century a combination of incipient timber and land exhaustion, stagnating palm oil prices (2010–20), and an internationalisation and monetisation of conservation efforts has slowed down the rate of deforestation. But the close involvement of Indonesian and East Malaysian political elites in extractive industries makes it unlikely that a reversal is imminent. Even if some ecosystem restoration can now be envisaged, the fact that this is most likely to take place under the stewardship of big business raises uncomfortable ethical questions.
